Theory documents

Written by four agents from the research notes and the decision graph. Use the filter to hide sections that do not mention your term.

General rules: empirical regularities in the hotel-stay and amenity carbon evidence

Derived from research notes 01-08 and the synthesis report in this review (evidence base closed 24 October 2026). Every number below is taken from those notes as they give it; where a figure is this file's own arithmetic on a cited figure it is labelled "own arithmetic". A rule's confidence is high when at least two independent primary sources agree and no primary source contradicts it, medium when the support is a single primary source, a preprint, secondary citations or conflicting primaries, and low when the evidence is grey literature or nameplate only.

Units: "room-night" = occupied room-night (CHSB "OCRM", HCMI Measure 1 or 3); "guest-night" divides by guests (1.80-1.92 per occupied room in full-service hotels, Attari & Hanrahan 2026 Table 1). kgCO2e throughout unless stated.

1. Boundary choice moves a hotel's per-night footprint more than property choice does: adding purchased food, waste and laundry to a Scope 1+2 inventory raises a full-service room-night by 70-130%, so the same hotel can sit anywhere between roughly 9 and 56 kgCO2e per room-night depending on what is counted.

Evidence

  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026 (preprint, four Irish hotels, 3- to 5-star): Scope 1+2 only 9.40 / 16.59 / 25.10 / 32.32 kgCO2e per room-night; full Scope 1-3 (food procurement, food waste, landfill, recycling) 15.87 / 40.30 / 47.15 / 56.14; the authors' own section 5.5 gives the uplift as 70-130%.
  • Filimonau et al. 2011 (two UK 3-star hotels, full text): operational 7.5 and 4.5 kgCO2e per guest-night; adding breakfast (1.3 kg), daily laundry (1.75 kg) and a 15% embodied uplift gives 11.65 and 8.25, a 55-83% increase (own arithmetic on the paper's figures).
  • Morales et al. 2026 (Beijing five-star): 95.9 kgCO2e per guest-night under an extended operational boundary vs 36.7 from the Greenview HFT benchmark for the same property class, a 2.6x spread from boundary alone.
  • Dolder Grand (Zurich 5-star, 2023-2025): Scope 3 is 89.8-90.5% of the whole-hotel total; Scope 1+2 alone would be about a tenth of the reported 38.1-39.3 kgCO2e per overnight stay.
  • The spread within the HCMI-basis benchmark set is smaller than the boundary effect: CHSB 2025 US luxury IQR 25.08-43.62 (1.7x) vs the 1.7-2.4x Scope 1+2 to full-scope ratio in the Irish sample.

Boundary conditions

  • Holds for full-service hotels with restaurants; for limited-service properties without F&B the uplift collapses to laundry and consumables (Filimonau's breakfast + laundry = 3.05 kg).
  • The HCMI / CHSB / LightStay / corporate-target figures are all Scope 1+2 (plus outsourced laundry); none includes purchased food, so every published hotel benchmark is at the low end of this range by construction.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • The Greek 5-star resort (CEST 2025) reports energy at 81% of guest-night CO2e, but its boundary excludes purchased food; it is an operational split, not a counterexample.
  • Attari & Hanrahan is a Research Square preprint under review and its Table 6 misquotes Puig 2017 (9-14 vs the primary 12, 22, 23).

Confidence: high. Three independent inventories with different methods (LCEA, GHG Protocol corporate, cradle-to-gate procurement) agree on direction and on an uplift of at least 50%.

2. Once purchased food and drink are inside the boundary they are the largest single category of a full-service hotel's footprint, at 40-58% of a room-night, and the share rises as the electricity grid gets cleaner.

Evidence

  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026: food and beverage procurement 43.2% / 57.7% / 43.1% / 39.7% of total at the four hotels, "the single largest emission category at every property".
  • Dolder Grand: Food & Beverages 1,713.9 of 3,541.9 t (48%, 2023), 52% (2024), 2,257.7 of 4,047.5 t (56%, 2025), against purchased electricity 3-4% and heating/cooling 11-12% on Zurich's hydro-nuclear grid.
  • Accor URD 2024 (group, worldwide, via Projet Celsius): catering and drinks 19% (1,229 kt) vs energy 65% (4,282 kt); the same source states that in a French hotel on low-carbon electricity "energy weighs less and catering more".
  • Santiago 2021 (six Gran Canaria hotels): F&B service demands over 50% of hotel energy, before any embodied food is counted.
  • SHA / Systemiq 2024 (top-down): hotel food systems 185 MtCO2e/yr, split 62% sourcing, 19% kitchen operations, 17% food waste, 2% packaging; "a typical hotel meal" estimated at three times the global-average meal.

Boundary conditions

  • The share is a ratio and depends on the denominator's electricity factor: 48-56% at 0.0x kg/kWh (Zurich), 40-58% at 0.226 kg/kWh (Ireland), 19% for a global portfolio weighted to fossil grids. On the New York grid (eGRID NYCW 0.392 kgCO2e/kWh; LL97 0.289) the F&B share of a Waldorf room-night will sit below the Dolder's and above Accor's; the notes do not give a NYC-specific share.
  • Holds for hotels serving three meals; the ADEME French labelling study puts breakfast alone at 30% of a per-night footprint.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Accor's 19% is a group figure that includes limited-service brands and was built partly on spend-based Scope 3 (abandoned by Accor in 2023); it is not a hotel-level inventory.
  • No hotel company (Hilton, Marriott, IHG, Hyatt, Scandic) publishes F&B as a share of footprint; Marriott's purchased goods line is spend-based with 0% supplier data.

Confidence: high on direction (two full inventories plus a sector estimate agree); medium on the 40-58% range, which rests on one preprint and one unaudited company report.

3. Energy-only (HCMI-basis) footprints for luxury hotels in the United States and Western Europe cluster at 20-50 kgCO2e per occupied room-night; in Asia and the Gulf the same metric runs 50-95.

Evidence

  • CHSB 2025 (CY2023, location-based eGRID factors): NYC luxury non-resort median 28.39 (n=21, IQR 23.95-38.19); US luxury 32.12 (n=137, IQR 25.08-43.62); US 5-star 36.04; US luxury Measure 3 (whole hotel, no apportionment) 32.19.
  • CHSB 2025 cross-country luxury medians: UK 23.34, Canada 20.56, Spain 17.65, France 15.60, Germany 33.29, US 32.12; India 84.01, UAE 68.52, China 66.64, Japan 52.13, Singapore 36.19.
  • Huang, Wang & Wang 2015 (58 Taiwanese luxury hotels, abstract verified): 50 kgCO2e per room-night sold, 29 per guest-night, 132 kgCO2e/m2/yr.
  • Brighter Planet 2012 (46,000 US hotels modelled): JW Marriott 38, Ritz-Carlton 33, Westin 34, Hilton 31, Hyatt 31 kgCO2e per room-night.
  • Puig et al. 2017 (Spanish 5-star, per overnight stay = per guest): 22 kgCO2e; GHDR 2017 baseline of 9.227 t per room-year converts to about 39 per occupied room-night at 65% occupancy (notes' arithmetic).
  • Morales et al. 2026: Beijing five-star 95.9 kgCO2e per guest-night (extended operational boundary).

Boundary conditions

  • The cluster is a grid-factor artefact as much as an efficiency one: US luxury fell from 43.30 (CY2016) to 32.12 (CY2023) while energy per occupied room fell only from 143.96 to 119.35 kWh (-17%), so most of the 26% drop is grid decarbonisation and sample change.
  • Small samples: NYC luxury n is 8-25 in every CHSB edition with SD 19.0 against a mean of 35.6; the IQR, not the median, is the citable statistic.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • The pandemic-recovery year CY2021 put NYC luxury at 40.54 (n=8) and US luxury resorts at 56.07, outside the cluster, because of low occupancy (see rule 6).
  • A Taiwanese life-cycle study gives 147.93 kgCO2e per standard room-night with embodied and amenity production included (repository record only; method not read).

Confidence: high for the US/Europe cluster (benchmark of 27,219 hotels plus three independent studies); medium for the Asia/Gulf band (medians with n of 9-21 and one case study).

4. The normalising denominator changes a hotel's ranking: per occupied room-night, per guest-night and per square metre give different orderings, and per-guest-night values are roughly half of per-room-night values in full-service hotels.

Evidence

  • Xuchao, Priyadarsini & Lee 2010 (Singapore, abstract verified): "a hotel's carbon intensity ranking is rather sensitive to the normalizing denominator chosen."
  • CHSB 2025 (CY2023): NYC luxury is lower than US luxury per occupied room (28.39 vs 32.12) but higher per m2 of conditioned space (88.68 vs 83.83 kgCO2e/m2) and in energy per m2 (321.61 vs 300.70 kWh/m2), because NYC properties are high-rise with less area per room and a lower grid factor.
  • CHSB 2025: US luxury is 2.3x upper-midscale per occupied room (32.12 vs 14.03) but 1.43x per m2 (83.83 vs 58.77).
  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026: 1.80-1.92 guests per occupied room, so per guest-night 8.81-24.47 vs per room-night 15.87-56.14.
  • CHSB 2023 and CHSB 2016: higher-rated hotels use more water per occupied room (5-star +23.31% 2019-21 vs 2-star +3.99%) but the luxury premium "did not hold per m2".
  • CHSB 2025 report: 5-star energy per occupied room improved 12.8% in one year while energy per m2 rose 2.4%, because occupied rooms rose 9.8%.

Boundary conditions

  • Applies to any comparison that mixes Puig (per guest), CHSB/HCMI (per occupied room), Huang (both) and LL84/LL97/Hilton (per m2 or ft2). A paper must state the denominator and carry at least two.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • None found that contradicts the direction; the HES 2011 European meta-analysis (no significant EUI difference by star rating per m2) is itself an instance of the rule, not a counterexample.

Confidence: high. Peer-reviewed statement plus three independent datasets that flip ordering between denominators.

5. The luxury premium is 2.3-3.1x midscale per occupied room-night but only 1.4-1.5x per square metre, so roughly 60% of the per-room-night gap is floor area per occupied room (bigger rooms, more public and back-of-house space, lower occupancy) and about 40% is higher intensity per square metre.

Evidence

  • CHSB 2025 (CY2023, US, Measure 3): luxury 32.19 vs upper midscale 12.62 (2.55x) and midscale 10.24 (3.1x); Measure 1: 32.12 vs 14.03 (2.3x) and 11.74 (2.7x). Per m2 (Measure 4): 83.83 vs 58.77 (1.43x). kWh per occupied room 119.35 vs 42.20 (2.83x); kWh/m2 300.70 vs 197.73 (1.52x). Decomposition 2.55 / 1.43 = 1.8x from area per occupied room (notes' arithmetic).
  • Brighter Planet 2012: "A hotel with 80 square feet of floor space per room consumes more than three times as much energy per room-night as a hotel with 20"; the average upscale hotel uses 25% more energy per room-night than the average budget hotel; square footage per room is the strongest property-level driver after climate.
  • CHSB 2023 outlier screens: full-service luxury accepted at 50-700 kWh per occupied room, midscale at 20-300, i.e. the benchmark's authors expect about 2x.
  • Arenhart et al. 2024 meta-analysis: pooled r with energy use 0.65 for floor area (0.84 raw energy), 0.58 for star level, 0.78 employees, 0.81 room revenue; EUI per m2 vs floor area r = 0.14, not significant.
  • Wang 2012 (Taiwan): international tourist hotels 26.7 MWh per room-year and 280.1 kWh/m2 vs B&Bs 9.4 MWh/room and 143.6 kWh/m2 (2.8x per room, 1.95x per m2).

Boundary conditions

  • The 60/40 split is a US CY2023 decomposition; the Taiwan figures give a 2.8x / 1.95x pair, i.e. more of the gap in intensity per m2 in a cooling-dominated climate.
  • The per-m2 premium comes from 24-hour F&B and kitchens, worker density and commercial refrigeration (ENERGY STAR coefficients 34.93 per worker/1,000 ft2 and 178.7 per refrigeration unit/1,000 ft2), not from the guestroom itself.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • HES 2011 found no statistically significant kWh/m2 difference by star rating across European studies, and Schick 2024's SLR gives 5-star 285.9 vs 1-star 238.2 kWh/m2 (1.2x); both put the per-m2 premium lower than CHSB's 1.43x.
  • Priyadarsini et al. 2009 (Singapore): 3-star EUI differs from 4/5-star at 95% confidence, but 4 vs 5-star is not significant; the premium saturates above 4-star.

Confidence: high for the per-room-night ratio and the direction of the split (benchmark of 13,445 US hotels, model of 46,000, meta-analysis); medium for the exact 60/40 decomposition, which is one year of one benchmark.

6. Occupancy inverts star-rating rankings in per-night metrics: a 2-star hotel at 30% occupancy out-emits a 5-star at 50%, pooled occupancy is not a significant predictor of total energy and turns negative for upscale hotels, and a 10-point occupancy swing moves per-room-night intensity by 10-25%.

Evidence

  • Puig et al. 2017 (full text): 2-star 23, 5-star 22, 4-star 12 kgCO2e per overnight stay at 30%, 50% and 90-100% occupancy; the authors call the footprint "inversely proportional to the occupancy rate".
  • Arenhart et al. 2024: pooled correlation of occupancy with energy use 0.20, not significant; 0.36 for general hotels, -0.21 for upscale.
  • CHSB trend: NYC luxury 40.54 kgCO2e per occupied room in CY2021 (pandemic recovery, n=8) against 21.78 (CY2019) and 26.19 (CY2022); US luxury resorts 56.07 in CY2021; CHSB 2024 reports energy per m2 -9.59% but energy per occupied room +9.26% across all hotels 2019-2022.
  • CHSB 2025: 5-star energy per occupied room -12.8% while energy per m2 +2.4% as occupied rooms rose 9.8%.
  • CHENACT Barbados audit: guestrooms occupied only ~30% of the time guests are registered, so even a sold room carries mostly unoccupied-hours load.
  • ENERGY STAR hotel score (CBECS 2012, n=139): occupancy rate tested and found not statistically significant for source EUI per ft2.

Boundary conditions

  • Holds for per-room-night and per-guest-night metrics; per-m2 metrics are nearly occupancy-neutral (fixed base load dominates), which is why CHSB's per-m2 and per-room series diverge in the same year.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026: at 59-80% occupancy the star ordering held (3-star 15.87 < 4-star 40.30/47.15 < 5-star 56.14), so inversion needs a large occupancy gap.
  • Chan 2005 (17 Hong Kong hotels) found number of occupied rooms one of only two significant predictors of total electricity, i.e. occupancy does drive absolute consumption even where it does not drive intensity.

Confidence: medium. One primary inversion (Puig), a meta-analysis and benchmark year-effects agree, but the magnitude per occupancy point is inferred from CHSB year-on-year changes, not estimated directly.

7. Area-based apportionment (HCMI, CHSB Measure 1, Hilton LightStay) gives every room-night the same footprint whether or not the guest used the spa, pool or restaurant, removes only about 5% from rooms in NYC luxury hotels, and no large benchmarking regression carries an amenity term.

Evidence

  • HCMI v2.0 sec 6.3-7.2: all remaining areas "of heart of house, spa, retail, fitness, public space, restaurants, etc." are "allocated proportionately to Rooms and Meeting Space"; "The methodology assumes equal intensity for all areas of the hotel"; "each occupied room shares the proportion of the total footprint equally"; it "does not account for different room sizes".
  • CHSB 2025 NYC luxury: Measure 1 (rooms, area-apportioned) 28.39 vs Measure 3 (whole hotel per occupied room) 27.02; US luxury 32.12 vs 32.19, so the meeting-space apportionment moves rooms by 0-5%.
  • CHSB 2025 report: "no additional information was collected on the range of amenities that could contribute towards hotels' utility use"; CHSB 2018 and 2023 state amenities cannot be accounted for.
  • ENERGY STAR hotel score (Aug 2018): variables are rooms, workers, commercial refrigeration, kitchen flag, degree-days, % conditioned; laundry, occupancy and conference space tested and non-significant; no pool or fitness term. Chan 2005 (CDD, occupied rooms) and Wang 2012 (area, rooms, occupancy, age) likewise carry none.
  • Santiago 2021 (six Gran Canaria hotels) is the only regression found with a significant pool term (pool volume, with RevPAR, diners and guests per occupied room); Bohdanowicz & Martinac 2007 found food covers sold significant and suggested "benchmarking of facility components may be necessary".
  • Urban Green Council 2020: about half of benchmarked NYC hotels have fitness centres but their presence "did not have a measurable impact on their efficiency grades or carbon intensities".

Boundary conditions

  • The 5% figure is for NYC luxury CY2023; hotels with large under-used ballrooms shift more away from rooms (HCMI assumes 10 h x 365 d meeting utilisation regardless of actual use), and hotels with no meeting space load 100% onto rooms.
  • Absence of an amenity term in the large models reflects data availability (CBECS does not record pool area), not evidence of no effect.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026: the 5-star spa property's electricity (333 tCO2e) was ten times the city 4-star's (32 t), "reflecting the continuous HVAC and spa load of a residential wellness property", so the amenity effect is real even though no benchmark can see it.

Confidence: high. The methodology text is explicit, the apportionment effect is measured in the benchmark itself, and the regression list is exhaustive for the papers reached.

8. The electricity emission factor chosen moves a New York hotel's energy-only footprint by 36-50%, more than any single operational intervention measured in the evidence base.

Evidence

  • eGRID2023 NYCW total output rate 864.5 lb CO2/MWh = 0.392 kgCO2e/kWh (location-based, Portfolio Manager, CHSB); LL97 prescribed coefficient 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh = 0.289 for 2024-29 (36% lower); LL97 2030-34 coefficient 0.000145 = 0.145 (50% below 2024-29, 63% below eGRID).
  • Portfolio Manager's embedded eGRID factor itself moved from 81.4 kgCO2e/MBtu (CY2013) to 92.8 (CY2016) to 73.8 (CY2021) in the Waldorf's LL84 filings, a 14-26% swing with no change in the building.
  • CHSB trend: US luxury HCMI median fell 26% CY2016-CY2018 (43.30 to 31.97) while kWh per occupied room fell 9% (143.96 to 131.28); the difference is grid factor and sample.
  • Hilton 2025: market-based minus location-based Scope 2 = 118,530 tCO2e (about 6% of managed Scope 2), the net effect of green tariffs and EACs; Marriott sourced 2.92% of energy from renewables (2023); US managed Hilton hotels' ~152M kWh renewable is ~2% of managed energy.
  • For scale (own arithmetic): applying 0.392 vs 0.289 to the CHSB NYC luxury 102.31 kWh per occupied room moves the room-night by up to 10.5 kgCO2e (upper bound, as if all 102 kWh were electricity); the largest measured operational lever, occupancy-based HVAC controls, saves 0.9-2.6 kWh per occupied room-night (rule 17), i.e. 0.3-1.0 kgCO2e at these factors.
  • The F&B share swings from 19% (Accor, fossil-weighted grid) to 48-56% (Dolder, Zurich) with the grid factor (rule 2).

Boundary conditions

  • Applies to electricity-heavy hotels; the Waldorf CY2024 filing was 79.4% electricity. For gas and steam the LL97 coefficients (0.00005311 and 0.00004493 to 0.0000432 tCO2e/kBtu) barely move (steam -3.9% for 2030-34), so a gas-fired flagship gets no factor relief.
  • LL97's marginal grid coefficient (0.247 to 0.114 tCO2e/MWh, 2024-29) applies only to beneficial-electrification credits, not footprint totals (notes' decision).

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Contractual (market-based) instruments are a small lever at portfolio scale today (2-6%), so the factor choice that matters is location-based vs regulatory coefficient, not location vs market; this may change after CHPE (1,250 MW, 10.4 TWh/yr from June 2026) makes Zone J RECs available.

Confidence: high. The factors are prescribed or measured values; the comparison with interventions uses measured savings from rule 17.

9. Per-square-metre intensity targets can be met while absolute emissions rise: every major chain reporting a per-m2 or per-room reduction of 11-51% since its base year also reports absolute emissions flat or up, by 8-84%.

Evidence

  • Hilton 2025 Performance Tables (DEKRA-assured): managed Scope 1+2 intensity -50.9% vs 2008 (0.1567 to 0.0769 tCO2e/m2) while absolute market-based managed Scope 1+2 rose from 2,229,587 to 2,443,342 tCO2e (+9.6%) and franchised Scope 3 from 2,180,912 to 4,011,353 (+84%); implied managed floor area 14.2M to 31.8M m2 (notes' arithmetic).
  • IHG Annual Report 2025: carbon per available room -11.0% vs 2019 (0.0297 to 0.0258 tCO2e) while "total carbon emissions are up 7.7% since 2019"; IHG self-declares off track against its validated absolute -46.2% target.
  • Mandarin Oriental 2023: carbon intensity per m2 -22% vs 2012 while absolute Scope 1+2 rose as floor area more than doubled from 574,935 m2.
  • CHSB 2024 (2019-2022, all hotels): GHG per m2 -15.62%, energy per m2 -9.59%, but energy per occupied room +9.26%, the opposite sign on a different denominator (rule 4).
  • Hilton's SBTi-validated targets are in fact absolute (-46.2% Scope 1+2, -27.5% franchise Scope 3 by 2030 vs 2019; SBTi dashboard), but Hilton publishes no 2019 base-year value, so progress against the validated target cannot be computed from its disclosures; CDP 2023 reported 5.14% of the absolute target achieved by 2022 and franchise emissions 12.7% above base.

Boundary conditions

  • Holds for asset-light groups growing floor area faster than intensity falls; HSH/Peninsula is the partial exception with absolute -33.6% vs 2010 alongside -48.4% intensity, because its portfolio grew slowly.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • HSH (above) shows intensity and absolute can fall together; Scandic reports CO2 per guest-night from 4.4 to 1.6 kg (1996-2011) with Nordic renewable electricity, but no absolute series was retrieved.
  • An earlier note described Hilton's SBTs as intensity-only; file 08 corrects this from the SBTi export. The validated target is absolute; the reported metric is intensity.

Confidence: high. Three assured or audited corporate disclosures plus the CHSB sector series show the divergence directly.

10. Offset-based "carbon-neutral stay" claims have failed scientifically (6-94% of credits non-additional in the project types hotels buy) and legally (courts in three jurisdictions and an EU per se ban from 27 September 2026), and the one hotel ruling turned on an omitted amenity boundary.

Evidence

  • West et al. 2023 (Science 381:873-877): only 6.1% of credits from 18 REDD+ projects were linked to additional reductions; 68% came from projects that did not significantly reduce deforestation (Verra disputes the method).
  • Badgley et al. 2022 (GCB): California forest offsets over-credited by 29.4% (30.0 MtCO2e, 90% CI 20.5-38.6); Coffield et al. 2022: 37 IFM projects "generally not additional"; Haya et al. 2023: IFM protocols "deviate from scientific understanding".
  • ICVCM (15 Nov 2024): CCP label for three new REDD+ methodologies only; legacy Verra methodologies that "account for the majority of REDD+ credits on the market" excluded.
  • Courts: Amsterdam District Court, 20 Mar 2024, 15 of 19 KLM statements misleading; LG Köln Mar 2024 and OLG Köln 13 Dec 2024 against Eurowings "CO2-neutral"; BGH 27 Jun 2024 (I ZR 98/23): "klimaneutral" permitted only if the ad itself says whether emissions are reduced or offset; Landgericht Berlin II (DUH v Best Western Mannheim): "climate neutral" stays prohibited because the calculation covered electricity and gas but "did not adequately account for other relevant emissions sources, including waste disposal and laundry services".
  • Directive (EU) 2024/825, applying 27 Sep 2026: per se ban on claims of a neutral, reduced or positive environmental impact based on offsetting outside the value chain (Annex wording from legal summaries; EUR-Lex text not fetched).
  • SBTi CNZS V1.3.1 and V2.0: carbon credits "do not count as reductions toward meeting near-term or long-term science-based targets"; Oxford Principles 2024: "most offsetting today is still not net zero aligned".
  • Populus Denver ("first carbon positive hotel", 2024): 80% of offset-planted spruce seedlings dead after one year vs 46% average mortality. Booking.com withdrew its "Travel Sustainable" badge (500,000+ properties) on 25 Mar 2024 after the Dutch ACM objected.

Boundary conditions

  • Legal exposure is strongest in the EU, Germany, Netherlands and UK (CMA fines up to 10% of global turnover since 6 Apr 2025); the US FTC Green Guides are unrevised since 2012 and no New York consumer action against a hotel was found. In New York the binding constraint is LL97, under which offsets (AHRF only) cover at most 10% of the limit.
  • Disclosure of measured emissions plus separately described contributions to removals is the formulation every source leaves open.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Durable removals ($180/t average; DAC median $650/t) are 5-30x the price of the credits found in hotel programmes ($6-35/t), but for a 50-150 kg guest-night that is $9-27, 1-2% of a $1,500 rate (inference in notes); the economics do not fail, the claim does.

Confidence: high. Peer-reviewed over-crediting studies, final court judgments and enacted law all point the same way.

11. Water heating is the largest single end use in US lodging at about 20% of site energy (16.5 kBtu/ft2 in 2018, 24.2 in 2012) and about half of a full-service hotel's gas; only HVAC taken as a whole exceeds it.

Evidence

  • EIA CBECS 2018 Table E2 (lodging, kBtu/ft2): water heating 16.5, space heating 15.7, cooking 14.1, ventilation 12.5, lighting 7.2, cooling 6.0, refrigeration 4.2 of 85.7 total; CBECS 2012: water heating 24.2 of 96.9, the largest end use by a wider margin.
  • PNNL/ACEEE 2010 (300+ room full-service hotel, Washington DC area, 2009): service hot water 51% of natural gas (~27,900 therms/yr, about 7.5 kWh thermal per available room-day, notes' arithmetic), kitchen ~25%, laundry ~25%.
  • EPA WaterSense: heating water needs 0.183 kWh per gallon = 0.048 kWh per litre for a 41.7 K rise, before heater efficiency; water heating is ~90% of a clothes washer's energy.
  • Santiago 2021 (Gran Canaria): hot water system is the second-largest demand after F&B.
  • Water heating + cooking + refrigeration = ~35 kBtu/ft2, 41% of lodging site energy (notes' arithmetic), the end uses tied to F&B and laundry rather than the guestroom.

Boundary conditions

  • "Largest single end use" is a CBECS end-use category statement; space heating + cooling + ventilation together (34.2 kBtu/ft2 in 2018) are twice water heating. CBECS "lodging" mixes hotels, motels, dormitories and nursing homes.
  • In a heating-dominated, electrically heated building (PNNL, PTHPs in resistance mode) guestroom and public-space heating were ~60% of winter electricity and water heating only ~6% of total energy; the share depends on fuel and climate.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • HES 2011 (Europe): space conditioning about half of total, DHW second at up to 15%, lighting 12-18%; Greek hotels 72-75% space conditioning + DHW combined. Europe ranks DHW second, not first.
  • SaskPower single-facility example: lighting 40%, HVAC fans/pumps 23%, hot water 4%, which conflicts with every national figure and is one building.

Confidence: medium. The US national figure is solid, but the ranking is category-definition dependent and European sources place DHW second.

12. Among wellness amenities only heated water (pools, hydrotherapy, thermal suites) carries a load of the same order as guestroom HVAC and hot water; treatment rooms, gyms and their equipment each add well under 1 kgCO2e per room-night.

Evidence

  • Borowski et al. 2022 (Hotel Turówka, Kraków, 51 rooms, 4,620 m2, heat-metered): pool-area heating 399.6 (June) to 657.4 kWh/day (May), peak 63.7 kWh/h, against domestic hot water 299.4-1,573.6 kWh/day; annualised lower bound 146-240 MWh/yr = 32-52 kWh per m2 of hotel floor area, 7-12% of a CIBSE TM46 typical hotel's 435 kWh/m2 before pumps, lighting and dehumidification (notes' arithmetic, winter not reported).
  • Attari & Hanrahan 2026: the 5-star spa/pool property's electricity (333 tCO2e) was ten times the city 4-star's (32 t).
  • CIBSE TM46:2008 (per m2 floor, per year): swimming pool centre 245 electricity + 1,130 fossil = 1,375 kWh/m2 vs hotel 105 + 330 = 435 and fitness & health centre 160 + 440 = 600 (secondary reproduction).
  • Gyms: metered 2 HP treadmill 712 W at 10 mph, 96 W at 1 mph, 3.76 W idle; a 20-machine gym at 10% utilisation is ~7 kWh/day of plug load vs ~24,000 kWh/yr for a 150 m2 room's lighting and conditioning on the TM46 basis (notes' arithmetic); SportsArt energy-generating equipment yielded 24 Wh in a 30-minute Bloomberg test.
  • Urban Green Council 2020: fitness centres in NYC hotels had no measurable effect on efficiency grades or carbon intensity.
  • Only metered hotel-spa electricity: Novotel Bali airport spa 7.24-11.22 kWh/m2/month (87-135 kWh/m2/yr), cooling-dominated, a lower bound for a temperate spa with hydrotherapy.
  • Spa-visit calculator (Communications Sustainability 2026, record only): ~5 kgCO2e per visit at a large wellness resort, ~3.5 at a small facility, all scopes.

Boundary conditions

  • "Heated water" includes whirlpools at 38-40°C (Sport England spa pools 40°C vs swimming 28-30°C), which lose heat faster by evaporation than the pool; no measured commercial whirlpool, sauna or steam-room energy exists, only nameplates (9-20 kW sauna heaters; an 11.4 kW steam generator at 55% duty for 16 h/day = 36,516 kWh/yr, manufacturer example).
  • The Waldorf Astoria New York hotel appears to have no guest pool (the 25 m Starlight Pool is a residents' amenity), so for that case the wet load is the spa's hammam, steam room, infrared sauna and hydrotherapy, not a pool.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • The "up to 35% of hotel energy for additional services such as a spa" figure (Spanish hotels) is unsourced and almost certainly bundles heated pools with the spa; it should not be applied to treatment rooms.
  • PwC cost shares (energy 4% of management costs for basic hotels vs 25% with spa and heated pools) are cost, not energy, shares.

Confidence: medium. One hotel-specific pool metering (no pool size, no winter), one preprint, benchmark tables from a secondary reproduction, and nameplate data for everything hot except the pool.

13. Pool-facility energy scales with bathers and water throughput, not primarily with climate, and evaporation is 46-60% or more of pool-water heat loss; the right allocation key for a hotel pool is visits or m2 of water surface, not degree-days or floor area.

Evidence

  • Kampel et al. 2016 (43 Norwegian facilities, 176 datasets, abstract verified): "Unlike in residential and commercial buildings, climate does not drive the total energy performance of swimming facilities. Instead, overall water usage of the facility was observed to be most strongly correlated with the energy usage, followed by the number of visitors"; energy "shall be preferably normalised with respect to the number of visitors".
  • Nitter et al. (NTNU survey, 37 facilities, 2017 data, climate-corrected): bather load and opening days explain ~77% of energy (adjusted R2 0.77, n=30); 6.7-35 kWh per bather.
  • Borowski et al. 2022 (Kraków hotel): correlation between pool-area heating demand and outdoor temperature "low" in summer; occupancy explained more of DHW.
  • Evaporation: 46-54% of total pool losses in a Thermal Science 2014 simulation; "at least 60% of heat loss in the water" in the four studies collated by Benakcha et al. 2025, whose case study also put filtration pumps at 40% of total demand.
  • Make-up water standardised at 30 L per bather (Sport England, PWTAG incl. backwash, WHO via Nova Scotia, DIN 19643); Polish measurement 40.6 L/bather/day; heating 30 L from 10 to 29°C is 0.66 kWh per bather before boiler losses (notes' arithmetic).
  • Chemicals are negligible: Euro Chlor 0.90 kgCO2e/kg chlorine, 0.93 per kg sodium hypochlorite, two orders of magnitude below the heating load.

Boundary conditions

  • Holds for indoor pools held at 27-30°C with hall air ~30°C and 60% RH (Sport England); the per-m2 water-surface benchmarks span 1,000-11,000 kWh/m2 (Kampel 2013), so the benchmark route is unreliable and a bottom-up build from bathers, water and evaporation physics is preferred (notes' decision).

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Smedegård et al. 2021 found outdoor dry-bulb temperature and relative pool usage factor the statistically significant predictors of facility power (R2 = 87%), and ENOVA is cited for "40% of the energy consumed in a pool facility depends on the outdoor temperature"; climate is not absent, it is secondary to use.
  • The 4,300 (Mediterranean) / 5,200 (continental) kWh/m2 WS figures attributed to Trianti-Stourna 1998 are climate-zone generalisations with "no indication about where these numbers originate from" (Kampel thesis); the measured Greek value is 450.1 kWh/m2 floor (~1,540 kWh/m2 WS at ratio 3.43).

Confidence: medium. The Norwegian evidence is primary and consistent, but two sources keep outdoor temperature as a significant predictor, and the kWh-per-visitor statistics (mean 26, median ~22) were not located in the primary text.

14. A beef-centred main carries 8-11 times the footprint of a vegetarian main, and a luxury-hotel restaurant cover runs 5.4-6.6 kgCO2e, about three times a global-average meal and above the WRI Cool Food lunch/dinner threshold of 5.38.

Evidence

  • Danish meal-service LCA (Int. J. LCA 2018): average main 3.70 kgCO2e; veal/beef mains 5-7x the average of other meals and 8-11x the average vegetarian meal.
  • Poore & Nemecek 2018 (OWID, kgCO2e/kg): beef-herd beef 99.48, dairy-herd beef 33.3, lamb 39.72, cheese 23.88, poultry 9.87, eggs 4.67, vegetables ~0.5; Clune et al. 2017 medians: beef 28.73 (10.74-109.5), lamb 27.91, cheese 8.86, chicken 4.12, field vegetables 0.47. A 200 g beef main is 7-20 kgCO2e, chicken ~2, legume/vegetable well under 1 (notes' arithmetic).
  • Dolder Grand: 5.9 (2023), 5.4 (2024), 6.1 (2025) kgCO2e per restaurant guest; Accor 2023: 1.66-6.57 kgCO2e per meal.
  • WRI Cool Food Meals (US 2020): 3.59 kgCO2e per breakfast portion, 5.38 per lunch or dinner portion; SHA 2024: a typical hotel meal is "three times higher than the global average".
  • Accor: meat is 3% of food and drink purchases by weight but 27% of their food-and-drink carbon footprint. Scarborough et al. 2014: high meat-eaters 7.19 vs vegans 2.89 kgCO2e per 2,000 kcal day (2.5x at diet level).
  • Filimonau 2011: breakfast 1.3 kgCO2e per guest-night at a UK 3-star (the only peer-reviewed per-breakfast figure); ADEME: breakfast = 30% of a French per-night footprint.

Boundary conditions

  • The ratio holds across factor sets; the absolute cover depends on the set chosen (Poore & Nemecek and Clune differ 3.5x on beef; Attari used Agribalyse/CONCITO beef 16.0 kg/kg). The paper must name the set.
  • Beverage covers are smaller: 125 ml wine 0.15-0.33, bottled beer 0.17-0.28, 50 ml spirit ~0.2 kgCO2e (own conversions in notes); operational bar energy per serve is grams (ice 0.08-0.12 kWh/kg, dishwasher 0.17-0.61 kWh/rack).

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Carbon labels shift choices only modestly: Brunner 2018 +11.5% low-emission dishes and -3.6% emissions; canteen trials 1.5-5%; Pechey 2022 (111,837 choices) null; SHA's labelled hotel menus moved 63% of stated intentions but "few orders".
  • The Dolder's per-event-guest figure jumped from 7.8-8 to 19.7 kgCO2e in 2025 with a change to ecoinvent 3.10 / IPCC 2021, so even one hotel's own series is not method-stable.

Confidence: high on the beef-to-vegetarian ratio (peer-reviewed LCA plus two independent factor sets); medium on the 5.4-6.6 cover, which is one unaudited inventory bracketed by a group average.

15. Hotel food waste runs 0.3-0.5 kg per cover with buffets two to three times à la carte, and kitchens that weigh it cut it about 21% within a year at a benefit-cost ratio near 7:1.

Evidence

  • Papargyropoulou et al. 2019 (five Malaysian operations incl. a five-star hotel restaurant and a high-end banquet hall): 0.53 kg food waste per customer average; the hotel restaurant produced over eight times the least wasteful site; the banquet hall prepared 30% more than required by policy; preparation waste 15-55%, buffet leftover 22-50%, plate waste 23-35% of total.
  • Camilleri-Fenech et al. 2020 (Malta five-star, one month): plate waste buffet dinner 0.48 kg/person vs à la carte dinner 0.16 and lunch 0.21; preparation waste 0.08 kg/person; removing the side plate cut plate waste from 0.059 to 0.043 kg.
  • EPA Ireland benchmarks (search-summary only): ~360 g per cover daily service, ~530 g per cover for functions; buffet breakfast 300 g vs plated 130 g (unsourced).
  • Champions 12.3 / WRI / WRAP 2018 (42 hotel sites, 15 countries, 21 luxury): pre-consumer waste -21% by weight within 12 months; benefit-cost ratio average 6.8:1 (median 4.7:1, range 0.4-28.8); 95% of sites net positive; waste ~5% of cost of goods sold. Hilton Green Ramadan 2024 (32 hotels): 129 to 102 g post-consumer waste per cover (-21%); Marriott UK -25% in six months; Mandarin Oriental -36% in four pilots.
  • SHA 2024: more than 20% of food bought by hotels is thrown away; 60% kitchen, 40% plate (Winnow data); Winnow 2018: >70% of waste before it reaches the plate.
  • CHSB 2025 waste index: luxury 5.49 kg total waste per occupied room vs midscale 0.78; US food waste 0.25 kg/OCRM.

Boundary conditions

  • Per-cover figures are for restaurants and banquets; breakfast-buffet plate waste alone is far smaller (Juvan et al. 2021, Slovenia: 14.3 g per person per day average, rising from 1.8 g at 7-8 am to 31.8 g at 9-10 am; 4-star 14.3 vs budget 9.2 g).
  • Disposal emissions are small and allocation-sensitive: DEFRA ~627 kgCO2e/t to landfill vs ~9 composted gives 0.2-0.3 kgCO2e per cover; the 1.9 kg/kg (BIO Intelligence) and ~4.35 kg/kg (implied by Hilton's 7.4 t per 1.7 t) factors include embodied emissions already inside purchased food and double count against Scope 3 (notes' decision: purchases carry the embodied, waste carries disposal only).

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Juvan's 14.3 g breakfast plate waste and Papargyropoulou's 0.53 kg per customer differ by a factor of 37 because they measure different streams (plate only vs prep + buffet + plate); the two must not be compared.
  • No controlled comparison of breakfast-included vs room-only rates exists; the inference that inclusive rates raise waste is plausible but untested.

Confidence: medium. The 21% programme result rests on 42 sites; the per-cover figures are one-hotel audits or unverified summaries.

16. Linen laundry adds about 6-10 kWh and 2-3 kgCO2e per occupied room-night at a full-service hotel (5-7 kg of linen at 1.6-1.7 kWh/kg and about 0.4 kgCO2 per kg), a figure corroborated by one hotel's own opt-out sign.

Evidence

  • Linen mass: 5-8 kg per occupied room-day when sheets, pillowcases, towels and robes are changed (Clean Group); 10-15 lb (4.5-6.8 kg) (Hospitality Institute); 12 lb (5.4 kg) for a full-service hotel with pool and banquets (Hotel Management 2021); observed range 8-55 lb PPOR with 83 PPOR at a 5-diamond resort on a peak day (Western State Design); HCMI default 5.12 kg per occupied room.
  • Energy and carbon per kg: TRSA 2018 (132 US plants) 2,422 Btu/lb = 1.57 kWh/kg and 0.413 lb CO2/lb = 0.41 kgCO2/kg, water 12.3 L/kg; UK trade benchmark 1.6-1.7 kWh/kg typical, 0.8-1.0 attainable; TRSA Clean Green thresholds 1.94-2.39 kWh/kg.
  • 6 kg x 1.65 kWh/kg = 10 kWh and 6 kg x 0.41 = 2.5 kgCO2e per room-night (own arithmetic); at best practice (1.0 kWh/kg) ~6 kWh.
  • Sheraton Boston "Make a Green Choice": each opted-out night saves 37.2 gal water, 0.19 kWh electricity and 25,000 BTU (~7.3 kWh) gas, consistent with the 6-10 kWh estimate. Filimonau 2011: daily linen and towel laundry 3 kWh and 1.75 kgCO2e per guest-night at a UK 3-star.
  • Dolder Grand: external laundry 233.9-259.7 t/yr, 6-7% of whole-hotel footprint; PNNL hotel: on-site laundry 25% of natural gas (3.7 kWh thermal per available room-day) plus 44 kWh/day electricity.
  • Water: washer-extractors 17-33 L/kg, tunnel washers <=17 L/kg (EPA WaterSense); on-site hotel machines 15-30 L/kg vs 3-6 L/kg at central plants.

Boundary conditions

  • Luxury properties with robes, extra towels and daily sheet changes sit at or above the top of the 5-8 kg range; a spa or pool visit adds 1.3-1.5 kg of towelling (Standard Textile 27x54 bath towel 570-640 g, hand towel 115-170 g) and ~2.5-3 kg with a robe (robe mass unsourced).
  • TRSA factors are plant-wide with lodging only 1.2% of pounds and a US fuel mix; the 0.41 kgCO2/kg is not hotel-specific.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Towel-reuse signage moves the laundry line only at the margin: Goldstein et al. 2008 raised reuse from 35.1% to 44.1% (same-room norm 49.3%), while German guests reuse at 64-93% regardless of message (Bohner & Schlüter 2014); Hyatt Centric's optional linen change cut linen output 18%.

Confidence: medium. No peer-reviewed kg-per-room-night figure exists; the mass is trade data, the energy factor is plant-level, and the corroboration is a hotel's marketing sign.

17. Guest-behaviour and room-control levers each save at most 1-2 kgCO2e per occupied room-night (under 10% of an energy-only luxury night); signage is cheap, hardware is not, and vendor claims of 33-50% do not survive controlled measurement.

Evidence

  • DOE/PNNL 2012 (six Marriott-brand hotels, five vendors): occupancy-based guestroom HVAC controls saved 10.8-26.5% of guestroom HVAC energy (mean 18.4%), 167-589 kWh per room per year, at US$345-426 installed per room with simple paybacks of 5.3-17.9 years; per occupied room-night at 63% occupancy that is 0.9-2.6 kWh (notes' arithmetic) or 0.3-1.0 kgCO2e at 0.289-0.392 kg/kWh (own arithmetic). Prior utility studies 5.6-28% (62-462 kWh/room/yr).
  • UC Davis / SDG&E: "an analysis of all data combined does not show a statistically significant savings due to the control technology"; BPA Grouse Mountain Lodge: savings below comparable studies because of manual override; vendor claims of 33% (Verde/Wyndham, 2 vs 2 rooms, 30 days) and 45-50% (Aqua Aloha Surf, uncontrolled) are not controlled trials.
  • Towel cards: +9 to +12 percentage points of reuse in the US (Goldstein et al. 2008); baseline cultural differences (US ~35% vs Germany 64% with no message) dwarf the signage effect.
  • Opt-out housekeeping: 37.2 gal + 0.19 kWh + 25,000 BTU per opted-out night (Sheraton Boston), about 1.4 kgCO2e at the LL97 gas coefficient (own arithmetic, 7.3 kWh x 0.181 kg/kWh fuel plus electricity); Hyatt Centric linen -18%.
  • Bathroom lighting: four 60 W lamps left on 24 h = ~600 kWh per room per year; motion-sensor control saves 76-154 kWh/yr per bathroom (PG&E ET 0609).
  • Menu carbon labels: -3.6% emissions (Brunner 2018), 1.5-5% in canteen trials, null in Pechey 2022.
  • Food-waste measurement is the exception on cost: -21% waste at 6.8:1 benefit-cost within a year (rule 15).

Boundary conditions

  • Against a NYC luxury energy-only night of 28.4 kgCO2e (IQR 24-38), 1-2 kgCO2e is 3-8%; against a full-scope night of ~40-56 kg (rules 1-2) it is 2-5%.
  • Luxury properties that avoid guest-visible restrictions and pre-condition rooms should be modelled at the low end of the controls range unless PMS-integrated (notes' inference); CHENACT found rooms occupied only ~30% of registered time, which is where the HVAC saving comes from.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • The candidate claim "small but cheap" fails for hardware: 5.3-17.9 year paybacks are not cheap; it holds for signage, defaults and weighing food.
  • HES 2011 quotes European savings potentials of 15-20% heating, 5-30% cooling, 40-70% hot water, 7-60% lighting (XENIOS project); these are technical potentials for retrofits, not behavioural levers, and were not measured per room-night.

Confidence: medium. The HVAC-controls figure is a multi-site controlled demonstration; the other levers rest on one field experiment each or on a hotel's own sign.

18. Luxury hotels use 500-800 litres of water per occupied room-night, two to five times the 140 L European frontrunner benchmark and about 1.5-2x the all-hotel average; the premium exists per room but not per square metre, and one large review finds no quality effect at all.

Evidence

  • Marriott managed portfolio: 0.788 m3 (788 L) per occupied room in 2022 (0.880 in 2021, 1.005 in 2020, 0.841 in 2016; 0.763 in 2023).
  • Ecologic 2007 / Hamele & Eckardt 2006 (via Ramazanova et al. 2021): average hotel 394 L per overnight stay, five-star 594 L; ~600 L/night in Moroccan five-star hotels (Eurostat 2009, secondary).
  • Puig et al. 2017 (primary): 0.58 / 0.32 / 0.19 / 0.54 m3 per overnight stay for 5* / 4* / 3* / 2* Spanish hotels; Morote et al. 2018: 520 L/tourist/day in 5-star vs 287 in 1-star; Olcina et al. 2016: 225.9 (5-star) to 127.87 L (2-star).
  • Styles et al. 2015: frontrunner <=140 L per guest-night for fully serviced hotels; best practice saves at least 228 L per guest-night. Scandic (Nordic midscale): 220 L per guest-night (2009). AHLA: 209 US gal (~790 L) per occupied room per day.
  • CHSB 2023: water per occupied room 2019-21 rose 23.31% for 5-star vs 3.99% for 2-star; "higher-rated hotels consume more water per occupied room than lower-rated hotels"; CHSB 2016: the luxury premium did not hold per m2.
  • Deyà Tortella & Tirado 2011 (abstract): higher-category, low-season and all-inclusive hotels use more water in Mallorca.

Boundary conditions

  • Per occupied room vs per guest matters (rule 4); Marriott and AHLA are per occupied room, Puig and Ecologic per guest or stay. Laundry is ~16% of hotel water (Lodging Magazine citing EPA, unverified) and pools add 30 L per bather plus ~16 m3 per filter backwash.
  • Hot water is not separated from cold in any luxury source; the hot-water energy line (0.048 kWh/L heated) must be modelled from fixture assumptions (a 250 L soaking tub ~8.4 kWh of heat per fill; a 10-minute rain shower ~3.9 kWh, notes' arithmetic on residential fixture volumes).

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Schick & Redlein 2025 (systematic review, 1,667 hotels, weighted mean 419 L per guest-night): quality level "not significantly" affecting water use, "contradicting prior research".
  • Puig's 2-star hotels at 0.54 m3 per stay nearly match the 5-star 0.58, again an occupancy effect (30% vs 50%).

Confidence: medium. Four independent sources put luxury at 500-800 L, but the largest review finds no quality effect and no source isolates hot water.

19. The operating phase is 70-90% of a hotel's life-cycle energy and emissions, so a 10-20% uplift on operational figures bounds embodied and amenity-production carbon for an existing building.

Evidence

  • Rosselló-Batle et al. 2010 (Balearic hotels, 50-year life cycle, abstract verified): operating phase 70-80% of total energy use; materials manufacture about one fifth; electricity the main CO2 source. The author's 2016 thesis states 80-90% of life-cycle CO2 (secondary).
  • Filimonau et al. 2011 (full text): non-operational (embodied) energy taken as 15% of operational for two UK hotels (1.1 and 0.7 kgCO2e per guest-night on 7.5 and 4.5 operational).
  • Taiwanese life-cycle study (repository record): 147.93 kgCO2e per standard room-night whole life cycle, with production and transport of amenities plus laundry ~16% of total and energy the main source.
  • Populus Denver claims 6,675 tCO2e of embodied carbon for a new-build hotel offset by credits and trees; the trees failed (rule 10).

Boundary conditions

  • Holds for existing buildings assessed over 50 years; for a gut renovation such as the Waldorf's (96% of the interior replaced per SOM; $2 billion+) the embodied share of the next decades is higher, but no figure exists in the notes.
  • Operational share rises as the grid is more fossil-heavy and falls as it decarbonises; the Balearic result is on a 2000s Spanish island grid.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • The Taiwanese 147.93 kgCO2e per room-night is three to five times the energy-only luxury benchmarks (rule 3), which suggests a broader embodied boundary than Rosselló-Batle's; the method was not read.

Confidence: medium. One peer-reviewed abstract, one assumed uplift and one unread record; no study separates amenity-production carbon from building embodied carbon.

20. Dry in-room and building services (minibar, elevators, ice, dishwashing, toiletries, bottled water) each add under 1 kgCO2e per occupied room-night, but a single chauffeured airport transfer (8-13 kgCO2e one way in a luxury diesel or petrol car) can exceed the room's entire Scope 1+2 footprint for the night, and no hotel group reports it.

Evidence

  • Minibars: Dometic HiPro absorption units 0.53-0.70 kWh per 24 h (236-265 kWh/yr, EN 62552-3), compressor units about a third of that; 0.1-0.3 kgCO2e per occupied room-night on typical grids (notes' arithmetic); ~70% of travellers never use one, so energy per transaction is several kWh.
  • Elevators: 141 kWh/day at the 12-storey, 300+ room PNNL hotel, ~1% of electricity, ~0.47 kWh per room-day; CIBSE worked examples 0.038-0.27 kWh per cycle with 200-900 W standby; a medium-usage hotel lift at 300 trips/day ~47 kWh/day (notes' arithmetic). No high-rise luxury measurement exists.
  • Ice 3.6-5.6 kWh per 100 lb (~0.02 kWh per 200 g serve); dishwashers 0.17-0.61 kWh per rack of 16-36 glasses (ENERGY STAR listings).
  • Bottled water: 5.6-10.2 MJ per litre (Gleick & Cooley 2009), so two 500 mL bottles are 1.6-2.8 kWh of primary energy, the same order as the in-room plug load (0.64 kWh/room-day lights/TV/other at PNNL).
  • Toiletries: no independent LCA; chain claims are plastic-mass only (one pump bottle = 10-12 miniatures; "95% less plastic").
  • Transfers: DEFRA 2024 luxury car 0.319 (petrol), 0.207 (diesel), 0.258 (hybrid), 0.121 (PHEV) kgCO2e/km; a 40 km one-way transfer is 12.8 kgCO2e petrol, 8.3 diesel, 4.8 PHEV, ~1.6 BEV on the UK grid (notes' arithmetic); compare 20.6 kgCO2 per room-night (Gössling via Filimonau) or the 9.40-32.32 Scope 1+2 range in Ireland.
  • Marriott 2022 Scope 3 (purchased goods 4.13 Mt, capital goods 0.78, fuel/energy 1.65, waste 0.16, business travel 0.055, commuting 0.59, franchises 4.61 Mt), Hilton (franchises, waste, business travel) and IHG (Category 14) report no guest-travel line; a hotel-owned fleet is Scope 1, a contracted limousine Scope 3 Category 1 if the hotel pays.

Boundary conditions

  • The sub-1 kg items are per room-night at typical grids; a 40-storey tower with express lifts would be several times the PNNL elevator figure, and nameplate minibar ratings are not field measurements.
  • Staff-delivered services (butler, 24-hour room service) are labour whose carbon is commuting (Marriott 594,040 tCO2e non-franchised, 2022), allocated by staff-to-room ratios for which no luxury data exist.

Counterexamples and conflicts

  • Bottled water's 1.6-2.8 kWh is primary energy, not delivered; converted to CO2e it is still well under 1 kg but is the largest of the "small" items.
  • The Taiwanese life-cycle study puts amenity production plus laundry at 16% of a 147.93 kg room-night (24 kg), which would contradict the sub-1 kg claim if amenities rather than laundry carried most of it; the split was not read.

Confidence: medium. The in-room figures are manufacturer ratings, one metered hotel and official emission factors; the transfer arithmetic is sound but no hotel discloses transfer volumes.

Rules considered and rejected

  • "Guest behaviour levers are small per night but cheap." Half rejected. Small holds (rule 17); cheap holds for signage, defaults and food-waste weighing (6.8:1), but the one multi-site controlled hardware result (DOE/PNNL 2012) has 5.3-17.9 year paybacks at $345-426 per room.
  • "Climate does not drive pool energy." Kept only in weakened form (rule 13): Kampel 2016 and Nitter say use dominates, but Smedegård 2021 (R2 87% with outdoor temperature as a significant predictor) and ENOVA's 40% keep climate as a secondary driver; the evidence does not support "does not drive".
  • "Spa facilities account for up to 35% of a hotel's energy." Rejected. The figure appears on a University of Thessaly project page without a source and bundles heated pools and thermal suites with the spa; no primary study supports it for treatment rooms.
  • "Indoor pools use 4,300-5,200 kWh per m2 of water surface per year." Rejected as a measured benchmark. Kampel's thesis shows these are unsourced climate-zone generalisations attributed to Trianti-Stourna 1998; the audited Greek value is 450.1 kWh/m2 floor (~1,540 kWh/m2 WS), and the 1,094.5 kWh/m2 WS figure could not be located in any text.
  • "Fitness centres raise a hotel's carbon intensity." Rejected. Urban Green Council 2020 found no measurable effect across NYC hotels with fitness centres; metered plug loads are ~7 kWh/day for a 20-machine gym; only the room's lighting and conditioning matter, and that is floor area, not amenity.
  • "Breakfast-included rates raise food purchased and wasted per guest-night." Not supported. Plausible from the buffet-waste evidence (Malta 0.48 vs 0.16-0.21 kg; EPA Ireland 300 vs 130 g), but no study has measured the counterfactual; the Slovenian breakfast-included hotel showed only 14.3 g plate waste per person.
  • "Hotel kitchens are 15-25% of a hotel's energy." Not supported as a rule. SHA states it is interview-based because "reliable industry wide data is not currently available"; the only metered figures (PNNL kitchen ~8% of electricity, ~10% of total energy in winter; Greek resort kitchen propane 3%) are lower and single-building.
  • "Luxury hotels are more energy-intensive per square metre." Conflicting, folded into rule 5 as the minority share of the premium: CHSB US 1.43x, Schick 1.2x, HES not significant, Priyadarsini 4 vs 5-star not significant.
  • "Hilton's science-based targets are intensity-only." Rejected and corrected (rule 9): the SBTi-validated targets are absolute vs 2019; the intensity goals are Hilton's reporting frame.
  • "Food waste costs 1.9-4.35 kgCO2e per kg wasted." Rejected as an additive line. Both factors include the embodied emissions of the wasted food, which already sit in purchased goods; only disposal (~0.63 kg/kg landfill, ~0.009 composted) can be added without double counting (notes' decision).
  • "Cocktails carry 150-700 gCO2e per drink." Not promoted to a rule. The only source is a catering supplier's marketing study; no peer-reviewed cocktail LCA with ice and glass washing exists.
  • "Renewable procurement materially lowers chain footprints today." Rejected. Marriott 2.92% of energy, Hilton ~2% of US managed energy and a 6% market-vs-location Scope 2 gap; it is folded into rule 8 as evidence that the factor choice that matters is location vs regulatory coefficient.

Measurement and allocation guidelines for the footprint of a hotel stay and its amenities

Status: theory track, derived from the Facet 1–8 notes and the decision graph (workspace hotel_carbon, branch main). Every number below traces to a note in this directory or to a graph node; nothing is estimated here that was not estimated there. Where a figure is this document's own arithmetic on cited inputs it is labelled "own arithmetic".

The one fact that organises everything else: the same luxury hotel night can be reported at 15 or 56 kgCO2e without anyone being wrong, because the published literature spans a 1.7–2.4x boundary uplift (Scope 1+2 versus full Scope 1–3, Attari & Hanrahan 2026), a 1.8–1.9x denominator ratio (room-night versus guest-night, same source), and a 1.36x electricity-factor ratio in New York alone (eGRID NYCW 0.392 versus LL97 0.289 kgCO2e/kWh, note 06). Multiplied through, the methodological envelope is about 4.5x. A footprint that does not state its functional unit, boundary tier, allocation key and factor basis on the same line as the number is not a measurement; it is a number.

Settled decisions this document builds on and does not reopen:

Graph decision Node What it fixes here
Dual reporting: activity-based allocation layered on an HCMI-comparable baseline ae05e756 Section 3 prints both figures; neither replaces the other
Bottom-up sub-metered/engineering attribution for wellness amenities over per-m2 water-surface benchmarks and regression marginal effects 1c627474 Section 3 and 5: pools and thermal suites are built from bathers, water and nameplate, benchmarks are plausibility checks
Embodied emissions of wasted food stay in the purchases bucket; waste line uses disposal-only factors fbddd1f2 Section 2 tier T2 definition and Section 3 F&B lines
LL97 prescribed coefficient as primary electricity factor, eGRID NYCW as location-based sensitivity, marginal/TOU rejected for the total 862478b9 Section 4
CBECS 2018 Table E2 lodging end uses as the base-building split, PNNL 2010 as cross-check dce656a1 Section 3 worked example and Section 5 metering priorities

1. Functional units

1.1 The five denominators and when each is the right one

Denominator Use it for Do not use it for Anchor figures (source)
Per occupied room-night (OCRM) Benchmarking against CHSB/HCMI; a guest-facing per-stay figure; LightStay Comparing hotels with different occupancy or guests per room without stating both CHSB CY2023 NYC luxury median 28.39, IQR 23.95–38.19 (note 01)
Per guest-night Comparing with Puig 2017, Filimonau 2011, Huang 2015; per-person claims Anything where guests per room was not measured 1.80–1.92 guests per occupied room in full-service hotels (Attari & Hanrahan 2026, note 01)
Per m2 of conditioned space per year Building efficiency; LL84/LL97 compliance; Hilton's intensity metric; separating area-per-key from intensity Guest-facing claims; comparing hotels with very different service programmes CHSB NYC luxury 88.68 kgCO2e/m2 and 321.61 kWh/m2 (note 01); LL97 Hotel cap 0.00987 then 0.00385 tCO2e/ft2 (note 06)
Per cover (restaurant guest, event guest) F&B and banqueting; the only unit on which food procurement can be benchmarked Kitchens whose covers are not counted Dolder Grand 5.4–6.1 kgCO2e per restaurant guest, 7.8–8.0 per event guest 2023–24 (report, F&B section)
Per bather or visit Pools, thermal suites, spa Climate-corrected pool benchmarks Kampel 2016: energy "shall be preferably normalised with respect to the number of visitors"; Nitter poster 6.7–35 kWh per bather, bather load plus opening days explain 77% of variance (notes 07, 02)
Per kg linen Laundry, on-site or outsourced; the only unit on which a towel-reuse programme can be audited Reporting laundry per room-night without stating kg per room TRSA 2018: 1.57 kWh/kg, 12.3 L/kg, 0.41 kgCO2/kg; HCMI default 5.12 kg per occupied room (notes 02, 01)
Per m2 of meeting space per hour Meetings and events on the HCMI basis Catering footprints (it is an area-apportioned building number) CHSB NYC luxury Measure 7 median 0.0457, IQR 0.0425–0.0534 (note 01)

Rule: report per occupied room-night and per m2 always, per guest-night whenever guests per room was recorded, and per cover, per bather and per kg linen for the amenity whose footprint is being claimed. Never report one alone.

1.2 Conversions, with the studies that show rankings flip

Room-night to guest-night. Divide by measured guests per occupied room; where unmeasured, use 1.80–1.92 and say so (Attari & Hanrahan 2026 Table 1). Huang et al. 2015 report 50 kgCO2e per room-night and 29 per guest-night for 58 Taiwanese luxury hotels, an implied 1.72 guests per room (own arithmetic, note 07). Trap: Morales et al. 2026 define "guest-night" as an occupied room-night, so their 95.9 kgCO2e per guest-night is a per-room figure (note 01). Check the definition before converting.

Room-night to available room-night. Multiply by occupancy. The ITP/Greenview 2010 baseline of 9.227 tCO2 per available room per year becomes about 39 kgCO2e per occupied room-night at 65% occupancy (own arithmetic in note 01); IHG's 0.0258 tCO2e per available room is about 25.8 kgCO2e per available room-night and cannot be compared with CHSB's per-occupied-room medians without IHG's occupancy (report). Puig et al. 2017 is the canonical flip: Spanish 2-star hotels at 30% occupancy emit 23 kgCO2e per overnight stay, 5-star hotels at 50% emit 22, 4-star at 90–100% emit 12. Per guest the budget hotel is the dirtiest; per available room it is not. Arenhart et al. 2024 find the pooled occupancy–energy correlation non-significant (r = 0.20) and negative for upscale hotels (−0.21), which is why per-room-night figures move inversely with occupancy while building energy barely moves (note 01).

Room-night to m2. CHSB CY2023 US luxury is 2.3x upper-midscale per occupied room-night (32.12 versus 14.03) but 1.43x per m2 (83.83 versus 58.77 kgCO2e/m2). Roughly 60% of the luxury premium is floor area per occupied room, 40% intensity (note 01 inference). NYC luxury is lower than US luxury per room-night (28.4 versus 32.1) while higher per m2 of energy (322 versus 301 kWh/m2): the grid and high-rise area-per-key dominate (note 01). Xuchao, Priyadarsini and Lee 2010 is the peer-reviewed authority: "a hotel's carbon intensity ranking is rather sensitive to the normalizing denominator chosen" (note 07, abstract verified). Hotel Energy Solutions 2011 found no statistically significant kWh/m2 difference between star ratings in Europe, while CHSB finds a 1.43x per-m2 gap in the US: two sources that disagree about intensity but agree that the per-room-night gap is mostly area (notes 04, 01).

Pandemic-year artefact. CHSB CY2021 luxury energy per occupied room rose 24.26% while energy per m2 fell 18.91% (note 04); across all hotels in CHSB 2024, energy per m2 fell 9.59% while energy per occupied room rose 9.26%. The building did not get worse; the denominator shrank. Any trend in a per-room-night series across 2020–2022 must be shown with the per-m2 series beside it.

Per cover. A cover must be defined as a served meal (restaurant guest, banquet guest), not a reservation or a room-night. The Dolder Grand's per-event-guest figure jumped from 7.8–8.0 (2023–24) to 19.7 kgCO2e (2025) when it moved to ecoinvent 3.10 and IPCC 2021 GWP100; the report records that these must not be averaged (report, F&B section). A factor-set change is a restatement, not a trend.

Per bather. Kampel 2016's visitor normalisation is the standard; the kWh-per-visitor statistics attributed to Kampel (mean 26, median about 22, range 10–80) come via Smedegård 2021 and are not in the 2016 abstract (note 07). Make-up water is standardised at 30 L per bather (Sport England, PWTAG, DIN 19643); heating it from 10 to 29 °C is 0.66 kWh thermal per bather (note 02, own arithmetic there). Do not convert pool energy per m2 of water surface into per bather without a bather count: the published per-m2 range is 1,000–11,000 kWh/m2 WS (Kampel 2013) and the most-quoted values (4,300/5,200) are unsourced (note 07).

Per kg linen. Soiled linen per occupied room is reported at 4.5–6.8 kg by trade sources, 8–55 lb (3.6–25 kg) as an observed industry range with a 5-diamond resort at 83 lb on a peak day (notes 04, 08). HCMI's 5.12 kg default is a mean for an unspecified segment. A luxury property reporting laundry per room-night without a measured kg figure has not measured laundry.

2. System boundary tiers

2.1 Tier definitions

Tier Name Includes Excludes Standards basis
T0 Scope 1+2, operational control On-site fuel combustion (gas, oil, LPG, CHP fuel), owned vehicles, refrigerant top-up; purchased electricity, district steam, chilled water Everything bought from outside the fence; outsourced laundry GHG Protocol Corporate Standard; Hilton and Marriott managed-estate inventories; LL97 (electricity, gas, steam, oil only)
T1 HCMI / CHSB Measure 1 T0 plus outsourced laundry (contractor energy by volume, or 180 kWh electricity + 1,560 kWh gas + 111 L oil per tonne, or 5.12 kg per occupied room), apportioned to rooms and meeting space by conditioned floor area Food, waste, water, consumables, guest and staff travel, leased space, embodied HCMI v2.0 sec 3.0, 5.3.3, 6.3 (note 01)
T2 Operational Scope 3 T1 plus purchased food and beverages cradle-to-gate (including the share later wasted, per decision fbddd1f2), waste disposal at disposal-only factors, water supply and treatment, consumables and amenities, staff commuting, contracted guest transport if the hotel pays Embodied emissions of the building, FF&E, capital goods; guest travel to the destination GHG Protocol Scope 3 categories 1, 5, 7; Attari & Hanrahan 2026; Dolder Grand inventories
T3 Life cycle T2 plus construction, renovation, FF&E and durable goods amortised over a stated life, plus guest travel if the functional unit is "the trip" Nothing by design, but every line beyond T2 is a model, not a meter ISO 14067 (Puig 2017 functional unit), Filimonau 2011 LCEA, Rosselló-Batle 2010

The tier is part of the number. "28 kgCO2e per room-night" is a T1 statement; "40 kgCO2e per room-night" is a T2 statement; both can be true of the same hotel in the same year.

2.2 Which published numbers belong to which tier

LL84 Portfolio Manager GHG intensities (Manhattan luxury peers 7–17 kgCO2e/ft2) T0, location-based with eGRID year-specific factors Factor changed 81.4 → 92.8 → 73.8 kgCO2e/MBtu across CY2013–2021 (note 06)
LL97 compliance emissions T0 with prescribed coefficients Not a GHG Protocol inventory; RECs and AHRF offsets deductible under stated limits
