2.3 Typical uplift between tiers

Step Uplift on the lower tier Source and conditions
T0 → T1 (add outsourced laundry) +5–10% at 5.12 kg per room at 0.41 kgCO2/kg (2.1 kg on a 28 kg night, own arithmetic); Filimonau's 1.75 kg laundry on 4.5–7.5 kg operational is +23–39% TRSA 2018, HCMI default; Filimonau 2011 (notes 02, 07). Vendor rule of thumb "laundry +5–10%" (NetNada) is unverified (note 01)
T1 → T2 (add food, waste, consumables) +70–130% (Attari & Hanrahan, Irish grid 0.226 kg/kWh); +40–70% from breakfast plus laundry alone (Filimonau, UK 0.58 kg/kWh); F&B 48–56% of total at Dolder (Zurich hydro-nuclear) versus 19% of Accor group (energy 65%) The uplift is larger on a cleaner grid because the denominator shrinks, not because the food changes. Report the F&B share with the electricity factor beside it (report, F&B section)
T2 → T3 (add embodied) +15% on operational (Filimonau's uplift); operating phase 70–80% of 50-year life-cycle energy (Rosselló-Batle 2010), i.e. embodied adds roughly 25–43% on operational (own arithmetic); amenities plus laundry about 16% of a 148 kg T3 night (Taiwan) Rosselló-Batle abstract only; Taiwan method unread (notes 07, 04)

What does not work: adding a T2 food line to a T1 benchmark and calling the result "the footprint". The Best Western Mannheim "climate neutral" claim fell in Landgericht Berlin II because the calculation covered electricity and gas but "did not adequately account for other relevant emissions sources, including waste disposal and laundry services" (note 05). A guest-facing figure must be at least T2, or must say it is T1.

What also does not work: SHA's hotel food-system split (62% sourcing, 19% kitchen, 17% waste, 2% packaging) stacked without netting. The 17% waste share carries the embodied emissions of the wasted food, which already sit in the 62% (decision fbddd1f2; report).

3. Allocation of shared loads to rooms, meetings, F&B and wellness

3.1 The HCMI floor-area method, exactly

HCMI v2.0 takes the whole-building T1 inventory, removes private space (leased restaurants, shops, staff housing) by sub-meter or area, and splits the remainder between guest rooms and meeting space "based on the ratio of space occupied by guest rooms and meeting space", with heart of house, spa, retail, fitness, public space and restaurants "allocated proportionately to Rooms and Meeting Space". It "assumes equal intensity for all areas of the hotel", "does not account for different room sizes", and measures the 12-month average, "not the incremental GHG emissions from a guest room" (note 01, sec 6.3, 6.4, 7.2). Rooms footprint / occupied rooms = per occupied room; meetings footprint / 365 / 10 h / m2 = per m2-hour, with the 10 h/day utilisation a standard assumption that "varies considerably from hotel to hotel" (sec 7.3). The draft v3.0 (April 2026) still apportions by floor area after deducting private space (note 04).

Two consequences that must be stated whenever an HCMI figure is used for an amenity question: a guest who never enters the spa carries the same footprint as one who does, and a hotel with a large under-used ballroom shifts footprint away from rooms. For NYC luxury the apportionment removes only about 5% from rooms (Measure 1 28.39 versus Measure 3 27.02, note 01).

3.2 Activity-based alternative, following the Product Standard hierarchy

The GHG Protocol Product Standard says to avoid allocation by subdividing where possible, then allocate on a physical relationship, then by economic value; ISO 14067 follows ISO 14044 and does not permit system expansion (note 01, secondary wording, to be checked against the standards). Applied to a hotel:

  1. Subdivide into service processes with sub-meters where they exist and with the CBECS 2018 Table E2 lodging end-use structure where they do not (decision dce656a1): space heating 15.7, cooling 6.0, ventilation 12.5, water heating 16.5, lighting 7.2, cooking 14.1, refrigeration 4.2, other 12.4 kBtu/ft2, each conditional on buildings using the end use (sum 90.2 against a total of 85.7).
  2. Allocate each process by a physical key: guestroom HVAC, lighting and hot water to occupied room-nights; cooking, kitchen refrigeration and dining to covers; laundry to kg linen; meeting space to m2-hours (as HCMI); pools and thermal suites to bathers or visits (Kampel 2016); spa treatments to treatment-hours plus linen per treatment; elevators, lobby, corridors, back of house and staff commuting by floor area or guest-nights.
  3. Use revenue shares only where no physical key exists, and say so.
  4. Print the HCMI figure beside the activity-based one (decision ae05e756).

What does not work: regression marginal effects for amenities. No large-N hotel model carries a pool, spa or F&B term (ENERGY STAR hotel score: rooms, workers, commercial refrigeration, degree-days, kitchen flag; Chan 2005; Wang 2012); the one with a pool term (Santiago 2021, n = 6) has paywalled coefficients; the Urban Green Council found NYC fitness centres had no measurable effect on carbon intensity (decision 1c627474; note 01). Per-m2 water-surface benchmarks also do not work as attribution, for the reasons in 1.2.

3.3 Worked example on the graph's NYC luxury defaults

Inputs, all from the notes: 375 keys, most rooms over 570 ft2, 43,000 ft2 of event space, a 22,000 ft2 spa and 7,000 ft2 fitness centre, no confirmed guest pool (note 06, post-reopening); Manhattan luxury-class occupancy 76.4% (PwC H1 2025, note 06); CHSB CY2023 NYC luxury medians 102.31 kWh and 28.39 kgCO2e per occupied room, 321.61 kWh/m2 and 88.68 kgCO2e/m2 (note 01); CBECS 2018 Table E2 shares (decision dce656a1); HCMI laundry default 5.12 kg per occupied room and TRSA 1.57 kWh/kg (notes 01, 02); CIBSE TM46 fitness centre 160 kWh/m2 electricity plus 440 fossil (report); the only metered hotel-spa electricity, 7.24–11.22 kWh/m2/month in Bali (note 08). Everything below is own arithmetic on those inputs.

Step 1, size the benchmark hotel. Occupied room-nights = 375 x 365 x 0.764 = 104,570. Site energy at the CHSB median = 102.31 x 104,570 = 10.70 GWh. The conditioned area that makes 10.70 GWh equal 321.61 kWh/m2 is 33,270 m2 (358,000 ft2), or 88.7 m2 (955 ft2) per key. This is the hotel the CHSB medians describe. It is not the Waldorf: if even 60% of the Waldorf's 1.67 million ft2 is hotel, that is about 2,700 ft2 per key (note 06), so the benchmark hotel is a third of the Waldorf's area per key and the real building will sit far above these figures on any per-room denominator before a single kWh of inefficiency.

Step 2, whole-hotel T0/T1 footprint. 88.68 kgCO2e/m2 x 33,270 m2 = 2,950 tCO2e per year, 28.2 kgCO2e per occupied room-night (CHSB Measure 3 median for the segment is 27.02; consistent). Average carbon intensity of delivered energy = 2,950 t / 10.70 GWh = 0.276 kgCO2e/kWh, a location-based eGRID figure embedded in CHSB.

Step 3, HCMI allocation. Guestroom area = 375 x 570 ft2 = 213,750 ft2 = 19,860 m2 (corridors omitted because unknown; HCMI would include them and push more onto rooms). Meeting area = 43,000 ft2 = 3,995 m2. Rooms share = 19,860 / (19,860 + 3,995) = 83.3%; meetings 16.7%. The remaining 9,415 m2 (spa, gym, F&B, lobby, back of house) is spread pro rata.

  • Rooms: 0.833 x 2,950 = 2,456 t → 23.5 kgCO2e per occupied room-night (HCMI Measure 1 equivalent).
  • Meetings: 494 t / 365 / 10 h / 3,995 m2 = 0.034 kgCO2e per m2-hour, below the CHSB NYC luxury IQR of 0.0425–0.0534. The reason is the 115 ft2 of event space per key: a large banqueting programme dilutes the per-m2-hour figure and pulls footprint off the rooms, exactly the artefact note 01 describes.

Step 4, activity-based allocation of the same 2,950 t.

Process Physical basis Energy (MWh) tCO2e at 0.276 Per unit
Kitchens (cooking 14.1 + refrigeration 4.2 of 90.2 CBECS) 20.3% of site energy 2,171 599 5.7 kg per occupied room-night; per cover needs a cover count (see below)
Laundry, on-site 5.12 kg x 104,570 = 535 t linen x 1.57 kWh/kg 841 232 2.2 kg per room-night; 0.43 kgCO2e/kg (TRSA plants: 0.41)
Fitness centre 650 m2 x 600 kWh/m2 (TM46) 390 108 1.0 kg per room-night if spread; per visit needs a count
Spa (treatment rooms only) 2,044 m2 x 135 kWh/m2 (Bali upper value x 12, a lower bound; thermal suite not included) 276 76 0.7 kg per room-night; a 10 kW sauna at 50% for 14 h/day adds about 25,600 kWh/yr each (report)
Remainder (HVAC, lighting, DHW, other), split 83.3/16.7 by area 7,021 MWh 7,021 1,938 Rooms 1,613 t = 15.4 kg per room-night; meetings 325 t = 0.022 kg per m2-hour
Total 10,699 2,953 28.2 kg per room-night (identity check holds)

The dual report for the room, T1: HCMI 23.5 kgCO2e per occupied room-night beside activity-based 15.4. The 8.1 kg gap is kitchens, laundry and wellness that HCMI smears onto the room. Neither is wrong; they answer different questions, and a hotel publishing one must publish the other.

Per cover check. If this hotel serves two covers per occupied room-night across three venues and banqueting (an illustrative assumption, not a finding), 209,000 covers carry 599 t of kitchen energy, 2.9 kgCO2e per cover. Metered pub kitchens give 1.5–3.3 kWh electricity per meal and an inferred hotel range of 0.5–1.5 kgCO2e per cover (note 03). The CBECS cooking share applied to a luxury hotel therefore gives roughly twice the metered-kitchen figure: either covers are higher than two per room (banqueting), or CBECS's lodging cooking share overstates this hotel. The only resolution is a kitchen sub-meter and a cover count, which is why both are first on the metering list in Section 5.

Step 5, T2 extension. At two covers per room-night and the Dolder's 5.4–6.1 kgCO2e per restaurant guest, embodied food adds 10.8–12.2 kg per room-night; food waste at 0.4 kg per cover and the DEFRA disposal-only factor of 0.627 kgCO2e/kg adds 0.5 kg (decision fbddd1f2). T2 per occupied room-night is about 40 kgCO2e, a 43% uplift on T1. That sits at the low end of the published 40–130% range, as it should on a grid where energy carries 0.276 kgCO2e/kWh rather than Zurich's or Ireland's. Per guest-night at 1.85 guests per room: T1 15.2, T2 21.8.

Step 6, factor sensitivity (Section 4). For every 100 kWh of electricity per occupied room-night, switching from eGRID NYCW (0.392) to the LL97 coefficient (0.289) removes 10.3 kgCO2e. On a 102 kWh night that is more than a third of the T1 total.

4. Emission factors

4.1 Three electricity factors, three jobs

Factor Value Use for Do not use for
Location-based, eGRID2023 NYCW 864.5 lb CO2/MWh = 0.392 kgCO2e/kWh Benchmarking against CHSB (eGRID embedded), LL84 Portfolio Manager peers, GHG Protocol location-based line, Hilton location-based LL97 compliance; target tracking on a market basis
Regulatory, LL97 prescribed 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh (2024–29) = 0.289; 0.000145 (2030–34), or hourly TOU on NYISO Zone J marginal data with hourly metering LL97 compliance, penalty exposure ($268/t over the cap), the primary factor in this project (decision 862478b9) Comparability with any published study; the 2030 value is a policy projection (CLCPA 70% renewables), not a measurement
Marginal grid coefficient 0.247 (2024) → 0.114 (2029) tCO2e/MWh Valuing beneficial electrification (heat pumps replacing gas or steam) under 1 RCNY 103-14 Footprint totals (rejected in decision 862478b9)
Market-based, contractual Contract-specific; Zone J Tier 4 RECs (CHPE from 2026, Clean Path NY expected 2027) Hilton and Marriott target tracking (both measure progress market-based); LL97 REC deduction Guest-facing claims without the location-based figure beside it

Sources: note 06 (eGRID, 1 RCNY 103-14, DOB REC policy), note 05 (Hilton, Marriott).

Other carriers: natural gas 0.00005311 tCO2e/kBtu (about 0.181 kgCO2e per kWh of fuel) for LL97, unchanged 2030; district steam 0.00004493 → 0.0000432 tCO2e/kBtu, a 3.9% relief against a 50% drop for electricity; No. 2 oil 0.00007421 (note 06). Con Edison's 2018 delivered-steam footprint of 110 lb CO2/Mlb brackets the LL97 steam coefficient, so the coefficient is not a concession (note 06). Refrigerants: measured top-up, not HCMI's 1% uplift, where data exist (note 01). For the GHG Protocol inventory use a DEFRA or EPA fuel factor and state it; the LL97 coefficients are compliance numbers.

4.2 Which factor for which purpose

Benchmarking: location-based, because CHSB, LL84 and the peer-reviewed literature are location-based. CHSB's own methodology: eGRID for US electricity, renewables on-site counted as zero, market-based intensities only in member reports (note 01).

LL97 compliance: the prescribed coefficient, period. Portfolio Manager GHG intensities overstate LL97-basis electricity emissions by about 36%, so a Manhattan hotel at 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 on LL84 is not necessarily 11.3 on LL97 (note 06). Year-one results: 604 hotels, 91% within limits, the lowest compliance rate among the large property types (note 06).

Target tracking: market-based is what Hilton and Marriott report against, and the gap is small (Hilton 2025: 1,965,471 market versus 2,084,001 location tCO2e, about 6%; Marriott 2.92% of energy from renewables) (note 05). Any per-stay figure that leans on market-based Scope 2 must show the location-based figure in the same table.

4.3 The constraints that bind from 2026

SBTi Corporate Net-Zero Standard V2.0 (published 11 June 2026, mandatory from 31 January 2028): Scope 2 contracts count only for plants up to 15 years old (existing contracts grandfathered), procured within the same deliverability region as consumption, with hourly-matching reporting for large consumers and recognition thresholds of at least 50% hourly matching to 2030, 75% to 2035, 90% after; carbon credits "do not count as reductions toward meeting near-term or long-term science-based targets" in V1.3.1 and that line is kept in V2.0 (note 05). The GHG Protocol Scope 2 revision (consulted October 2025 to January 2026, final expected 2027) proposes the same direction: hourly matching and deliverability (note 05).

LL97 Tier 4 REC constraint: RECs "may only be deducted from the emissions attributed to consumption of utility supplied electricity", must come from a NYISO capacity resource located in or delivering into Zone J, be solely owned and retired by the owner for the same reporting year; the only eligible sources to date are CHPE (operational June 2026) and Clean Path NY (expected 2027); owners on the decarbonisation-plan good-faith pathway may not use RECs in 2024–29; break-even against the $268/t penalty is $77/MWh in 2024–29 and about $39/MWh in 2030–34, when "the cost of RECs is likely to exceed the cost of maximum penalties"; AHRF offsets are capped at 10% of the limit (note 06). A gas-fired plant, such as the Waldorf's eight condensing boilers and 555 kW CHP (report), gets nothing from any of this: RECs do not touch Scope 1.

Zone J-deliverable CHPE RECs are the one instrument that satisfies LL97, SBTi V2 deliverability and the GHG Protocol direction at once; unbundled national Green-e RECs of the type Hyatt cites are the instrument most at risk (report).

5. Data quality

5.1 What to meter, in priority order

The evidence base contains one public sub-metered guestroom dataset (PNNL, 32 of 300-plus rooms, 1–5 minute intervals, October 2009 to January 2010), one metered hotel pool (Kraków, heat meters, no pool size, no winter months), one metered hotel spa (Bali, electricity only), zero metered hotel saunas, steam rooms, whirlpools or kitchens (notes 04, 02, 08, 03). Metering priority follows where the allocation keys are weakest:

  1. Kitchen electricity and gas (cooking, refrigeration, extraction, dishwashing), with a cover count by venue and by service (breakfast buffet, à la carte, banquet). PNNL's gas was split 51% DHW, 25% kitchen, 25% laundry by proxy metering; a sub-meter replaces the proxy (note 04).
  2. Laundry electricity, gas and water, with linen scales (pounds per occupied room is the industry's own metric, 8–55 PPOR observed) (note 08).
  3. Guestroom HVAC on a sample of at least 10% of rooms (PNNL's fraction), with outdoor temperature, because PNNL's PTHP load correlated with outdoor temperature at R2 = 0.92 and a control fault had many units in resistance mode (note 04). Without the temperature series the occupancy-control saving (18.4% mean, 10.8–26.5% range, DOE/PNNL 2012) cannot be separated from weather (decision b21e5837).
  4. Pool and thermal suite: heat meters on pool water and hall air, electricity on pumps and dehumidification, make-up water meter, and a bather count. Kampel 2016: water usage and visitor numbers, not climate, drive pool energy; Nitter: bather load plus opening days explain 77% (notes 07, 02). Sport England's 30 L per bather make-up and 28–30 °C water give the physics check.
  5. Elevators (PNNL metered 141 kWh/day, about 1% of electricity; no high-rise luxury measurement exists) (note 04).
  6. DHW by zone (guest floors, spa, kitchen, laundry) because no source separates hot from cold water for luxury guests and the fixture arithmetic (250 L tub = 8.4 kWh heat; 10-minute rain shower = 3.9 kWh) rests on residential volumes (note 04).

5.2 What to survey

Guests per occupied room (the per-guest-night denominator); covers by venue and service type; bathers, spa visits and treatment hours; linen kg per occupied room by room type (standard versus suite, which HCMI ignores); actual meeting-space hours rather than HCMI's 10 h x 365; housekeeping opt-out uptake (Marriott did not disclose it; Hyatt Centric reported an 18% linen reduction) (note 04); minibar transactions (about 70% of travellers never use one, so energy per transaction is several kWh) (report); airport transfers booked through the hotel and the vehicle class (DEFRA luxury petrol 0.319, diesel 0.207, PHEV 0.121 kgCO2e/km) (note 04); hotel-only versus residential floor area where the lot is mixed (the Waldorf is benchmarked as one 1.67 million ft2 "Hotel" containing 372 condominiums) (note 06).

5.3 Red flags

Occupancy-insensitive electricity. If total electricity does not move with occupied rooms, either the base load dominates (PNNL: about 400 kW base on a 300-room hotel; public-area HVAC on 100% outside air continuously) or the meter is wrong. Arenhart's pooled occupancy correlation of 0.20 (n.s.) and −0.21 for upscale says the first is normal; it also says that a per-room-night saving claimed from an occupancy change is a denominator effect (note 01).

EUI-only reporting. The Waldorf scored ENERGY STAR 100 in CY2018 while closed and 97 in CY2024 while under construction (note 06). HES 2011 found no significant EUI difference by star rating (note 04). An EUI without occupancy, guests per room, covers and area per key is not a service footprint; it is a building statistic. GFA restatements move it materially: Pierre +30%, Carlyle +71%, Four Seasons +27% between CY2023 and CY2024 filings (note 06). Report absolute tCO2e and the area used.

Estimated or default values. The Waldorf CY2024 LL84 row carries default_values = Yes and estimated_values_energy = Yes (note 08); the graph rejected it as performance data (decision 50ff3638).

Vendor savings claims. Occupancy controls: vendor-linked studies claim 33–50% on two to four rooms or uncontrolled comparisons; the DOE/PNNL six-hotel demonstration measured 18.4% mean with two independent studies finding savings not statistically significant or eroded by override (note 04; decision b21e5837). Toiletry dispensers: "95% less plastic" and a supplier's 600 tCO2e are plastic-mass and internal accounting, and the LCA a vendor calculator cites could not be located (note 04). Food-service and laundry "rules of thumb" (+10–15%, +5–10%) are unverified (note 01). A savings claim without a control group, a sample size and a baseline period is marketing.

Factor-set changes inside a time series. Portfolio Manager's eGRID factor moved 81.4 → 82.9 → 92.8 → 84.7 → 79.5 → 73.8 kgCO2e/MBtu across CY2013–2021; the US luxury CHSB decline from 43.3 to 32.1 kgCO2e per room-night (CY2016–2023) is mostly grid factor and sample change, since energy per room fell only from 144 to 119 kWh (notes 06, 01). The Dolder's per-event-guest figure jumped 2.5x on a database change (report).

Unverified data. CHSB: "about 20 percent of the members, covering approximately 71 percent of hotels in the dataset" assured to ISO 14064; the 2024 report says "the data have not been verified" at property level; outlier screens accept luxury records between 50 and 700 kWh per occupied room (notes 01, 04). Dolder: "checked for completeness and plausibility, but have not been audited" (report). Marriott's purchased goods are spend-based with 0% supplier data; Accor said its spend-based Scope 3 "has reached its limits" (note 01).

Denominator mislabels. "Guest-night" used for a room-night (Morales 2026); "efficiency gap" ratios in CHSB PDFs that are upper-to-lower-quartile dispersion, not intensities (note 01); "carbon per available room" compared with "per occupied room" (IHG versus CHSB).

Unsourced benchmark values. The 4,300/5,200 kWh/m2 WS pool figures (Trianti-Stourna, "no indication about where these numbers originate from"); the 1,094.5 kWh/m2 WS figure (unlocated); "up to 35% of total energy for a spa" (unsourced page); SHA's kitchen 15–25% (interview-based, "reliable industry wide data is not currently available") (notes 07, report).

6. Reporting and claims

6.1 What the law now allows

From 27 September 2026, Directive (EU) 2024/825 lists as a per se unfair practice any claim that a product has a neutral, reduced or positive environmental impact based on offsetting outside the product's value chain; generic environmental claims without recognised excellent performance; and sustainability labels not based on a certification scheme or public authority. Communicating about credit investments is permitted if not presented as compensation (note 05; Annex wording from legal summaries, EUR-Lex not fetched). The Green Claims Directive is stalled since June 2025.

Courts have already applied existing unfair-practice law: Amsterdam District Court, 20 March 2024, 15 of 19 KLM statements misleading; LG Köln and OLG Köln (13 December 2024) on Eurowings "CO2-neutral" flights offset "for a few euros", with details required in the advertisement itself, not behind links; BGH 27 June 2024, I ZR 98/23, "klimaneutral" permitted only if the advertisement makes clear whether emissions are reduced or offset; Landgericht Berlin II, DUH v German Hospitality Management (Sure Hotel by Best Western Mannheim), "climate neutral" stays prohibited because laundry and waste were omitted from the calculation (note 05). The UK CMA can fine up to 10% of global turnover since 6 April 2025 and the ASA banned "Eco Resort" and "Eco Hotel" wording in September 2026; the US FTC Green Guides remain at their 2012 revision, which already requires disclosure where offset benefits occur more than two years out (note 05). Booking.com withdrew its Travel Sustainable badge in March 2024 after the Dutch ACM objected (note 05).

6.2 The disclosure-without-neutralisation posture

The graph's Facet 5 ruling (decision 11d0368f): physical decarbonisation plus disclosure-without-neutralisation and value-chain insetting, with amenity choice architecture as the guest-facing lever; avoidance credits for "carbon-neutral stays" rejected; market-based Scope 2, durable removals and guest-funded packages treated as reporting or contribution instruments, never as the basis of a neutrality claim. The accounting rules agree: SBTi V1.3.1 and V2.0 exclude credits from target achievement; the Oxford Principles 2024 say "most offsetting today is still not net zero aligned" and call "carbon neutral" a "less rigorous, interim claim"; West et al. 2023 tie only 6.1% of REDD+ credits from 18 projects to additional reductions, Badgley et al. 2022 find 29.4% over-crediting in California forest offsets (note 05). Durable removals run about $180/t against about $35/t for forestry and about $6 spot for generic credits; on a 50–150 kgCO2e night that is $9–27, 1–2% of a $1,500 rate, and a mandatory levy at that level must be marketed as a contribution, not neutrality (note 05 inference; report).

A per-stay disclosure that satisfies the BGH test, the 2024/825 blacklist, the Best Western ruling and SBTi V2 at once carries, on one line:

  1. The number, with its functional unit (per occupied room-night, and per guest-night with the guests-per-room ratio used).
  2. The boundary tier (T2 minimum for a guest-facing figure, naming food, laundry and waste as included; T1 if that is all there is, said plainly).
  3. The allocation method (HCMI floor-area figure beside the activity-based figure; which amenities are attributed per use and which are spread).
  4. The electricity factor basis and year (location-based eGRID for comparability; LL97 coefficient for compliance; market-based only with the location-based figure beside it).
  5. The data quality (metered, estimated, modelled; assurance level; the CHSB or LL84 figure it was checked against).
  6. Reductions achieved in the inventory, named (electrification, Zone J RECs, menu change), with their effect size.
  7. Any credits or removals bought, described as a contribution with registry retirements, in a separate sentence that does not contain the words neutral, net zero, offset or compensate.

What does not work, by name: "carbon-neutral stay" or "climate-neutral meeting" on avoidance credits (Best Western, Eurowings, KLM, 2024/825); a T1 figure presented as "the footprint of your stay" (Best Western, laundry and waste); a market-based Scope 2 figure without its location-based twin (SBTi V2 hourly matching and deliverability; GHG Protocol revision); "Eco Hotel" without substantiation (ASA, September 2026); a tree-planting claim without survival data (Populus, 80% seedling mortality in year one) (notes 05, report).

7. One-page checklist

Functional unit

  • Per occupied room-night and per m2 of conditioned space reported; per guest-night reported with the measured or stated guests-per-room ratio (1.80–1.92 default, Attari & Hanrahan 2026).
  • Occupancy stated as an explicit input, not folded into a segment multiplier (Puig 2017; Arenhart 2024).
  • Per cover, per bather, per kg linen, per m2-hour used for the amenity whose footprint is claimed; the count behind each denominator stated.
  • "Guest-night" checked for whether the source means a room-night (Morales 2026).

Boundary

  • Tier (T0/T1/T2/T3) stated on the same line as the number.
  • Guest-facing figure at least T2 (food, laundry, waste included) or labelled T1.
  • Food purchases carry the embodied emissions of what is later wasted; waste line uses disposal-only factors (DEFRA about 627 kgCO2e/t landfill, about 9 compost) (decision fbddd1f2).
  • Food factor set named (Poore & Nemecek, Clune, Agribalyse/CONCITO differ 3.5x on beef) with a sensitivity.
  • F&B share reported with the electricity factor beside it.

Allocation

  • HCMI floor-area figure (rooms per occupied room-night; meetings per m2-hour at stated utilisation hours) computed and printed.
  • Activity-based figure computed from sub-meters or the CBECS 2018 E2 structure, with physical keys; revenue used only where stated.
  • Identity check: activity lines sum to the whole-hotel total.
  • Pools and thermal suites built bottom-up (bathers, make-up water, nameplate x duty); per-m2 WS benchmarks used only as plausibility checks (decision 1c627474).
  • Area per key stated, and the benchmark hotel's implied area per key compared with the real building's.

Factors

  • Location-based (eGRID NYCW 0.392) and LL97 (0.289; 0.145 from 2030) columns both shown; market-based column only with the location-based beside it (decision 862478b9).
  • Marginal/TOU factors used only to value electrification measures, not in totals.
  • Gas, steam and refrigerant factors stated with source and year; refrigerants from measured top-up where available.
  • Any REC claimed is Zone J-deliverable, same-year, solely owned and retired, and applied to electricity only.

Data quality

  • Kitchens, laundry, a 10% guestroom HVAC sample, pool/thermal suite and elevators sub-metered or flagged as modelled.
  • Covers, bathers, linen kg, guests per room, meeting hours and opt-out uptake counted, not assumed.
  • Estimated or default utility values flagged; closed or construction years excluded from performance series.
  • Factor-set changes identified and series restated, not trended through.
  • Savings claims carry sample size, control group and baseline period; vendor figures labelled as such.
  • Assurance level stated (none, limited, reasonable; standard).

Claims

  • No "neutral", "net zero", "offset" or "compensate" on a guest-facing claim; no uncertified sustainability label (Directive 2024/825 from 27 Sep 2026; BGH I ZR 98/23; DUH v Best Western).
  • Reductions achieved named with effect sizes; credits or removals described as a contribution with registry retirements, in a separate sentence.
  • Boundary, denominator, factor basis and data quality printed with the number wherever the number appears.

Intervention library: every lever, policy event and shock the evidence quantifies

Companion to 03_intervention_library.json (schema hotel-carbon-interventions/1, generated 2026-10-24). 45 records. Effect columns are low / mid / high in the unit of the effect type; where mid is a midpoint of a stated range or a ratio of two stated numbers the JSON notes field says so, and nothing is estimated beyond that. Confidence: high = measured in hotels or prescribed in law; medium = measured outside hotels, manufacturer rating, or a stated range whose application needs a split the notes do not give; low = no measured effect (values are bounds, proportionality or a scenario, or null).

Slices: heat, cool, vent, dhw, light, bother (refrigeration, plug, other building loads), pool, sauna (sauna + steam), gym, spa, laundry (energy per kg linen), kitchen (energy per meal), food (purchased food kg CO2e), foodwaste (disposal), waste, minibar, elev, transfers. A policy or shock record with no targets acts on the whole model (caps, claims, occupancy).

Effect types: fraction_reduction = fraction of target slice removed; electrify = share of thermal load electrified; ef_multiplier = share of electricity factor removed (market-based); occupancy_multiplier = multiplier on occupied room-nights; cap_multiplier = multiplier on the 2024-29 LL97 cap (106.2 kgCO2e/m2); treatment_factor = kg CO2e per kg food waste (replaces disposal factor).

Building (base-building HVAC, hot water, lighting, lifts)

elevator_regenerative_drives_standby Regenerative drives and standby (sleep-mode) reduction for elevators elev fraction_reduction 0.3 / 0.35 / 0.4 1 - - Sachs 2005, ACEEE, Opportunities for Elevator Energy Efficiency Improvements (new technologies promise 30-4... low

guestroom_occupancy_hvac_controls - Measured against GUESTROOM HVAC energy on rented days only (unrented-room set-back not evaluated). Site results 26.5/18.3/17.8/23.2/13.7/10.8%, i.e. 167-589 kWh per room per year; earlier utility studies 5.6-28% (62-462 kWh/room/yr). Apply only to the guestroom share of heat and cool, not to public-area or central-plant loads. Guest override and housekeeping behaviour dominate variance; two independent studies found no significant saving, so a luxury property that avoids guest-visible restrictions should be run at the low end unless PMS-integrated. Vendor claims of 33-50% (Verde/Wyndham, Aqua Aloha) rest on 2-4 rooms or uncontrolled comparisons and are excluded. Capex: USD 345-426 installed per room (2012); cost savings USD 20.96-79.03 per room per year.

bathroom_lighting_sensors - Fraction is of BATHROOM lighting only (PIER estimate 46-56%, ~50%); absolute saving 76-154 kWh per bathroom per year against a ~600 kWh/yr baseline of four 60 W lamps left on 24 h; 30-60 MWh/yr for a 400-room hotel. The simulator must apply this to the bathroom sub-share of the light slice (or use the absolute kWh/room/yr), not to all lighting. Leisure hotels at the top of the range, business hotels lower. Baseline assumes incandescent lamps; with LED the absolute saving shrinks proportionally.

led_lighting_retrofit - No hotel-measured percentage exists in the notes. Low/high are the HES/XENIOS European savings POTENTIAL range for lighting (7-60%); mid is the midpoint of that range, not a measurement. The one audited figure (Irish fitness centre, 122,976 kWh/yr from lighting alone) lacks a published baseline so no fraction can be derived. Lighting is 7% of electricity in the PNNL hotel (winter) and 12-18% (up to 40%) of hotel energy in HES; luxury rooms with decorative lighting sit higher.

envelope_glazing_retrofit - No measured effect size in the notes. Lu et al. report double glazing as statistically significant without a coefficient; SOM states the Waldorf has new double glazing with no energy claim. The HES/XENIOS 15-20% heating and 5-30% cooling figures are whole-hotel potentials across all measures, not envelope-specific, and are recorded here only as an upper bound. Included so the simulator can carry an explicit null rather than a guessed number.

heat_pump_space_heating - low/mid/high are the SHARE of space-heating thermal energy moved to electricity (scenario values, not measured). COP = 3 is an ASSUMPTION taken from the notes' own worked example (04 KQ2: '100 L of hot water ... at a heat-pump COP of 3 = 1.6 kWh electricity'); no hotel heat-pump COP was measured in the evidence base. Carbon effect depends on the electricity factor chosen: at LL97 0.289 kg/kWh and COP 3, electric heat is 0.096 kg/kWh-thermal versus gas at 0.181 kg/kWh-fuel before boiler losses (simple arithmetic on the two prescribed coefficients); at eGRID NYCW 0.392 the advantage narrows. The Waldorf installed eight Viessmann gas condensing boilers (DEP certificate CW006321, Jan 2025), so this is a counterfactual for that building. Hotel Marcel (165 rooms, all-electric) is the only Hilton-brand precedent in the notes; it publishes no kWh. Scope shift: Scope 1 natural gas to Scope 2 electricity; earns LL97 beneficial-electrification credit at the marginal grid coefficient (0.247 to 0.114 tCO2e/MWh, 2024-29), bankable; Hilton Scope 1 is 'almost exclusively natural gas'.

heat_pump_dhw - low/mid/high are the SHARE of DHW thermal energy electrified (scenario). COP 3 and 80% gas-boiler efficiency are the notes' own worked assumptions, not measurements; the Viessmann Vitocrossal condensing boilers at the Waldorf would run higher than 80% but no efficiency is in the notes. DHW is ~20% of lodging site energy (CBECS) and 51% of gas at the PNNL hotel, so this lever is of the same order as space heating for a luxury property with 500-800 L of water per occupied room. The Waldorf's DHW heat source is not stated (Holby mixing valves only). Scope shift: Scope 1 gas to Scope 2 electricity; LL97 beneficial-electrification credit applies.

elevator_regenerative_drives_standby - NOT hotel-measured: 30-40% is Sachs's technology promise for office lifts (mid = midpoint); the notes' CIBSE-based arithmetic puts standby at ~25% of a medium-usage hotel lift's energy, which bounds the sleep-mode part. Elevators were ~1% of electricity (0.47 kWh per room-day) at the 12-storey PNNL hotel; a 47-storey tower with express lifts would be several times that per room, but no high-rise luxury measurement exists (KONE/Schindler/Otis ISO 25745 reports not public).

Wellness (pool, sauna/steam, spa, gym)

pool_cover_and_heat_recovery - low = Kampel's 28% national saving potential for Norwegian swimming facilities (whole-facility, all measures incl. heat exchangers and heat pumps on outgoing water and air; climate-corrected, 41 facilities). high = 1 - 378/567 = 0.333, the cover effect in Selectra's residential hot-tub model (modelled, vendor). mid is the midpoint. Evaporation at 46-60%+ of pool-water heat loss sets the physical ceiling for cover-related savings but is not itself a measured saving. Applies to the pool slice (water heating, hall heating, dehumidification, pumps). The Waldorf hotel has NO documented guest pool; the 25 m Starlight Pool is a residents' amenity, so for the hotel guest-night this lever is zero unless access is confirmed.

pool_grey_water_heat_recovery - Fraction is of the energy for POOL-WATER PREHEATING AND DHW only, not the whole pool or DHW slice; mid is the midpoint of the measured 34-67% range. Single measured case study (recreation centre, not a hotel). Make-up water is 30 L per bather (Sport England/PWTAG/DIN 19643; one Polish measurement 40.6 L), heated from ~10 C to 29 C = 0.66 kWh thermal per bather (notes' arithmetic), which bounds the recoverable term.

sauna_steam_scheduling - NOT measured. Under the only available model (nameplate x ~50-55% duty x hours), energy is proportional to hours held hot, so cutting a 24 h/day unit to 16 h/day removes 1/3 (0.33) and to 14 h/day removes 0.42 (10/24). No hotel sub-metering of a sauna, steam room or whirlpool exists in any source reached (Harvia, TyloHelo, Finnleo, KLAFS, Amerec, Mr Steam, Jacuzzi commercial, Carbon Trust, NYSERDA, ASHRAE Journal all checked). The Waldorf's Guerlain spa has an infrared sauna, steam room and hammam (Spa Business, Oct 2025); treat hours and duty as inputs to be metered.

spa_setpoint_management - No measured setpoint effect for a spa exists in the notes. Design temperatures are recorded so the simulator can expose setpoints as inputs; the Bali audit found oversized air conditioning and over-lighting but gives no saving. A 40 C spa pool in a 30 C / 60% RH hall loses heat chiefly by evaporation (higher vapour-pressure differential than a 28 C pool), so setpoint sensitivity is high in physics but unquantified in evidence.

energy_generating_cardio - A messaging measure, not mitigation: the notes' arithmetic puts the offset at 'well under 1%' of gym building energy because lighting and HVAC dominate (TM46 fitness centre 160 kWh/m2 electricity + 440 fossil). low/mid/high (0-1%) encode that bound; no hotel gym has published a measurement. Treadmill plug load itself is 0.1-0.7 kWh per user-hour (metered 2 HP unit at 1-10 mph).

laundry_outsourcing_central_plant - Fraction is of laundry ENERGY PER KG of linen. low = 1 - 1.0/1.6 = 0.375 and high = 1 - 0.8/1.7 = 0.53 from the UK typical-vs-attainable benchmarks, bracketed up to 0.60 by the Lodging/EPA fuel claim; mid = the Lodging/EPA ~50% electricity claim. Water falls further: 15-30 L/kg on site to 3-6 L/kg at a modern plant (80-90%). TRSA plant factors (1.57 kWh/kg, 0.41 kg CO2/kg, US fuel mix) describe a mixed healthcare/F&B/industrial product mix (lodging 1.2% of pounds). Linen mass is 4.5-6.8 kg per occupied room-night (trade), 5.12 kg HCMI default, 8-55 lb observed range; luxury with robes sits at the top. Dolder Grand external laundry = 234-260 tCO2e/yr, 6-7% of its whole-hotel footprint, is the only full-boundary outsourced-laundry line in the notes. Scope shift: On-site Scope 1 gas + Scope 2 electricity move to Scope 3 purchased services (HCMI and Dolder count outsourced laundry; the Best Western Berlin ruling struck a neutrality claim for omitting it).

laundry_ozone_and_water_recycling - Water effects are measured (65% less hot water at Charleston Place; 80% less water at Grand Hyatt Seattle) but no ENERGY fraction for the whole laundry slice is stated: washers are only part of laundry energy (dryers and ironers dominate in high-capacity plants per Bobak 2010), so the hot-water saving cannot be converted to a laundry-energy fraction without an unmeasured split. Effect left null on purpose.

Guest behaviour and service design

id name targets effect type low / mid / high ramp (yr) duration (yr) payback first source confidence
towel_reuse_norm_cards Towel-reuse programme with descriptive-norm cards laundry fraction_reduction 0.056 / 0.09 / 0.121 1 - - Goldstein, Cialdini & Griskevicius 2008, Journal of Consumer Research 35(3):472-482 (percentages as reporte... medium
housekeeping_opt_out Opt-out / opt-in daily housekeeping and linen change laundry, dhw fraction_reduction 0.18 / 0.18 / 0.18 1 - - Boston Globe 2018 (Sheraton Boston 'Make a Green Choice' sign: each opted-out night saves 37.2 gal water, 0... medium
electrify_guest_transfers Electrify chauffeur / airport-transfer fleet (PHEV or BEV) transfers fraction_reduction 0.62 / 0.88 / 1 1 - - UK Government GHG Conversion Factors 2024, Cars by market segment (Luxury: petrol 0.31949, diesel 0.20695, ... medium

towel_reuse_norm_cards - Values are the INCREASE IN TOWEL-REUSE RATE in percentage points, to be applied to the towel sub-share of laundry mass (towel share not in the notes; a luxury bath towel is 570-640 g, hand towel 115-170 g from Standard Textile weights). Exp 1: 35.1% to 44.1% (+9.0 pts); Exp 2: 37.2% to 49.3% same-room norm (+12.1 pts), other normative messages combined 42.8% (+5.6 pts). Effect is culturally bounded: German hotels sit at 64-93% reuse regardless of message. Percentages come from the replication, not the JCR abstract. Relative to a 35% baseline the top result is a 23% increase in reuse (49.3/37.2 - 1 = 0.33 on Exp 2 baseline), which is the per-towel laundry avoided only if every reused towel displaces a wash.

housekeeping_opt_out - The only HOTEL-WIDE measured effect is Hyatt Centric Waikiki's 18% cut in linen output, so low = mid = high = 0.18 of laundry mass; no range exists. Per OPTED-OUT night the Sheraton Boston sign gives 0.19 kWh electricity + 25,000 BTU (~7.3 kWh) gas + 141 L water, which matches the notes' 6-10 kWh laundry-plus-housekeeping estimate per room-night, i.e. an opted-out night removes most of that night's laundry energy. Uptake is the missing variable: Marriott did not disclose opt-out frequency (six Boston hotels logged ~89,000 Green Choice room-nights in 2016); Hilton moved non-luxury US properties to opt-in in 2021 with no published savings. Luxury brands were excluded from Hilton's change, so apply with caution to the Waldorf. Capex: Incentive cost: USD 5 F&B voucher or 250-500 loyalty points per opted-out night (Sheraton Boston).

electrify_guest_transfers - Fraction of TRANSFER emissions against a luxury PETROL baseline (0.319 kgCO2e/km): low = petrol to PHEV (1 - 0.121/0.319 = 0.62), mid = petrol to BEV on UK grid incl. electricity (1 - 0.039/0.319 = 0.88), high = tailpipe-only BEV (1.0). Against a diesel baseline (0.207) the PHEV fraction is 0.42. Factors are DEFRA 2024 (UK) with no US luxury-SUV equivalent found; NYC electricity (0.289-0.392 kg/kWh) is dirtier than the UK grid, so the BEV mid would be lower in New York. A 40 km one-way transfer is 12.8 kg CO2e in a petrol luxury car (notes' arithmetic). No hotel group reports guest ground transport in Scope 3; a hotel-owned fleet is Scope 1, a contracted limousine Scope 3 category 1 if the hotel pays. Scope shift: Owned fleet: Scope 1 fuel to Scope 2 electricity; contracted: Scope 3 category 1 (purchased services) or outside the inventory if the guest pays.

Food and beverage

menu_carbon_labels - Fraction of PURCHASED-FOOD emissions. low = 0 (null results in large workplace trials), mid = Brunner's -3.6%, high = top of the canteen-trial range (5.0%). All trials are canteens or restaurants, none a hotel; effects concentrate in early exposure and may fade or, per a Swedish natural experiment, grow with repeated exposure. Legally the safest F&B claim form under EU 2024/825 and the BGH ruling (factual disclosure, no 'neutral').

low_carbon_menu_design - Fraction of purchased-food emissions per plate, measured at Cool Food HOSPITAL members (not hotels): 16% (US hospitals), 21% (global), 31% (early adopters). The Cool Food Pledge target (-25% absolute / -38% per calorie by 2030) is the policy anchor. Mechanism is substitution of ruminant protein: beef-herd beef 99.5 (dairy-herd 33.3) vs poultry 9.9 vs vegetables ~0.5 kg CO2e/kg (Poore & Nemecek via OWID), or 28.7 vs 4.1 vs 0.5 on Clune et al. medians; the factor set changes the attainable fraction by up to 3.5x on beef and must be named. Luxury covers (Dolder 5.4-6.1 kg CO2e per restaurant guest; Accor 1.66-6.57 kg per meal) exceed the Cool Food dinner threshold with a beef main, so the headroom is large; uptake evidence at hotels is weak (SHA: few orders of labelled low-carbon dishes).

food_waste_measurement_programme - Fraction of FOOD WASTE MASS (pre-consumer in Champions 12.3; plate waste excluded there, included in Hilton Ramadan). low = WWF pilot overall (~10%), mid = Champions 12.3 mean (21%), high = WWF best site (38%); other programmes: Marriott UK 25% in six months, Mandarin Oriental 36%, Scandic -31% vs 2019. Effect on PURCHASED FOOD is smaller: SHA says >20% of food bought is wasted and waste is ~5% of COGS, so a 21% waste cut is ~4% of food purchases (0.21 x 0.20, simple arithmetic) at most. Disposal emissions scale with the mass cut; embodied emissions of avoided waste must be taken from the food slice, not double counted (notes' decision). Measured baseline 0.36-0.53 kg per cover (EPA Ireland daily 360 g, functions 530 g; Malaysia 0.53 kg). Capex: Nearly 90% of sites invested < USD 20,000; cost of reduction = 0.9% of annual food sales (Champions 12.3).

organics_diversion_composting - Replaces the food-waste DISPOSAL factor: baseline landfill 0.587-0.656 kg CO2e/kg (DEFRA, ~0.627 for the organic food line) becomes 0.0089-0.0090 kg CO2e/kg for composting (DEFRA 2024). No DEFRA factor for anaerobic digestion is in the notes; WARM gives compost-vs-landfill net 0.61 tCO2e per short ton (0.67 kg/kg), consistent with the DEFRA difference. In New York this is a REGULATORY BASELINE, not an optional lever: LL146 requires source-separated organics for food service in hotels with 100+ rooms, with Int. 31 timing compliance to the Commercial Waste Zone rollout (Phase 5 of 10). Disposal-only accounting is the notes' decision; the 1.9 or 4.35 kg/kg cradle-to-grave factors include embodied food emissions already in the food slice. DEFRA values are from a Climatiq mirror and should be confirmed against the gov.uk workbook for the year used.

plate_waste_nudges - Fraction of PLATE WASTE only, which is ~40% of hotel food waste (Winnow via SHA) or 23-35% (Papargyropoulou 2019); scale down accordingly for the whole foodwaste slice. low = JRC signage (14.4%), mid = plate-diameter effect (20%), high = family stamp (34%); side-plate removal (27%) sits inside the range. Field studies, not RCTs, mostly European buffets.

buffet_to_plated_service - Fraction of PLATE WASTE per cover: low = 1 - 0.21/0.48 = 0.56, high = 1 - 0.16/0.48 = 0.67 (Malta measurements), mid = midpoint; EPA Ireland's 300 vs 130 g (0.57) is consistent but unsourced. Buffet leftovers (22-50% of total waste in Malaysian operations) also fall but are not separately measured here. Breakfast-inclusive rates convert a purchase into a buffet entitlement; no controlled comparison of breakfast-included vs room-only exists (notes gap).

kitchen_high_efficiency_equipment - Fraction of KITCHEN energy per meal: CEC demonstration range 20-40% (mid = midpoint 30%); Rational's -34% is cooking-only and manufacturer data. None is a hotel kitchen. Baseline: Mudie et al. 2016 1.52-3.32 kWh electricity per meal (UK pubs, refrigeration 41% of kitchen electricity), Portuguese audit 1.28 kWh elec + 1.67 kWh gas per meal; notes infer 3-5 kWh delivered per hotel cover. Kitchens are 15-25% of hotel energy per SHA (interview-based).

kitchen_demand_controlled_ventilation - Fraction of KITCHEN VENTILATION energy only ('>50%' at two demonstration sites; no range given, so low = mid = high = 0.50 as a floor). Kitchen HVAC is 28% of restaurant energy in the ENERGY STAR split, so the whole-kitchen effect is at most ~0.14 (0.5 x 0.28, simple arithmetic) if the ventilation share matches.

kitchen_hot_holding_elimination - Fraction of KITCHEN ELECTRICITY: the one kitchen-wide measured figure is hot-holding elimination at 16% (bain-maries + heat lamps, 14 UK pub kitchens, Nov 2012). The 45% freezer and 70% behavioural figures are single-appliance or potential, not kitchen-wide, and are not used. Excludes gas, dishwashing, extraction and lighting.

minibar_compressor_swap - Fraction of MINIBAR energy: low = SuitePad 60 vs 150 kWh/yr (0.60), mid = Tefcold 'nearly 1/3' (0.67), high = catalogue extremes 55 vs 292 kWh/yr (0.81). All manufacturer/vendor ratings (EN 62552-3), no independent test; Bansal & Martin 2000 confirms vapour-compression uses least energy. Absolute scale is small: 0.53-0.70 kWh per room-night = 0.1-0.3 kg CO2e on typical grids (notes' arithmetic); 236 kWh/yr x 300 rooms = 71 MWh/yr.

minibar_removal - Removes the whole minibar slice by definition (0.53-0.70 kWh per room-night for absorption units). Revenue at risk ~1% or less of hotel revenue with ~70% non-use; no study quantifies product waste or restocking transport. For a luxury brand the constraint is service expectation, not carbon.

renewable_ppa_or_green_contract - Share of ELECTRICITY covered by a contract whose attributes are retired to the hotel: low = Hilton's two solar PPAs at 70% of demand, high = 100% contracts (Hampton Inn LAX, UK managed hotels 2021), mid = midpoint. Applies to the market-based Scope 2 factor only; location-based and LL84/CHSB figures are unchanged. Counts toward Hilton's SBTi target only if SBTi V2 conditions hold from 2028 (deliverability region, plant age <=15 yr, hourly matching); unbundled national RECs (Hyatt's Green-e) are the instrument most at risk. Does NOT count for LL97 unless the resource is Zone J Tier 4 (see tier4_recs_zone_j). At portfolio scale the lever is small today: Hilton's market-vs-location Scope 2 gap is 118,530 tCO2e (~6%) in 2025; Marriott 2.92% renewables. Scope shift: Market-based Scope 2 only; no change to Scope 1 gas, location-based Scope 2, LL84 Portfolio Manager or CHSB figures.

onsite_solar_pv - No hotel in the notes publishes PV kWh or share of load; the two Hilton-brand precedents are low-rise with parking canopies. A 47-storey Manhattan landmark with a Level-20 cooling-tower well and landmarked roofline has little PV area, so the share is left null rather than guessed. LL97 counts on-site solar directly (31 solar deductions in year one).

coned_steam_instead_of_onsite_gas - Fraction of heating/DHW emissions per kBtu DELIVERED when steam replaces gas: low = 1 - 0.04493/0.05311 = 0.154 (LL97 coefficients, 2024-29; ignores that gas kBtu is fuel input and steam kBtu is delivered heat, so boiler efficiency - not in the notes for the Waldorf's condensing Viessmann units - would shrink this), high = Con Edison's own ~25% claim for cogenerated steam vs on-site boilers, mid = midpoint. The Waldorf went the other way (steam to eight gas condensing boilers + 555 kW CHP, inferred from permits and a zero steam line in CY2024 LL84; unannounced), so this record is the counterfactual. Steam gets only 3.9% coefficient relief in 2030 (0.04493 to 0.0432) versus ~50% for electricity; see coned_steam_decarbonisation for the utility's 2040/2050 pathway. Scope shift: Scope 1 gas to Scope 2 purchased steam (GHG Protocol); both count under LL97.

coned_steam_decarbonisation - Fraction of STEAM emissions removed over time, as a step series: 0.039 in 2030-34 (prescribed), 0.36 in 2035-39 (Con Edison's requested coefficient cut, not yet adopted by DOB), 1.0 by 2050 (utility target, offsets allowed, so not necessarily physical). ramp_years = 24 spans 2026-2050; the shape between steps is an assumption. Irrelevant to the Waldorf unless it reconnects to steam; relevant to the pre-war Manhattan luxury peer set (St. Regis, Peninsula, Carlyle, Pierre, Aman all report steam).

gas_chp_555kw - No measured or modelled emission effect for a hotel CHP exists in the notes; effect left null. Direction of effect depends on the electricity factor: CHP displaces grid electricity at 0.289 kg/kWh (LL97 2024-29) or 0.392 (eGRID NYCW) with on-site gas at 0.181 kg/kWh-fuel, and the LL97 electricity coefficient halves to 0.145 in 2030 while gas does not move, so any 2024-29 benefit erodes after 2030 (qualitative; CHP electrical efficiency is not in the notes). Commissioning status unknown (LL84 CY2024 'electricity_use_generated' Not Available). Adds Scope 1 gas, cuts Scope 2 electricity; LL97 recognises CHP via a credit. Capex: USD 2.6M CHP room and ventilation (DOB NOW M00342203) plus USD 0.8M boilers and USD 1.1M flues/piping (permit estimates, not total MEP cost). Scope shift: Scope 2 grid electricity to Scope 1 natural gas; LL97 CHP credit.

grid_decarbonisation_ll97_coefficient - Prescribed COMPLIANCE factor, not physical grid intensity: 1 - 0.145/0.289 = 0.498 reduction in the electricity factor, as a single step in 2030 (ramp_years 0, duration 5 covers 2030-34; later periods not yet set). The notes' decision is to use the LL97 coefficient as the primary electricity factor with eGRID NYCW 0.392 as the location-based sensitivity. Gas (0.05311) is unchanged and steam falls only 3.9%, so the step favours electrified buildings. The marginal grid coefficient for beneficial-electrification credits follows its own path: 0.247 (2024) to 0.114 (2029) tCO2e/MWh, a 54% fall.

grid_decarbonisation_clcpa_physical - Applies to the LOCATION-BASED factor (eGRID NYCW 0.392 kg/kWh in 2023). The notes contain the policy endpoints but NO projected factor path: low = 0.70 (if the 2030 renewable share were the terminal state), mid = high = 1.0 at 2040 (zero-emission electricity). ramp_years 14 (2026-2040) with a LINEAR SHAPE IS AN ASSUMPTION, as is treating the 70% renewable share as a 70% factor cut (renewable share and emission factor are not the same quantity). CHPE's physical effect first appears in eGRID 2026 data (published ~2028) and does not change the LL97 coefficient unless DOB revises the rule. Downstate generation was ~10% zero-emission in 2024, so the local trajectory starts far below the state average.

tier4_recs_zone_j - Share of utility ELECTRICITY emissions deducted under LL97 via retired Zone J RECs: 0 before CHPE RECs exist (2026), up to 1.0 (currently uncapped in DOB rules); mid 0.5 is a scenario anchored on Urban Green's 'as much as half' citywide estimate, not a hotel figure. If Intro 159 passes the high collapses to 0.10. Electricity only: no effect on the Waldorf's gas boilers or CHP. Economics: at USD 77/MWh a REC equals the USD 268/t penalty in 2024-29; after 2030 the break-even is ~USD 39/MWh and DOB expects REC cost to exceed the penalty. Also the one instrument that satisfies SBTi V2 deliverability and the GHG Protocol direction simultaneously (same deliverability region; CHPE commissioned 2026 so under 15 years). Capex: REC price: break-even USD 77/MWh (2024-29) and ~USD 39/MWh (2030-34) against the USD 268/t penalty (DOB). Scope shift: LL97 compliance basis and market-based Scope 2; no change to location-based or LL84 figures.

ll97_cap_2024_2029 - Baseline cap: 0.00987 tCO2e/ft2 x 10.7639 ft2/m2 = 106.2 kgCO2e/m2 of gross floor area, multiplier 1.0 for 2024-2029 (duration 6). For the Waldorf's 1,666,763 ft2 'Hotel' filing the cap is ~16,450 tCO2e/yr; pre-closure emissions were 15,435-17,916 t (Portfolio Manager basis) and the CY2024 luxury peer median of ~11.3 kg/ft2 implies ~18,800 t, so even the first cap binds a peer-typical building. Penalty for overshoot = (actual - limit) tCO2e x USD 268 per year (s28-320.6); non-filing USD 0.50/ft2 per month. Emissions are computed with LL97 coefficients (electricity 0.289 kg/kWh), which are ~36% below Portfolio Manager's eGRID basis for electricity. Year one: 604 hotel filers, 91% met the limit (lowest of the major types). Capex: Penalty USD 268 per tCO2e over the cap per year.

ll97_cap_2030_2034 - THE TWO SERIES CONFLICT. The statute gives R-1 hotels 0.00526 tCO2e/ft2 (56.6 kgCO2e/m2, multiplier 0.533 vs 2024-29); the December 2023 rule assigns the ESPM 'Hotel' type 0.003850668 tCO2e/ft2 (41.4 kg/m2, multiplier 0.390) and makes ESPM reporting mandatory from 2026, so low = mid = 0.390 (rule) and high = 0.533 (statute). Which governs a given hotel after 2030 is flagged for legal review in the notes. For the Waldorf filing: ~6,420 tCO2e/yr (ESPM) or ~8,770 (statutory); at the ~18,800 t peer-median illustration the ESPM shortfall of ~12,400 t costs ~USD 3.3M/yr at USD 268/t (notes' bounding arithmetic, not a forecast). The 61% cap drop exceeds the ~50% electricity-coefficient drop, so electrification alone does not guarantee compliance; gas gets no relief. Penalty for overshoot = (actual - limit) tCO2e x USD 268 per year (s28-320.6); non-filing USD 0.50/ft2 per month. Capex: Penalty USD 268 per tCO2e over the cap per year; REC break-even ~USD 39/MWh in this period.

ll97_cap_2035_2039 - 0.002640017 x 10.7639 = 28.4 kgCO2e/m2; multiplier 0.268 vs the 2024-29 cap. ~4,400 tCO2e/yr for the Waldorf filing. Only the ESPM series extends this far; no statutory R-1 figure exists for 2035+. Utility coefficients for 2035-39 are not yet set (Con Edison seeks a ~36% steam cut). Penalty for overshoot = (actual - limit) tCO2e x USD 268 per year (s28-320.6); non-filing USD 0.50/ft2 per month. Capex: Penalty USD 268 per tCO2e over the cap per year.

ll97_cap_2040_2049 - 0.001465772 x 10.7639 = 15.8 kgCO2e/m2; multiplier 0.149 vs 2024-29. Coincides with CLCPA's 100% zero-emission electricity target (2040), so an all-electric building's compliance would then rest on the electricity coefficient DOB adopts for that period (not yet set). Penalty for overshoot = (actual - limit) tCO2e x USD 268 per year (s28-320.6); non-filing USD 0.50/ft2 per month. Capex: Penalty USD 268 per tCO2e over the cap per year.

ll97_ahrf_offsets - Effective cap multiplier 1.10 at most (offsets equal to 10% of the LIMIT, not of emissions), purchased at the same USD 268/t as the penalty, so AHRF offsets change compliance status but not cost. Only AHRF offsets are eligible; voluntary carbon credits have no LL97 role. Stated for 2024-29; 2030+ treatment not extracted. Capex: USD 268 per tCO2e (same as the penalty).

eu_2024_825_claims_ban - ZERO emissions effect by construction: this is a MARKETING constraint. From 27 Sep 2026 a 'carbon-neutral stay' or 'carbon-neutral meeting' sold to EU consumers on purchased credits is a per se unfair practice; German courts already apply it (Best Western Berlin, Eurowings, BGH); UK CMA fines up to 10% of global turnover since 6 Apr 2025; US FTC Green Guides unrevised since 2012. Hilton's ClimeCo 'Carbon Neutral Meetings' product and Hyatt's offset-backed meetings are the exposed products. In the simulator this event should flip the permitted-claims flag (disclosure-only plus 'contribution' wording), not any slice. EUR-Lex text was not fetched; Annex wording rests on legal summaries.

sbti_v2_scope2_rules - ZERO direct emissions effect; changes WHICH market instruments count against Hilton's validated absolute target (-46.2% Scope 1+2 by 2030 vs 2019). From 2028 unbundled national RECs and old-plant contracts stop counting; Zone J CHPE RECs and new PPAs in the NYISO deliverability region still do. In the simulator: gate renewable_ppa_or_green_contract by region/age/hourly-match from 2028. Carbon credits count at no point under V1.3.1 or V2.0.

Shocks (scenarios)
