pandemic_occupancy_shock Pandemic-style occupancy collapse (scenario) (whole model) occupancy_multiplier 0.5 / 0.7 / 0.9 0 2 - Cornell CHSB 2023, Exhibit 7 (2019 to 2021: energy per occupied room luxury +24.26%, upper upscale +10.13%;... low
renovation_closure Full closure for renovation (Waldorf 1 Mar 2017 to 15 Jul 2025) (whole model) occupancy_multiplier 0 / 0 / 0 0 8 - LL84 CY2018 (closed shell: district steam 56.4 GBtu, gas 0, electricity 5.5 GWh, site EUI 44.8, 5,340 tCO2e... high

pandemic_occupancy_shock - The multipliers (0.5/0.7/0.9 on occupied room-nights for duration_years 2) are a SCENARIO; no 2020-21 occupancy figure for Manhattan luxury is in the notes. What IS measured is the consequence: luxury energy per occupied room rose 24% and NYC luxury carbon per occupied room rose from ~28 to 40.5 kgCO2e (2019 to 2021) while energy per m2 fell 19%, i.e. base-building load does not scale down with occupancy. The simulator should hold heat/cool/vent/light/bother/pool/sauna/gym/spa/elev near-fixed and scale dhw, laundry, kitchen, food, foodwaste, minibar, transfers with occupancy when applying this shock; the CHSB ratios are the test of that split.

renovation_closure - Occupied room-nights = 0 for duration_years 8 (8.4 years at the Waldorf). The building still emitted: the closed-shell CY2018 filing shows site EUI 44.8 vs 125-141 operating, i.e. 32-36% of operating energy (steam 54%, electricity 21% of pre-closure), and 5,340 tCO2e vs 15,435-17,916; the construction-year CY2024 filing shows 4,723 tCO2e. LL97 caps apply to the building regardless of occupancy (the cap is per ft2), so a closed landmark can still be compliant or not; per-room-night metrics are undefined (CY2018 ENERGY STAR 100 while closed shows why). Reopening is phased (rooms, then ballroom, then spa) so the first operating year (CY2026, disclosed 2027) is itself a ramp.

Counts

By category: building 7, wellness 5, laundry 2, guest_behaviour 3, fnb 11, energy_supply 7, policy 8, shock 2.
By confidence: high 12, medium 22, low 11.

What the evidence does not quantify

Levers that appear in the notes as recommendations, vendor claims or industry practice but carry no measured effect size usable by a simulator. Each is listed with the closest thing the notes contain.

  • Key-card master switches (all-off on exit). Vendor case studies claim ~45% lighting and ~50% HVAC (Aqua Aloha, uncontrolled) and 33% (Verde/Wyndham, 2 vs 2 rooms); no controlled trial. The controlled evidence is only for occupancy-based HVAC set-back (DOE/PNNL 2012).
  • Guest-facing real-time energy feedback in rooms. No study found (04 KQ7 gap).
  • Pre-arrival room conditioning and PMS-integrated set-back of unrented rooms. DOE/PNNL 2012 explicitly did not evaluate PMS integration; housekeeping reset set-points at random in unrented rooms.
  • Public-area HVAC on 100% outside air, corridor and lobby 24-h lighting. PNNL metered 2,804 kWh/day public-space resistance heat and 959 kWh/day public lights but no lever; no luxury corridor/lobby lighting per room exists.
  • Low-flow fixtures, deep-tub and rain-shower specification. Physics is fixed (0.048 kWh/L heated); Styles et al. 2015 best practice saves >=228 L/guest-night and 209,541 kWh/yr for a 100-room model hotel (abstract only, no per-night energy); no luxury fixture volumes or hot-water share exist.
  • Shower and DHW waste-heat recovery. Sport England lists it; Liebersbach 2021 (34-67%) covers pool preheating/DHW in a recreation centre only.
  • Pool-hall humidity/CO2-modulated ventilation, reduced air-change rates. Sport England 4-10 ACH design; Nitter et al. found water parks at 2.65 vs 4.81 ACH conventional; no saving measured. Kampel's 28% bundles all measures.
  • Whirlpool / hydrotherapy pool covers and dilution control. Only residential hot-tub data (2,514 kWh/yr NSPI; Selectra covered vs uncovered 378 vs 567 kWh/30 d); Nova Scotia caps spa dilution at 20% of volume; no commercial measurement.
  • Spa treatment-room equipment (hot-towel cabinets, stone heaters), linen per treatment, robe mass. Wattages and kg per treatment not retrieved; robe ~1.2-1.5 kg is unsourced in the notes.
  • Gym lighting and HVAC upgrade. Wynne & McLean 2017 identified 122,976 kWh/yr from lighting at an Irish fitness/leisure centre but the baseline is in an unretrieved PDF, so no fraction.
  • Linen per occupied room reduction (duvet/sheet change frequency) beyond opt-out. Only Hyatt Centric -18% linen output; TRSA Lodging benchmark (44 properties) is members-only.
  • Toiletry dispensers vs miniatures. No independent LCA; claims are plastic mass only (one pump bottle = 10-12 minis; '95% less plastic'; Groupe GM '600 tCO2e' internal).
  • Bottled water removal. Gleick & Cooley 2009 give 5.6-10.2 MJ/L (1.6-2.8 kWh/L primary energy; two 500 mL bottles = 1.6-2.8 kWh per night) but there is no slice for it and no hotel measurement of bottles per night.
  • In-room coffee machines and capsules; printed collateral; slippers; welcome gifts. Not researched; nothing recorded.
  • Room service, butler and business-centre footprints. No per-order or per-hour figure; only elevator per-trip energy (0.04-0.27 kWh) as a component.
  • Breakfast-included vs room-only rates. No controlled comparison; the Slovenian breakfast study (14.3 g plate waste/person) was at a hotel with breakfast included.
  • Banquet default low-carbon menus and attendee-count forecasting. Hyatt Regency Orlando cut production 25% from first-day order analysis (single property); Dolder 7.8-19.7 kgCO2e per event guest is a footprint, not a lever.
  • Beverage programme (keg vs bottle, lighter glass, local wine). Per-container factors exist (beer 510-842 g/L by packaging; wine 0.9-2.0 kg/bottle; Scotch 2.83 kg/70 cl) but no hotel bar intervention is measured; cocktail figures are a supplier's marketing study.
  • Anaerobic digestion of food waste. No DEFRA AD factor in the notes; only landfill (627 kg/t) and composting (9 kg/t).
  • Other solid waste (slice waste). CHSB 2025 luxury waste 5.49 kg per occupied room vs midscale 0.78 and Pandox 1.2 kg per guest-night are baselines; no diversion lever or factor is quantified in the notes.
  • Elevator destination dispatch, hoistway lighting, car HVAC. ISO 25745-2 excludes hoistway lighting and car HVAC; no hotel measurement.
  • Valet parking and garage energy. Not found anywhere (04 KQ6 gap).
  • Staff commuting reduction (shift design, transit benefits). Dolder 12-15% and Marriott 594,040 tCO2e are footprints; no lever measured; staff-to-room ratios for luxury not found.
  • Refrigerant management. HCMI applies a flat 1% uplift; Accor reports refrigerant leaks at 1.8% of group emissions; no leak-rate reduction measured.
  • Chiller plant efficiency, heat-recovery chillers, thermal storage. Waldorf chiller type/capacity not in any filing; Con Edison's pathway lists thermal storage at system scale only.
  • Durable carbon removals bought as a 'contribution'. Price is quantified (~USD 180/t average; DAC ~USD 650/t median; biochar USD 50-150/t; 1-2% of a USD 1,500 rate for a 50-150 kg night) but it is not an emissions lever and must not enter any slice; SBTi V2 OER and EU 2024/825 govern how it may be described.
  • Low-carbon room rate / amenity carbon labels (spa, pool, minibar). No field evidence at any hotel (05 KQ7 gap); the closest analogue is towel-reuse norm messaging.
  • Opening ramp-up year occupancy. Luxury-class monthly occupancy ran 65.4% (Jan) to 82.5% (Jun) in H1 2025 but no Waldorf or new-opening occupancy curve exists; the first operating year (CY2026) is the validation point.
  • Hilton LightStay property-level reductions. Portfolio claims only (34% intensity cut 2008-2018; 'USD 1 billion saved'); no per-property or per-measure figure.

Reading the ranking

No single per-room-night baseline exists in the notes for a NYC luxury hotel split by slice, so the library deliberately does not rank levers by absolute kg CO2e; the simulator does that once it has a baseline. What the notes do support, from stated figures only: the CHSB NYC luxury energy-only median is 28.4 kgCO2e per occupied room-night (IQR 24-38); full-scope inventories put purchased food at 40-58% of a room-night (Attari & Hanrahan 2026) or 48-56% of a whole hotel (Dolder Grand), so a 21% food-emission cut (Cool Food members' global mean) is of the order of 10% of a full-scope night, larger than any single building lever applied to the guestroom; guestroom HVAC at 3-13 kWh per room-day times 18.4% is 0.9-2.6 kWh per occupied room-night (notes' arithmetic); laundry at 6-10 kWh per room-night times 0.375-0.6 is 2-6 kWh; DHW at ~7.5 kWh thermal per room-day (PNNL) electrified at COP 3 removes roughly two thirds of its fuel energy; the LL97 electricity coefficient step halves the compliance factor on every electric slice in 2030; and a renovation closure zeroes room-nights while leaving a third of building energy in place.

Cross-facet insights and hypotheses

Theory note 04. Written from the eight research notes (01-08), the review report, and the graph's nine decisions, eleven outcomes and three revisits. Every number below is taken from those notes as they state it; where I do arithmetic on cited numbers it is labelled as such and the inputs are named. Where a number used by the companion apps is not in the notes, I say so rather than supply one.

Two conventions. "Studio" is the static what-if app (knobs for property, CBECS end uses, amenities, food, laundry, waste, levers; results per room-night, per guest-night, per m2, sliced by amenity, end use, scope and carrier; CHSB and LL84 benchmarks; LL97). "Storyline" is the dated-intervention simulator (interventions and policy/grid paths 2025-2040, Monte Carlo over effect ranges, story comparison, LL97 penalty exposure). Hypotheses are phrased as runs on one or both.

Coefficients used repeatedly, all from 1 RCNY 103-14 as quoted in note 06: electricity 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh (0.289 kg/kWh, 2024-29) and 0.000145 (0.145 kg/kWh, 2030-34); natural gas 0.00005311 tCO2e/kBtu, which is 0.181 kg per kWh of fuel (times 3.412 kBtu/kWh); district steam 0.00004493 then 0.0000432 tCO2e/kBtu (0.153 then 0.147 kg per kWh thermal); eGRID2023 NYCW 864.5 lb CO2/MWh = 0.392 kg/kWh. Hotel caps 0.00987 tCO2e/ft2 (2024-29), 0.00385 ESPM (2030-34), 0.00264 (2035-39), 0.00147 (2040-49); penalty $268/t.

Part 1. Insights

I1. The F&B share of a hotel footprint is a property of the grid, not the hotel: 48-56% in Zurich, 19% at Accor group level, and it will rise mechanically in New York when the LL97 coefficient halves in 2030

Facets: 3 (F&B), 6 (NYC grid), 1 (baseline).

Evidence. The Dolder Grand (Zurich, full Scope 3, myclimate/ecoinvent) reports food and beverages at 1,713.9 of 3,541.9 tCO2e (48%) in 2023, 52% in 2024 and 2,257.7 of 4,047.5 t (56%) in 2025, with purchased electricity 3-4% and heating/cooling 11-12% (note 03 KQ1). Accor's 2024 URD puts energy at 65% and catering and drinks at 19%, and the same source says that in a French hotel on low-carbon electricity "energy weighs less and catering more" (note 03 KQ1). The Irish four-hotel preprint gives food procurement 39.7-57.7% on a grid at 0.226 kg/kWh (note 07 KQ2). New York's factors are 0.392 (eGRID), 0.289 (LL97 2024-29) and 0.145 (LL97 2030-34) (note 06 Q4-Q5).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. "Food is half a luxury hotel's footprint" reads as a fact about hotels. It is the ratio of a constant (embodied food, which does not depend on where the hotel is) to a variable (building energy carbon, which does). The same kitchen serving the same menu in Zurich and Manhattan produces the same food emissions but a different share.

Implication for the Waldorf. The F&B share is a time-varying output of the model. Under the 2024-29 LL97 factor the share will sit below Zurich's; when the coefficient falls to 0.145 in 2030 the energy denominator roughly halves on the electric part and the share rises with no change in the kitchen. Every F&B share the paper prints must carry the electricity factor beside it (the report already recommends this); the Storyline should show the share drifting upward along the grid path even in a no-intervention story.

I2. Boundary moves the luxury room-night more than segment does: full-scope is 1.7-2.4x energy-only, while luxury is 2.3x upper-midscale

Facets: 1 (baseline and HCMI), 3 (F&B), 7 (verified papers).

Evidence. CHSB CY2023 US luxury non-resort is 32.12 kgCO2e per occupied room (HCMI) against upper-midscale 14.03, a ratio of 2.3 (note 01 KQ1). Attari & Hanrahan 2026 give Scope 1+2-only room-nights of 9.40 / 16.59 / 25.10 / 32.32 and full-scope 15.87 / 40.30 / 47.15 / 56.14, ratios of 1.69, 2.43, 1.88 and 1.74 (note 07 KQ2). HCMI excludes purchased food by design and allocates spa, F&B and pool by floor area under an "equal intensity for all areas" assumption (note 01 KQ2).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. The industry's benchmarks make segment look like the big axis and boundary look like a footnote. In the only inventories that count food, the boundary multiplier is of the same size as, or larger than, the entire luxury-versus-midscale gap. A midscale hotel on a full boundary (14.03 x 1.7-2.4 = 24-34 kg, my arithmetic) overlaps the HCMI luxury IQR of 24-38.

Implication for the Waldorf. Any comparison of the Waldorf's full-scope night with the CHSB NYC luxury IQR (23.95-38.19) is a comparison across boundaries and is meaningless unless the HCMI-comparable column is printed beside it, which is what the dual-reporting decision (graph decision "Recommend dual reporting") requires. The Studio's boundary toggle is therefore the single most consequential control in the app.

I3. The denominator reverses the verdict on luxury: 2.55x upper-midscale per room-night, 1.43x per m2, so ~60% of the premium is floor area per key, and the Waldorf is at roughly 2,700 ft2 per key

Facets: 1 (CHSB), 4 (guestroom), 6 (Waldorf property).

Evidence. CHSB CY2023 Measure 3 US luxury 32.19 vs upper-midscale 12.62 kgCO2e per occupied room (2.55x); Measure 4 luxury 83.83 vs 58.77 kgCO2e/m2 (1.43x); kWh per occupied room 119.35 vs 42.20 (2.83x); kWh/m2 300.70 vs 197.73 (1.52x) (note 01 KQ4). HES 2011 found no statistically significant EUI difference by star rating per m2 (note 04 KQ8). CHSB finds luxury uses more water per occupied room but not per m2 (note 04 KQ8). Xuchao et al. 2010: "a hotel's carbon intensity ranking is rather sensitive to the normalizing denominator chosen" (note 07 KQ1). Waldorf: 1,666,763 ft2 benchmarked as one "Hotel" with 375 keys and 372 residences; if 60% is hotel that is about 2,700 ft2 per key, against about 1,800 at the St. Regis (427,877 ft2 / 238 keys, key count unverified) (note 06 Q1; report).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. "Luxury hotels are 2.5 times as carbon-intensive" is true per room-night and roughly false per square metre. The two regimes that bind the Waldorf, LL97 and Hilton's intensity goal, are per-area; the two that guests and benchmarks see, HCMI and the paper's own functional unit, are per room-night. The same building is average on one axis and worst-in-class on the other.

Implication for the Waldorf. At the Manhattan luxury peer median of 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 and 76.4% occupancy, 1,000 ft2 per key gives 40.5 kgCO2e per occupied room-night and 2,700 ft2 per key gives 109 (my arithmetic: 11.3 x ft2 per key x 375 / (375 x 365 x 0.764)). Both exceed the CHSB NYC luxury upper quartile of 38.19. The Waldorf will look above-IQR per room-night at any plausible area per key, and near peer-median per m2, and both statements will be correct.

I4. The hotel/residence single-lot filing makes the LL97 cap and the hotel-only GFA two unknowns with one equation, and the Studio's logged defaults imply a hotel area 2.7 times smaller than the report's bounding case

Facets: 6 (LL84/LL97), 1 (allocation), Studio outcome node.

Evidence. The Waldorf's LL84 record is lot 7501, a single 1,666,763 ft2 "Hotel" property that includes the 372 condominiums; hotel-only GFA is not public (note 06 Q1, Q3). The LL97 cap is computed on that GFA: about 16,450 tCO2e (2024-29), 6,420 (2030-34 ESPM), 4,400 (2035-39) (note 06 Q4). The report's bounding case assumes 60% hotel, about 1.0 million ft2. The Studio v1 outcome node logs defaults of 36.4 kgCO2e per occupied room-night, 22.8 per guest-night, 127 kg/m2/yr and 4,296 t/yr. Those four numbers imply (my arithmetic, no access to the Studio source) 4,296,000 / 127 = 33,800 m2 (about 364,000 ft2, about 970 ft2 per key), 4,296,000 / 36.4 = 118,000 occupied room-nights (86% occupancy on 375 keys), and 1.60 guests per room.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. The Studio's 36.4 kg looks like it sits comfortably inside the CHSB IQR of 24-38. It sits there because of an implied hotel area a third of the report's and an occupancy ten points above PwC's luxury-class 76.4%. Neither assumption is wrong on the evidence, because there is no evidence; but the two companion documents are using hotel areas that differ by a factor of about 2.7 for the same building.

Implication for the Waldorf. Hotel-only GFA is the highest-leverage unknown in the whole model. Per room-night and LL97 exposure both scale with it directly. The Studio should expose GFA per key as a primary knob with the 970 and 2,700 ft2 cases both visible, and the Storyline should treat a split filing (hotel vs multifamily) as a dated policy event.

I5. The 2030 LL97 cliff is a 61% cut in the cap against a 50% cut in the electricity coefficient and a 0% cut in the gas coefficient, so electrification is necessary and not sufficient

Facets: 6 (LL97), 5 (targets), 8 (Waldorf plant).

Evidence. Hotel cap 0.00987 to 0.00385 tCO2e/ft2 (-61%); electricity 0.289 to 0.145 kg/kWh (-50%); natural gas 0.00005311 unchanged; steam 0.04493 to 0.0432 (-3.9%) (note 06 Q4). In CY2024, 90% of Manhattan hotels (404 of 447) exceeded 5.26 kgCO2e/ft2 and 28% exceeded 9.87 on the Portfolio Manager basis, which uses eGRID electricity (0.392) and so overstates LL97-basis electricity by about 36% (note 06 Q3). The renovated Waldorf has eight Viessmann Vitocrossal 300 CA3B 6.0 gas condensing boilers (DEP certificate 22 Jan 2025) and a 555 kW gas CHP plant (note 08 A1). DOB break-even: RECs at about $39/MWh equal the penalty after 2030, "the cost of RECs is likely to exceed the cost of maximum penalties" (note 06 Q4).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. "The grid cleans up, so electrified buildings comply" fails on arithmetic. An all-electric building whose kWh does not change sees emissions fall 50% while the cap falls 61%. A gas-heated building gets relief only on its electric share. My arithmetic on the cited numbers: an all-electric Waldorf at the luxury peer-median site EUI of 135 kBtu/ft2 (1,666,763 ft2 x 135 / 3.412 = 65.9 GWh x 0.145) emits about 9,560 t against a 6,420 t cap, 49% over; compliance at 0.145 needs a site EUI of about 90.6 kBtu/ft2 all-electric, which is roughly the Manhattan all-hotel median (94.2) rather than the luxury median (135). Heat pumps lower site EUI relative to boilers, so this is a bound, not a forecast.

Implication for the Waldorf. The 2030 path needs three things at once: electrified heat, a demand reduction of order 30% from the luxury peer EUI, and Zone J RECs for the remainder. Any Storyline story with fewer than all three should show residual penalty in 2030-34, and the 2035 cap (4,400 t) tightens again with no published coefficient.

I6. Gas heat beats electric resistance today and loses to any heat pump with COP above 1.95 on the eGRID factor, 1.44 on LL97 2024-29 and 0.72 on LL97 2030, so "wait for the grid" has no carbon case in New York

Facets: 6 (grid factors), 4 (guestroom HVAC and DHW), 8 (Waldorf boilers).

Evidence. Gas 0.181 kg per kWh of fuel (0.00005311 tCO2e/kBtu x 3.412); electricity 0.392 (eGRID NYCW), 0.289 (LL97 2024-29), 0.145 (LL97 2030-34) (note 06). CBECS 2018 lodging: water heating 16.5 and space heating 15.7 kBtu/ft2, together about 40% of site energy (note 01 KQ5). PNNL: service hot water 51% of a full-service hotel's gas (note 04 KQ1). Beneficial-electrification marginal coefficient 0.247 (2024) falling to 0.114 (2029), bankable (note 06 Q4).

My arithmetic. A condensing boiler at an assumed 90% efficiency delivers heat at 0.181 / 0.9 = 0.201 kg per kWh of heat. A heat pump delivers heat at (electricity factor) / COP. Break-even COP = factor / 0.201: 1.95 at 0.392, 1.44 at 0.289, 0.72 at 0.145. Electric resistance (COP 1) loses to gas today under both current factors and wins under the 2030 coefficient.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. Two opposite errors are common. One says New York's grid is too dirty for heat pumps; it is not, at any COP a modern unit achieves in all but design-day conditions. The other says resistance electrification is fine because the grid is cleaning up; it is not until 2030, and the PNNL hotel's PTHPs running in resistance mode because of a control fault is the cautionary case (note 04 KQ1).

Implication for the Waldorf. The eight gas boilers forgo an LL97 beneficial-electrification credit that declines every year it is not claimed (0.247 to 0.114 tCO2e/MWh across 2024-29). The only argument for the gas plant is capital and the landmark's mechanical constraints, not carbon. The Storyline should run electrification at COP 2, 2.5 and 3 against gas at 0.90 and 0.95 under all three factors; the sign should not flip.

I7. After 2030 a gas CHP cannot beat the LL97 grid coefficient on electricity alone, because it would need 125% electrical efficiency; its entire case rests on recovered heat

Facets: 8 (Waldorf CHP), 6 (LL97 coefficients), 5 (Scope 1).

Evidence. The Waldorf has a "proposed 555 kilowatt (kW) combined heating and power plant" (AKRF) with DOB NOW job M00342203 for CHP units on the 6th floor, $2.6 million (note 08 A1). LL97 charges all on-site gas at 0.00005311 tCO2e/kBtu and lists CHP credits among year-one deductions (10 used citywide) whose mechanics were not researched (note 06 Q4). Con Edison says cogenerated steam is about 25% lower carbon than on-site boilers (note 08 A1). Hilton's Scope 1 is "almost exclusively natural gas for comfort heating and hot water generation" (note 05 KQ1).

My arithmetic. CHP electricity carries 0.181 / e kg per kWh before any heat credit, where e is electrical efficiency. To match the grid on electricity alone, e = 0.181 / 0.289 = 63% in 2024-29 and 0.181 / 0.145 = 125% in 2030-34. The first is above any reciprocating engine; the second is impossible. The CHP's carbon merit is therefore entirely the boiler gas its recovered heat displaces, at 0.201 kg per kWh of heat, and it holds only while the heat is actually used.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. CHP is sold as efficient because it uses fuel twice. Under a falling grid coefficient the electricity half of that argument disappears on a known date, and a plant commissioned in 2025 with a 15-20 year life spends most of it on the wrong side of 2030.

Implication for the Waldorf. In the Storyline the CHP should be a dated asset with an electrical-efficiency knob (sweep 0.30-0.45) and a heat-utilisation knob, with a "retire CHP" event available from 2030. The CHP also places electricity generation inside Hilton's Scope 1, which is the scope its absolute SBT most needs to cut.

I8. Hilton's validated target is absolute (-46.2% Scope 1+2 by 2030 vs 2019), its managed absolute emissions rose from 2.23 to 2.44 Mt between 2008 and 2025, and the 2019 base is undisclosed, so adding a gas-fired flagship is a cost to the target that Hilton's own reporting cannot show

Facets: 5 (corporate targets), 8 (SBTi dashboard, CDP), 6 (Waldorf plant). Graph revisit "'Hilton's SBTs are intensity-only' is wrong".

Evidence. SBTi dashboard export (24 Oct 2026): "Hilton commits to reduce absolute scope 1 and 2 GHG emissions 46.2% by 2030 from a 2019 base year ... absolute scope 3 GHG emissions from franchises 27.5%"; net-zero fields blank (note 08 B). Hilton reports against -75% and -56% per m2 from 2008: 50.9% and 36.0% achieved by 2025 (note 05 KQ1). Managed Scope 1+2 market-based: 2,229,587 (2008), 2,350,694 (2022), 2,570,111 (2023), 2,504,014 (2024), 2,443,342 (2025) tCO2e; franchised Scope 3 2,180,912 to 4,011,353 (note 05 KQ1). CDP 2023: 5.14% of the absolute Scope 1+2 target achieved by 2022; franchise emissions 12.7% above the 2019 base of 3,884,715 t; 2019 Scope 1 and 2 base-year cells blank (note 08 B). FY2025 10-K: zero hits for "Local Law 97", "carbon", "greenhouse", "SBTi" (note 08 B).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. Hilton's headline (50.9% achieved against 75%) reads as two-thirds of the way there. Against the validated absolute target the managed estate has moved roughly 4% in the wrong direction since 2022, the year CDP recorded 5.1% progress. Intensity per m2 can fall indefinitely while floor area doubles (implied 14.2 to 31.8 million m2).

Implication for the Waldorf. Under the intensity frame the Waldorf need only be efficient per m2; under the validated frame its 1.67 million ft2 of new gas-heated Scope 1 must be netted by cuts elsewhere in a portfolio that is growing. Hilton's 2030 intensity goal (75% below 0.1567 = about 39 kgCO2e/m2, my arithmetic) and LL97's 2030 cap (0.00385 tCO2e/ft2 = about 41 kgCO2e/m2) converge on the same number from independent directions, and both are about a third of the Manhattan luxury peer median (about 122 kgCO2e/m2). The Storyline should carry a "Hilton absolute 2019 base" story-level constraint even though the base-year value must be a knob, because Hilton has not published it.

I9. Pool energy is driven by visitors and water, not climate, while every hotel benchmark normalises by climate, so a hotel pool is invisible to the benchmark and the Waldorf's only pool is residents-only

Facets: 2 (wellness), 7 (Kampel verification), 1 (ENERGY STAR, CHSB), 6 (Waldorf amenities).

Evidence. Kampel et al. 2016 (43 Norwegian facilities, 176 datasets): "climate does not drive the total energy performance of swimming facilities. Instead, overall water usage of the facility was observed to be most strongly correlated with the energy usage, followed by the number of visitors" (note 07 KQ4). Nitter et al.: bather load and opening days explain about 77% of pool energy (adjusted R2 0.77, n=30) (note 02 KQ1). ENERGY STAR's hotel score uses rooms, workers, refrigeration, HDD/CDD and a kitchen flag and has no pool term; CHSB segments by climate zone and collects no amenity data (note 02 KQ5; note 01 KQ1). Arenhart 2024 pooled r with hotel energy: outdoor temperature 0.91 (note 01 KQ4). Kraków hotel pool-area heating 399.6-657.4 kWh/day for a 51-room hotel (note 02 KQ1). The Waldorf's 25 m Starlight Pool is a residents-only 25th-floor amenity; no 2025 source confirms a hotel-guest pool (note 06 Q1; note 08 A2).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. A hotel with a pool in a cold climate gets a climate adjustment that explains none of its pool load, and so looks inefficient; the same hotel in a warm climate looks efficient for the wrong reason. The pool-energy literature itself confuses floor-area and water-surface bases by a factor of 2-4 (note 02 scope note), and the most-quoted 4,300 kWh/m2 figure is unsourced (graph revisit on Trianti-Stourna).

Implication for the Waldorf. The guest-night pool line is zero unless hotel access is confirmed, but the pool's energy is inside the single-lot LL84/LL97 filing (I4). The Kraków metering puts a hotel pool at 7.8-12.9 kWh per room per day for a 51-room hotel (my arithmetic: 399.6-657.4 / 51), the same order as the PNNL guestroom PTHP at about 13 kWh per room-day. In the Studio the pool must be allocated per bather or per m2 of water surface and attached to the residences, not to room-nights; in the Storyline it belongs in the LL97 denominator but not the guest-night numerator.

I10. The Waldorf's wellness load is thermal suites with no measured data anywhere, and at the only applicable benchmark the spa is about 2.5 kgCO2e per room-night, 9% of the NYC luxury median, if it is charged to rooms rather than to visits

Facets: 2 (wellness), 8 (gap fill), 6 (spa facts), 1 (allocation).

Evidence. Guerlain spa: 22,000 ft2, 16 treatment rooms, cryotherapy chamber, infrared sauna, steam room, hammam, "Arctic snow cave"; day pass $75 for hotel guests, $150 for locals (note 06 Q1; note 08 A2). No measured commercial hotel sauna, steam room or whirlpool energy exists (note 02 KQ2; note 08 C). Available inputs: nameplate 18-20 kW sauna and steam units, Harvia's "about 50% of the maximum" after 30-40 minutes, Cleopatra's worked example of 36,516 kWh/yr for an 11.4 kW generator at 16 h/day and 55% load (note 02 KQ2; note 08 C). CIBSE TM46 fitness and health centre 160 kWh/m2 electricity plus 440 fossil, from a secondary reproduction (note 02 KQ4). Bali hotel spa 7.24-11.22 kWh/m2/month electricity, cooling-dominated, a lower bound for New York (note 08 C). Spa-visit calculator: about 5 kgCO2e per visit at a large wellness resort, 3.5 at a small one, record only (note 01 KQ6).

My arithmetic. 22,000 ft2 = 2,044 m2. At TM46 fitness intensities: 2,044 x 160 = 327 MWh electricity x 0.289 = 94.5 t; 2,044 x 440 = 899 MWh fossil x 0.181 = 163 t; total about 257 t/yr. Over 104,600 occupied room-nights that is 2.46 kgCO2e per room-night, 8.7% of the CHSB NYC luxury median of 28.39. At 3.5-5 kg per visit, 257 t implies 51,000-73,000 visits a year, 140-200 a day.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. HCMI would spread the spa over every room-night by floor area, and 2.5 kg looks small enough to ignore. But the spa is priced as a separate product ($75 day pass), the Best Western Berlin ruling turned on omitted service lines (note 05 KQ6), and the entire number rests on a UK benchmark table reproduced secondhand. The visit-count implied by the per-visit figures is a plausibility test the paper has no data to run.

Implication for the Waldorf. Allocate the spa per visit, not per room-night, with the per-visit figure as a Monte Carlo range; report the HCMI-smeared value beside it. The Guerlain spa is the single most valuable sub-metering target in the building for the paper's purpose, because nothing published can replace it.

I11. Claims law closes the offset route on a known date, and the demand-side levers that remain legal move the footprint by low single digits, while the material levers are procurement, electrification and floor area

Facets: 5 (claims regulation, offsets), 3 (F&B levers), 4 (guestroom levers).

Evidence. Directive (EU) 2024/825 applies from 27 Sep 2026 and bans neutral/reduced/positive-impact claims based on offsetting outside the value chain; BGH I ZR 98/23 (27 Jun 2024) requires an ad to state whether emissions are reduced or offset; Landgericht Berlin II struck down Best Western Mannheim's "climate neutral" stays because laundry and waste were omitted (note 05 KQ6). LL97 offsets are AHRF only, at most 10% of the limit (note 06 Q4). SBTi V1.3.1 and V2.0: credits "do not count as reductions toward meeting near-term or long-term science-based targets" (note 05 KQ3). West et al. 2023: 6.1% of REDD+ credits linked to additional reductions (note 05 KQ4). Legal demand-side levers: menu carbon labels 1.5-5% emission cuts with some null results (Lowrie & Lohmann 2025; Brunner 2018 -3.6%); towel cards +9 to +12 percentage points of reuse in the US (Goldstein via Bohner & Schlüter); occupancy-based HVAC controls 18.4% of guestroom HVAC (DOE/PNNL 2012) (note 05 KQ7; note 04 KQ3, KQ7). Material drivers: meat 3% of Accor's food purchases by weight but 27% of food carbon (note 03 KQ1); floor area per key explains about 60% of the luxury premium (I3); electrification (I5, I6).

Why the obvious reading is wrong. The guest-facing programme a marketing team can run (labels, towel cards, opt-in housekeeping, a removals levy) and the programme that moves the number (central plant, procurement contracts, how much building there is per key) barely overlap. The first is now the only legally safe claim set; the second is where the tonnes are.

Implication for the Waldorf. The paper should state effect sizes for guest-facing measures as the controlled trials give them (low single digits) and locate the material reductions in decisions guests never see. A removals "contribution" at $180/t on a 50-150 kg full-scope night is $9-27, 1-2% of a $1,500 rate (note 05 KQ4 inference), and must be worded as contribution, not compensation.

I12. The largest lines in a legally complete per-stay footprint are the ones no hotel group measures: a return luxury-car airport transfer (about 25.6 kgCO2e) is 90% of the NYC luxury energy-only room-night (28.4)

Facets: 4 (transport, services), 3 (F&B), 5 (scope 3 disclosure, Best Western ruling), 1 (HCMI boundary).

Evidence. DEFRA 2024 luxury petrol car 0.31949 kgCO2e/km; 40 km one way is 12.8 kg tailpipe (note 04 KQ6). Marriott, Hilton and IHG report no guest-travel line in Scope 3 (note 04 KQ6). Dolder: employee commuting 12-15% of total (note 03 KQ1). Food procurement 40-58% of a full-scope room-night (note 07 KQ2). HCMI excludes guest travel, food, waste and water (note 01 KQ2). The Berlin court required laundry and waste in a neutrality boundary (note 05 KQ6).

My arithmetic. Return transfer 2 x 12.8 = 25.6 kg, against the CHSB NYC luxury median of 28.39; per guest it is comparable to the whole energy-only room-night shared by 1.8 guests.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. The paper's title case is food, but transport is the line most likely to be litigated if a "per-stay" claim is made to a European guest, and it is the one line with no hotel data at all. The hotel controls it (house cars, contracted limousines, Scope 1 or Scope 3 category 1 depending on who pays).

Implication for the Waldorf. The Studio needs a transfer module with the DEFRA segment factors (petrol 0.319, diesel 0.207, PHEV 0.121, BEV on grid) and a knob for share of guests transferred; the per-stay disclosure should show it as a separate line so that the room-night figure is not contaminated by a guest choice.

I13. Counting food waste at cradle-to-grave factors overstates the waste line by 3-7 times and makes a 21% waste cut look like a 21% carbon cut when it is about 4% of food carbon

Facets: 3 (food waste), 1 (allocation). Graph decision "Carry embodied emissions of wasted food in the purchases bucket".

Evidence. Factors: DEFRA food waste to landfill about 627 kgCO2e/t, composting about 9; BIO Intelligence 2010 cradle-to-grave 1.9 kg/kg; Hilton Green Ramadan implies 7.4 t / 1.7 t = 4.35 kg/kg (note 03 KQ4). Hotel waste 0.36-0.53 kg per cover; SHA "more than 20% of the food that is bought by hotels is thrown away"; Champions 12.3 (42 hotels) 21% reduction in pre-consumer waste in 12 months at 6.8:1 benefit-cost (note 03 KQ4).

My arithmetic. Per cover, 0.36-0.53 kg at 1.9-4.35 kg/kg is 0.7-2.3 kgCO2e; at 0.627 it is 0.23-0.33. If waste is about 20% of purchases and falls 21%, purchases fall about 4%, and only if purchasing follows.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. The waste programmes' own carbon claims (Winnow, SHA's 17% "food waste" share) carry the embodied emissions of the food, which are already in purchased goods. In a correctly bounded inventory a waste programme shows up as a small purchases reduction plus a tiny disposal reduction, not as a headline waste-line cut. Organics diversion under LL146 (mandatory for the Waldorf's three restaurants and 43,000 ft2 of banqueting) moves 627 to 9 kg/t on the disposal line only (note 06 Q8).

Implication for the Waldorf. The Studio's waste knob should be disposal-only and its food knob should carry waste as a purchases multiplier; otherwise the two knobs double count and the waste lever looks 3-7x too powerful.

I14. Occupancy inverts the star ranking and the Waldorf's own pricing works against its per-room-night figure: a 10-point occupancy gap is a 13% change at constant building load

Facets: 1 (Puig, Arenhart), 6 (Manhattan market), 4 (CHSB thresholds).

Evidence. Puig 2017: 2-star hotels at 30% occupancy emit 23 kgCO2e per overnight stay, 5-star at 50% emit 22, "inversely proportional to the occupancy rate" (note 07 KQ2). Arenhart 2024: occupancy r = 0.20 non-significant overall, -0.21 for upscale (note 01 KQ4). CHSB NYC luxury CY2021 (pandemic recovery, n=8) 40.54 against 26-28 in normal years (note 01 KQ1). PwC H1 2025: Manhattan luxury 76.4% occupancy and $490.75 ADR; all-class 82.3%; Waldorf entry rate $1,500, about 3x luxury ADR (note 06 Q7).

My arithmetic. With building load fixed, per room-night scales as 1/occupancy; 86% to 76% is a factor 1.13; 76% to 65% (a plausible ramp-up year) is 1.17.

Why the obvious reading is wrong. A hotel that prices for rate rather than occupancy is choosing a higher per-room-night footprint for the same building, and the paper's first validation point (CY2026 LL84, disclosed 2027) falls in a ramp-up year that opened mid-July 2025 and will have a lower occupancy than steady state.

Implication for the Waldorf. Occupancy must be an explicit knob (the report recommends 76.4%, not 82%), the Studio's implied 86% (I4) should be revisited, and the Storyline should carry an occupancy ramp 2025-2028 so that the CY2026 comparison is not misread as a performance gap.

Part 2. Hypotheses

Notation: "support" and "refute" name the result that would do each. All effect ranges are the notes' ranges; where a knob has no evidence base I say "assumption knob".

Ordering and sequencing

H1. Electrify heat before buying RECs; a story that does them in the other order pays more cumulative LL97 penalty 2030-2040. Test (Storyline): Story A: heat pumps replace the eight gas boilers in 2028, Zone J RECs purchased from 2030 for residual electricity; Story B: RECs from 2026 (CHPE available 2026), heat pumps in 2033. Same demand path. Metric: cumulative tCO2e (LL97 basis) and cumulative penalty dollars 2025-2040; also banked beneficial-electrification credit. Expected: A wins. RECs deduct only from electricity emissions (1 RCNY 103-14 (e)(1)), so in B the gas Scope 1 is untouched until 2033; the marginal coefficient for beneficial-electrification credit falls from 0.247 (2024) to 0.114 (2029), so A banks more credit; DOB says RECs cost more than the penalty after 2030, so B's early RECs are the expensive instrument. Refuted if B wins, which would mean REC price and the CHPE-era REC supply dominate, and the capital-timing knob should be examined.

H2. Gas-side demand measures keep their carbon value through 2040; electric-side measures lose half of it in 2030. Sequence DHW, laundry and kitchen hot-water efficiency before guestroom HVAC controls. Test (Storyline): Introduce 1 GWh/yr of saving as (i) gas in 2026, (ii) electricity in 2026, (iii) gas in 2032, (iv) electricity in 2032. Metric: cumulative tCO2e 2025-2040 per GWh saved. Expected: (i) ≈ (iii) ≈ 0.181 kg/kWh x years; (ii) falls from 0.289 to 0.145 kg/kWh at 2030; electric saving is worth about half as much carbon after 2030. PNNL: service hot water is 51% of hotel gas and laundry about 25% (note 04 KQ1). Refuted only if the Storyline's gas coefficient path falls, which the rule text does not provide for.

H3. One opted-out housekeeping night saves 2-5 times the carbon of one occupancy-controlled room-night today and 4-10 times in 2030, so opt-in housekeeping outranks HVAC controls as a guest-facing lever. Test (Studio levers slice, then Storyline): Opt-out night at the Sheraton Boston values (37.2 gal water, 0.19 kWh electricity, 25,000 BTU = 7.33 kWh gas); OBC at the DOE/PNNL 18.4% of guestroom HVAC (0.9-2.6 kWh per occupied room-night). Metric: kgCO2e per room-night per measure under 2024-29 and 2030-34 coefficients. Expected (my arithmetic): opt-out 7.33 x 0.181 + 0.19 x 0.289 = 1.38 kg (1.35 kg in 2030); OBC 0.26-0.75 kg (0.13-0.38 in 2030). Supported if the ratio is in those ranges. Refuted if the Studio's laundry module gives much lower gas per opted-out night; luxury linen at 12-25 lb per room should push the other way. Uptake is unknown (no Hilton or Marriott opt-in rates found) and must be a Monte Carlo range.

H4. Procurement before kitchen: a cut in the meat share of food carbon beats full kitchen electrification on the full-scope guest-night in every year, and has zero effect on LL97 exposure. Test (Studio food knobs and CBECS cooking end use; Storyline): Story A: meat share of food carbon from 27% (Accor) to half that (assumption knob) in 2026; Story B: all kitchen gas (CBECS cooking 14.1 kBtu/ft2, 10.1 of it gas) to electric induction in 2026. Metrics: full-scope kgCO2e per guest-night; LL97 tCO2e. Expected: A moves the full-scope night by several kg (food is 40-58% of it; Dolder 5.4-6.1 kg per restaurant guest); B moves it by well under 1 kg per cover (kitchen energy 0.5-1.5 kgCO2e per cover inferred in note 03) and reduces LL97 emissions by the gas-to-electric delta. The hypothesis is that the two lever sets are disjoint: A is invisible to LL97, B is nearly invisible to the full-scope night. Refuted if B's full-scope effect approaches A's, which would mean the Studio's kitchen energy per cover is far above Mudie's 1.5-3.3 kWh.

H5. Retire the CHP in 2030: a story that keeps the 555 kW CHP running after 2030 has higher LL97 emissions than one that buys grid electricity, unless heat utilisation exceeds a threshold the Storyline can compute. Test (Storyline): CHP asset with electrical-efficiency knob (sweep 0.30-0.45), heat-utilisation knob (0-100% of recoverable heat displacing boiler gas at 0.201 kg/kWh heat), 6,000 h/yr (assumption). Metric: net LL97 tCO2e versus a no-CHP story, 2025-29 and 2030-34. Expected: before 2030 the CHP is near break-even only with high heat utilisation; after 2030 it loses at every efficiency in the sweep unless heat utilisation is high, because 0.181/e exceeds 0.145 for all e below 1.25 (I7). Refuted if the Storyline's LL97 CHP credit (mechanics not researched) is large enough to flip the sign; that would be a finding about the rule, not the plant.

Boundary and denominator

H6. The full-scope multiplier on the Waldorf's energy-only night is below the Irish 1.7-2.4 under the 2024-29 LL97 factor and rises toward it under the 2030 factor, with no change in the food knobs. Test (Studio boundary toggle, scope slice; Storyline no-intervention story): Fix food, laundry, waste at defaults; vary electricity factor 0.392, 0.289, 0.145. Metric: full-scope / Scope 1+2 ratio. Expected: the ratio falls as the factor rises (I1); Zurich (electricity 3-4% of total) is the limit case. Supported if the ratio spans roughly 1.4-2.2 across the three factors; refuted if it is flat, which would mean the Studio's food line is small relative to the notes' 5-6 kg per cover.

H7. The Waldorf exceeds the CHSB NYC luxury upper quartile (38.19) per occupied room-night at any hotel GFA per key above about 950 ft2, while sitting at the Manhattan luxury peer median per m2. Test (Studio property knobs, benchmarks): Fix intensity at 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 (122 kg/m2) and occupancy at 76.4%; sweep GFA per key 800-2,700 ft2. Metric: kg per occupied room-night against the IQR 23.95-38.19; kg/m2 against the peer set. Expected (my arithmetic): 40.5 kg at 1,000 ft2 per key, 109 at 2,700; crossing 38.19 at about 940 ft2 per key. Refuted if hotel-only GFA is published below that, which no source suggests (most rooms over 570 ft2 before corridors, public space, 43,000 ft2 of events and a 22,000 ft2 spa).

H8. Splitting the single-lot filing into hotel and multifamily raises the hotel's LL97 penalty exposure and lowers the residences', because the hotel floors are more intense per ft2. Test (Storyline policy event "split filing" in 2027 with a hotel-share knob 40-70% and an intensity-ratio knob hotel:residence 1.5-3, assumption knobs): Metric: penalty by component and total. Expected: total penalty is roughly conserved but shifts to the hotel component; the hotel alone at luxury-peer intensity exceeds its reduced cap by more than the combined lot does. This cannot be settled on current evidence (see H15) but the simulator can show the sensitivity, which is the point: the hotel/residence boundary is a policy and disclosure decision, not a physical one.

H9. Per guest-night the Waldorf's full-scope figure is roughly half the per-room-night figure, and the ratio the Studio uses (1.6 implied) is below the only measured full-service value (1.80-1.92). Test (Studio denominator toggle): Sweep guests per occupied room 1.4-2.0. Metric: per guest-night. Expected: a 1.6 to 1.86 change moves the guest-night figure by 14%. The food line scales per guest, the building line per room, so the guest-night figure is less sensitive to the ratio under full scope than under energy-only; supported if the Studio shows that asymmetry. Refuted if it does not, which would mean food is not modelled per guest.

H10. Breakfast included in the rate adds 8-30% to an energy-only guest-night depending on the grid, and ADEME's 30% is the low-carbon-grid limit. Test (Studio food knob "breakfast included", buffet format): Breakfast at 1.3 kg (Filimonau), 3.59 kg (Cool Food threshold) and 5.1 kg (30% of ADEME's 17.11 kg 5-star night, my arithmetic); waste at 0.3 kg per cover disposal-only; energy-only guest-night 28.39 / 1.8 = 15.8 kg. Expected: +8% to +32% at the 2024-29 factor; the share rises under the 2030 factor; buffet waste adds 0.2 kg disposal-only but 0.6-1.3 kg if the cradle-to-grave factor is wrongly used (I13). Refuted if the Studio's breakfast line falls outside 1-5 kg, which would mean its factor set differs from all three sources.

Waldorf-specific

H11. No single lever reaches the 2030 cap; only a story with electrified heat, a demand reduction of order 30% from the luxury peer EUI, and Zone J RECs for the remainder is compliant in 2030-34, and even that story fails the 2035 cap without further measures. Test (Storyline): Baseline: gas boilers plus CHP at luxury peer EUI 135 kBtu/ft2 on 1,666,763 ft2. Stories: (a) electrify 2028 only; (b) electrify plus 30% demand cut; (c) b plus CHPE RECs for the gap; (d) RECs only. Metric: tCO2e against caps 16,450 / 6,420 / 4,400; penalty. Expected (my arithmetic in I5): (a) about 9,560 t in 2030, 49% over; (b) near 6,700 t, marginal; (c) compliant; (d) non-compliant because gas is untouched. In 2035 (cap 4,400) (c) needs either further demand cuts or a lower electricity coefficient that no rule yet provides. Refuted if (a) complies, which would require the heat-pump COP to cut site EUI far below 135, and the Storyline should print the COP that achieves it.

H12. The Waldorf's first LL84 operating year (CY2026) will read 15-20% worse per room-night than steady state because of occupancy ramp, and the per-m2 figure will read close to steady state. Test (Storyline with occupancy ramp 2025-2028: 55-65% in 2026 rising to 76% by 2028, assumption knob): Metric: per room-night and per m2 in 2026 vs 2029. Expected: per room-night 1.17-1.38x steady state at 65%-55%; per m2 unchanged except for occupancy-dependent loads (DHW, laundry, F&B covers), which CBECS puts at roughly 40% of site energy, so per m2 moves by far less. Supported if the two denominators diverge by that order. Refuted if the Storyline's load model is nearly all occupancy-variable, which the PNNL base load (about 400 kW continuous) argues against.

Uncertainty structure

H13. The Monte Carlo variance of the full-scope guest-night is dominated by the food factor set, not by any amenity assumption. Test (Storyline MC): Ranges from the notes: beef 16.0 (CONCITO, Irish inventory) / 28.73 (Clune) / 99.48 (Poore & Nemecek beef herd) kgCO2e/kg; spa 87-600 kWh/m2 (Bali electric lower bound to TM46); thermal-suite duty 30-100% of nameplate; laundry 4.5-11 kg per room-night; OBC 10.8-26.5%. Metric: variance decomposition of the full-scope guest-night. Expected: the beef factor alone spans a factor of 6 on the single largest line; amenity ranges span a factor of 7 on lines that are 3-9% of the total. Food wins. Refuted if the Studio's menu composition gives beef a tiny share; the Accor figure (meat 27% of food carbon) argues against that.

H14. The grid-factor choice (eGRID 0.392 vs LL97 0.289) changes the Scope 2 line by 36% and the full-scope total by well under that, and its effect on the F&B share is larger than the effect of any amenity knob. Test (Studio factor toggle, scope slice): Metric: Scope 2 line, total, F&B share, under both factors. Expected: Scope 2 +36%; total +10-20% depending on electricity share; F&B share moves by several points. Supported if the F&B share moves more under the factor toggle than under the spa knob at its full range. This is the test of I1 inside the app.

Cannot be settled with the simulator

H15. The hotel-only floor area and the hotel/residence energy split determine both the per-room-night result and the LL97 exposure, and no public record will supply them. What the simulator can do: show sensitivity (H7, H8). What it cannot do: pick the value. The CY2026 LL84 record (disclosed 2027) is still a single-lot filing. Data that would settle it: the owner's sub-metered split by property use (Article 311 tenant sub-meters are an LL88 requirement the notes cite in the GFE criteria), a split LL84 filing, or the Department of Finance condominium declaration with floor-area schedules (not retrievable online per note 08).

H16. The actual energy of the Guerlain thermal suite (infrared sauna, steam room, hammam, snow cave, cryotherapy chamber) is unknown to within a factor of 3 and the simulator can only propagate the assumed range. What the simulator can do: carry nameplate x duty (30-100%) as a Monte Carlo range and show its share. What it cannot do: narrow it. No measured commercial hotel sauna, steam or whirlpool energy exists anywhere the team reached (Harvia, Tylö, Finnleo, KLAFS, Amerec, Mr Steam, Carbon Trust, ASHRAE Journal checked, note 08 C), and the snow cave and cryotherapy chamber have no published data of any kind. Data that would settle it: twelve months of sub-metering on the 5th-floor spa circuits, or a manufacturer M&V study under hotel duty.

H17. The choice of food emission-factor set (Poore & Nemecek vs Clune vs Agribalyse/CONCITO) moves the headline more than every building lever combined, and only supplier-specific data can resolve it. What the simulator can do: run the three sets as scenarios and show the spread (H13). What it cannot do: say which is right for the Waldorf's suppliers. The sets differ by 3.5x on beef (28.73 vs 99.48) and 6x including the Irish inventory's 16.0; they are not errors but different boundaries (land-use change, herd averaging, regional distribution centre). Data that would settle it: supplier-level procurement data through Hilton's Watershed partnership (2025) or Avendra-type procurement records with mass by category; Accor's finding that meat is 3% of weight and 27% of carbon shows that mass by category is enough to get the first-order answer.

Part 3. Thin ice

The five numbers carrying the most weight relative to how well they are verified, in descending order of weight.

1. The hotel-only floor area and occupancy implicit in the Studio defaults (about 364,000 ft2, about 86%) versus the report's bounding case (about 1.0 million ft2, 76.4%). Neither is sourced; the public record gives only the 1,666,763 ft2 single-lot GFA, "most rooms over 570 ft2", 43,000 ft2 of events, a 22,000 ft2 spa and a 7,000 ft2 gym. The per-room-night result scales linearly with the area and inversely with the occupancy, so the two documents' implied room-night figures for the same building differ by roughly a factor of 3 before any physics. Firm up: expose GFA per key and occupancy as primary Studio knobs with both cases visible; obtain the condominium declaration's area schedule or a split LL84 filing; use PwC luxury-class occupancy (76.4%) and a 2025-28 ramp.

2. The full-scope multiplier of 1.7-2.4 and the F&B share of 40-58%, which carry the report's title. They rest on one unaudited company inventory (Dolder: "checked for completeness and plausibility, but have not been audited"; F&B sub-categories locked in a chart) and one Research Square preprint of four Irish hotels that is "not peer-reviewed" and misquotes Puig 2017 in its own comparison table. The direction is corroborated by Filimonau 2011 (breakfast plus laundry +40-70% on 4.5-7.5 kg) and by Accor's group split, but the magnitude for a New York hotel is extrapolated across grids (I1). Firm up: a third full-boundary hotel inventory on a fossil-heavy grid; the Dolder's F&B sub-categories; journal publication of Attari & Hanrahan.

3. The spa intensity. The only applicable benchmark is CIBSE TM46 "fitness and health centre" at 160 electricity plus 440 fossil kWh/m2, taken from a secondary reproduction of a 2008 UK table, applied to a Manhattan thermal suite with infrared sauna, steam, hammam, snow cave and cryotherapy, none of which has any measured data. The Bali figure (87-135 kWh/m2/yr electricity) is a cooling-dominated lower bound. The Waldorf's entire wellness line, and under HCMI about 9% of its room-night (I10), rests on this. Firm up: the CIBSE TM46 primary table; sub-metering of the Guerlain spa; manufacturer duty-cycle data for the 18-20 kW units.

4. The 2.2-night average stay. This number is not in the evidence base. Note 06 Q7 states: "Average length of stay for Manhattan luxury guests: not found in any fetched source (STR/CoStar and NYC Tourism + Conventions data behind paywalls)." If the Studio converts per room-night to per stay with 2.2 nights, every per-stay figure, including the removals-contribution price per stay, inherits an unsourced multiplier. Firm up: STR/CoStar length-of-stay for Manhattan luxury; NYC Tourism + Conventions visitor data; Hilton PMS data for the property. Until then the per-stay output should be labelled as an assumption and the per-night output preferred.

5. The pool benchmarks and the base-building EUI. Pool: the 4,300 and 5,200 kWh/m2 water-surface values are unsourced generalisations per Kampel's reading of Trianti-Stourna; the measured Greek value is 450.1 kWh/m2 of usable area (about 1,540 kWh/m2 water surface at a 3.43 ratio); the 1,094.5 figure remains unlocated; the Kraków hotel metering omits pool size and winter months. For the Waldorf this matters to the LL97 single-lot total, not to the guest-night, because the pool is residents-only. Base-building EUI: the Manhattan luxury peer median of about 135 kBtu/ft2 and 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 is eleven hotels whose GFA restatements between CY2023 and CY2024 moved intensities by 10-71% (Pierre +30%, Carlyle +71%, Four Seasons +27%); the CHSB NYC luxury figures are n=21 with a standard deviation two-thirds of the mean. Firm up: read Trianti-Stourna 1998 and Kampel 2013 in full; pull peer key counts and hotel-only GFA for per-key intensities; wait for the Waldorf's first full operating year (CY2026, disclosed 2027), knowing it is a ramp-up year on a single lot.

A sixth, lower-weight item for completeness: Deng & Burnett's 564 kWh/m2 is known only through two citing papers. It carries little weight in the Waldorf case (1995 Hong Kong, cooling-dominated) and is listed because the brief asked; it would be firmed up by the primary text, which no open repository holds.

The hypothesis to test first

H11, the three-lever 2030 compliance story. It uses only numbers the notes already carry (cap, coefficients, peer EUI, GFA), it is the one the Storyline is built for, and its result decides whether the paper's regulatory section says "electrify" or "electrify, cut demand by a third, and buy Zone J RECs". If (a) complies on its own, I5 and I7 are overstated and the CHP question becomes secondary; if it does not, the ordering hypotheses H1 and H5 become the next runs.

Counting Food Doubles the Luxury Guest-Night Footprint

Published benchmarks place an energy-only New York City luxury hotel room-night at a median 28.4 kgCO2e (HCMI basis, calendar 2023, n=21, interquartile range 24–38), but the only hotel inventories that include purchased food and drink show that figure roughly doubling: at the Dolder Grand in Zurich food and beverages were 48–56% of the whole-hotel footprint in 2023–2025 and at four Irish hotels food procurement was 40–58% of a room-night, so a full-scope luxury room-night runs 1.7–2.4 times its Scope 1+2 value. Among the building-side amenities, heated pools and thermal suites are the only services whose energy is of the same order as guestroom HVAC and hot water; spa treatment rooms, gyms, minibars, elevators, ice and toiletries each add well under 1 kgCO2e per night, and the published evidence for all of them is nameplate, trade or single-building data rather than peer-reviewed hotel sub-metering. The notes correct two things earlier drafts got wrong: Hilton's SBTi-validated targets are absolute (−46.2% Scope 1+2 and −27.5% franchise Scope 3 by 2030 from 2019), not the 75%/56% per-square-metre goals Hilton reports against, and the "4,300 kWh per m2 of pool water" figure attributed to Greek audits is an unsourced generalisation, with the measured Greek value being 450 kWh per m2 of floor area. For the Waldorf Astoria New York (375 keys, 1.67 million ft2 benchmarked as one "Hotel" with 372 condominiums, eight gas condensing boilers and a 555 kW gas CHP plant per permit records), Local Law 97's 2030 hotel cap of 0.00385 tCO2e/ft2 (about 41 kgCO2e/m2) sits far below the CY2024 Manhattan luxury peer median of about 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 (about 122 kg/m2), and credits can cover at most 10% of the limit. A credible green commitment therefore cannot be a "carbon-neutral stay": it is a disclosed, full-boundary per-stay footprint that includes food and laundry, physical reductions and Zone J renewable electricity that count under both LL97 and SBTi, and durable removals bought as a stated contribution, not as compensation.

Energy-only benchmarks put a NYC luxury room-night at 24–38 kgCO2e, and the denominator decides the ranking

The Cornell Hotel Sustainability Benchmarking Index is the only large benchmark that segments by chain scale and market, and its absolute values live in the Excel index tool rather than the PDF reports. Parsed directly, the CHSB 2025 edition (calendar-year 2023 data, 27,219 hotels) gives a New York City luxury non-resort median of 28.39 kgCO2e per occupied room-night on the HCMI rooms basis (n=21, lower quartile 23.95, upper quartile 38.19, mean 35.64 with standard deviation 19.0), alongside 102.3 kWh per occupied room, 321.6 kWh/m2/yr and 88.7 kgCO2e/m2/yr of conditioned space (CHSB 2025 Excel index). The whole-hotel measure without meeting-space apportionment (Measure 3) is 27.02 for the same segment, so HCMI's apportionment removes only about 5% from rooms in NYC luxury. The US luxury non-resort median is 32.12 kgCO2e per occupied room (n=137), 2.3 times upper-midscale (14.03) and 2.7 times midscale (11.74), but only 1.43 times upper-midscale per square metre (83.8 versus 58.8 kgCO2e/m2), which means roughly 60% of the per-room-night luxury premium is floor area per occupied room and about 40% is higher intensity per square metre (CHSB 2025 Excel index). The index's own outlier screens tell the same story: full-service luxury records are accepted between 50 and 700 kWh per occupied room, midscale between 20 and 300 (CHSB 2023 report).

New York, NY – luxury non-resort 21 23.95 28.39 38.19

United States – luxury non-resort 137 25.08 32.12 43.62
