Source: CHSB 2025 Excel index tool, parsed by the research team; GHG = CO2, CH4, N2O with eGRID location-based electricity factors and AR4 GWPs (CHSB 2025 report PDF).

Two cautions apply before any of these numbers enter a paper. First, the NYC luxury sample is 8–25 hotels in every edition and its standard deviation is two-thirds of the mean, so the interquartile range, not the median, is the citable statistic; the series runs 28.89 (CY2016, n=9), 27.52, insufficient, 21.78, 40.54 (CY2021, pandemic recovery, n=8), 26.19 and 28.39 kgCO2e per occupied room (CHSB workbooks CY2016–CY2023). Second, the US luxury decline from 43.30 (CY2016) to 32.12 (CY2023) is mostly grid decarbonisation and sample change rather than efficiency, because energy per occupied room fell only from 144 to 119 kWh over the same period (CHSB workbooks). CHSB itself states that "no additional information was collected on the range of amenities that could contribute towards hotels' utility use" (CHSB 2025 report PDF), so the segment gap cannot be decomposed by service from CHSB alone.

The peer-reviewed per-night literature spans an order of magnitude, and the spread is boundary and denominator, not measurement error. In full text, Puig et al. (2017) report 23, 22 and 12 kgCO2e per overnight stay for Spanish 2-, 5- and 4-star coastal hotels (ISO 14067 functional unit, operational electricity, fuels, water, cleaning products and waste, no food or outsourced laundry, data 2000–2013), with the 2-star hotels out-emitting 5-star because their occupancy was 30% against 50% (Puig et al. 2017, author manuscript). Filimonau et al. (2011), also read in full, give two UK 3-star hotels at 7.5 and 4.5 kgCO2e per guest-night operational, rising to 11.65 and 8.25 once breakfast (1.3 kg), daily laundry (1.75 kg) and a 15% embodied uplift are added (Filimonau et al. 2011). Huang, Wang and Wang (2015), whose abstract was verified through a Wayback capture, give 58 Taiwanese luxury hotels at 132 kgCO2e/m2/yr, 29 kgCO2e per guest-night and 50 kgCO2e per room-night, energy only (Huang et al. 2015). A 2026 Beijing five-star case finds 95.9 kgCO2e per guest-night under an extended operational boundary against 36.7 from the Greenview footprinting benchmark, with the authors noting that restaurants, conference rooms, wellness and laundry "could not be separated" from whole-building data (Morales et al. 2026). At the far end, a Taiwanese life-cycle study reports 147.93 kgCO2e per standard-room night including embodied and amenity production, with amenities and laundry about 16% of the total; this is a repository record whose method was not read and should be cited as such (Airiti record). Xuchao, Priyadarsini and Lee (2010) showed for Singapore that "a hotel's carbon intensity ranking is rather sensitive to the normalizing denominator chosen" (Xuchao et al. 2010), and the Irish data make the point concrete: full-service hotels run 1.80–1.92 guests per occupied room, so per-guest-night values are roughly half of per-room-night values (Attari & Hanrahan 2026 preprint). Any comparison between Puig (per guest), CHSB (per occupied room) and Huang (both) must state the denominator.

The drivers literature is consistent about what the luxury premium is made of. Brighter Planet's 2012 model of 46,000 US hotels names square footage per room, climate, amenities, energy source and grid cleanliness, finds that a hotel with 80 ft2 of floor space per room uses more than three times the energy per room-night of one with 20, and ranks JW Marriott at 38 kgCO2e per room-night against Hilton at 31 and Motel 6 at 23 (Brighter Planet 2012). The EPA ENERGY STAR hotel score explains source energy with room density, worker density, commercial refrigeration density, degree-days and a kitchen flag, and found laundry, occupancy rate and conference space statistically non-significant; it carries no pool or fitness term (EPA Technical Reference, Aug 2018). A 2024 meta-analysis gives pooled correlations with energy use of 0.65 for floor area, 0.58 for star level, 0.78 for employees, 0.81 for room revenue and a non-significant 0.20 for occupancy, which turns negative (−0.21) for upscale hotels (Arenhart et al. 2024). Bohdanowicz and Martinac's 184-hotel Hilton International and Scandic study (2004 data) found that "hotel standard, total hotel floor area, number of guest-nights sold and number of food covers sold all affect the energy and water use" and suggested that "benchmarking of facility components may be necessary"; its abstract carries no kWh figure, and the 364 and 285 kWh/m2 values often quoted for Hilton and Scandic come from later reviews, not the paper (Bohdanowicz & Martinac 2007; Schick 2024 SLR). Two sources disagree on whether star rating changes intensity per square metre at all: the Hotel Energy Solutions meta-analysis found no statistically significant EUI difference by star rating in Europe (HES 2011), while CHSB CY2023 shows US luxury at 1.43 times upper-midscale per square metre (CHSB 2025 Excel); the paper should carry area per key and occupancy as explicit drivers rather than a segment multiplier.

HCMI spreads every amenity across room-nights by floor area, so no benchmark isolates a spa or a kitchen

The Hotel Carbon Measurement Initiative, used by more than 30,000 hotels and the basis of CHSB Measure 1, is a whole-building Scope 1 and 2 inventory plus outsourced laundry. Its boundary explicitly includes "restaurants, meeting spaces, shops, casinos, golf courses, spas, garden space, fitness centres, 'back of house', and any other amenities", and it then divides the total between guestrooms and meeting space "based on the ratio of space occupied by guest rooms and meeting space", with the remaining areas "of heart of house, spa, retail, fitness, public space, restaurants, etc." allocated proportionately to the two outputs (HCMI v2.0 methodology, sec 3.0 and 6.3). The methodology states that it "assumes equal intensity for all areas of the hotel", that it "does not account for different room sizes (e.g. standard room v. suite)", and that it measures the 12-month average, "not the incremental GHG emissions from a guest room" (HCMI v2.0, sec 6.4 and 7.2). Food, waste, water, guest travel and all other Scope 3 are excluded by design, and the How-to Guide applies a flat 1% uplift for refrigerants when no data are entered (HCMI v2.0 How-to Guide). The consequence for an amenity paper is central: under HCMI a guest who never enters the spa carries exactly the same footprint as one who does, and a spa-heavy hotel's extra load is smeared over every room-night and meeting-hour pro rata to area. HCMI's own authors list "amenities or other services within hotels but separate from their direct operations for the provision of the room night, which will impact the allocation of emissions" as an open issue for a v3.0, and the April 2026 v3.0 process flow still apportions by floor area between guest rooms, meeting space and remaining space after deducting private space (HCMI v2.0, sec 1.0; HCMI v3.0 process flow, April 2026). A 2026 critique describes the result as "per-room-night benchmarks that are internally inconsistent" across boundaries, with food "explicitly absent" (Attari & Hanrahan 2026).

The general standards offer a hierarchy but no hotel guidance. The GHG Protocol Product Standard says to avoid allocation by subdividing processes where possible, otherwise to allocate "based on the inherent physical characteristics of the product", and only then by economic value; ISO 14067 follows ISO 14044 but does not permit system expansion to avoid allocation; these wordings were taken from secondary summaries and must be checked against the standards' text (CarbonChain summary; GHG Protocol Product Standard summary of changes). A NASA comment on the Scope 3 Standard draft noted that its discussion of shared facilities "provides no guidance on how to address this issue" (NASA comment). No peer-reviewed study was found that allocates a hotel's emissions to spa, pool, F&B and rooms separately from sub-metered data; the Irish and Beijing studies both state their data were whole-building (Attari & Hanrahan 2026; Morales et al. 2026). The nearest thing to a service-level footprint is a 2026 spa-visit calculator reporting about 5 kgCO2e per visit at a large wellness resort and about 3.5 at a small facility, read only from the publisher record (Communications Sustainability 2026, DOI 10.1038/s44458-026-00122-x), and the Dolder Grand's per-restaurant-guest and per-event-guest figures discussed below.

National survey data give the structure an activity-based split needs. CBECS 2018 puts US lodging site energy at 85.7 kBtu/ft2, with water heating (16.5 kBtu/ft2, about 20%) and space heating (15.7) the largest end uses, then cooking 14.1, ventilation 12.5, lighting 7.2, cooling 6.0 and refrigeration 4.2 kBtu/ft2 (EIA CBECS 2018 Table E2); water heating, cooking and refrigeration together are about 41% of lodging site energy and are the end uses most tied to F&B and laundry rather than to the guestroom. CBECS lodging electricity of 14.4 kWh/ft2 plus 37 cubic feet of gas per ft2 is about 280 kWh/m2 of site energy, which matches the CHSB US full-service median of 264 kWh/m2 and is bracketed by limited service (210) and luxury (301) (EIA Table C14; EIA Table C24; CHSB 2025 Excel). CBECS does not separate hotels by class, and its lodging category mixes hotels, motels, dormitories and nursing homes.

Food and drink outweigh the building once Scope 3 is counted, and the share depends on the grid

The single most important finding of the review is that the two inventories with a full Scope 3 boundary agree that purchased food and beverages are the largest category at a full-service hotel, and that this is invisible in every HCMI, CHSB and corporate figure. The Dolder Grand, a 5-star urban hotel in Zurich reporting under the GHG Protocol with myclimate factors on ecoinvent, attributes 1,713.9 of 3,541.9 tCO2e (48%) to food and beverages in 2023, 52% in 2024 and 2,257.7 of 4,047.5 t (56%) in 2025, with Scope 3 about 90% of the total, heating and cooling 11–12%, employee commuting 12–15%, external laundry 6–7%, waste 3–4% and purchased electricity 3–4%; per overnight stay the hotel reports 39.2, 39.3 and 38.1 kgCO2e, per restaurant guest 5.9, 5.4 and 6.1 kgCO2e, and per event guest 8.0, 7.8 and then 19.7 kgCO2e (Dolder Grand Carbon Footprint 2023; 2024; 2025). The report says its data were "checked for completeness and plausibility, but have not been audited", guest travel is excluded, and the 2025 jump in the per-event-guest figure coincides with a change to ecoinvent 3.10 and IPCC 2021 GWP100, so the 2023–24 and 2025 event values should not be averaged. The Irish preprint reaches the same conclusion with a different method: across a 3-star, two 4-star and a 5-star hotel (the last with spa, pool, gym and LPG heating), per-room-night footprints are 15.87, 40.30, 47.15 and 56.14 kgCO2e (mean 39.87), per guest-night 8.81–24.47, and food procurement is 43.2%, 57.7%, 43.1% and 39.7% of the total; Scope 1+2 alone gives 9.40–32.32 kgCO2e per room-night, so adding food, waste and recycling raises the figure by 70–130% (Attari & Hanrahan 2026). That document is a Research Square preprint under review, not peer-reviewed, and its Table 6 misquotes Puig 2017 as 9–14 kgCO2e where the primary text gives 12, 22 and 23.

The group-level disclosures put F&B far lower, and the difference is the electricity factor rather than a contradiction. Accor's 2024 universal registration document, as summarised by a French consultancy, puts energy at 65% of group emissions (4,282 kt) and catering and drinks at 19% (1,229 kt), with the same source noting that in a French hotel on low-carbon electricity "energy weighs less and catering more" (Projet Celsius citing Accor URD 2024). Zurich's hydro-nuclear grid gives the Dolder a purchased-electricity line of only 3–4%, so F&B dominates arithmetically; on New York's grid, where eGRID2023 puts the NYCW subregion at 864 lb CO2/MWh (EPA eGRID summary data), the energy share will be higher and the F&B share correspondingly lower. The paper should therefore report the F&B share together with the electricity factor used. Sector-level, the Sustainable Hospitality Alliance and Systemiq estimate hotel food systems at 185 MtCO2e per year, split 62% food sourcing (116 Mt), 19% kitchen operations (35 Mt), 17% food waste (32 Mt) and 2% packaging, with "a typical hotel meal" estimated at "three times higher than the global average", and kitchen energy "generally 15–25% of a hotel's overall energy use" on the explicit basis of interviews because "reliable industry wide data is not currently available" (SHA, Decarbonizing Hotel Food Systems, Dec 2024). Accor reports that meat is 3% of food and drink purchases by weight but 27% of their carbon footprint (Reuters Events). Lenzen et al.'s global tourism footprint, read in its open supplement, gives accommodation 282 MtCO2e (6.37%) and food and beverage serving 227 Mt (5.11%) of 4.5 Gt in 2013, with an author correction revising air travel to 12% (Lenzen et al. 2018 SI; Author Correction).

Per-meal evidence brackets a luxury cover between roughly 2 and 10 kgCO2e depending on the main. Poore and Nemecek's global means, as published by Our World in Data (2010 data, kgCO2e per kg), are beef-herd beef 99.48, dairy-herd beef 33.3, lamb 39.72, cheese 23.88, farmed prawns 26.87, pork 12.31, poultry 9.87, eggs 4.67, coffee 28.53, wine 1.79 and vegetables about 0.5 (OWID grapher); Clune, Crossin and Verghese's systematic-review medians are markedly lower for ruminants (beef 28.73, range 10.74–109.5; lamb 27.91; cheese 8.86; chicken 4.12), a divergence driven by land-use change and herd averaging that must be stated when a factor set is chosen, and the Clune values here were transcribed from a Wikimedia rendering of Table 4 that should be verified against the paper (Clune et al. 2017 via Wikimedia Commons). The OWID explainer text also quotes "60 kg" for beef, which does not match the 99.48 beef-herd value in its own dataset (OWID, Environmental impacts of food). The WRI Cool Food Meals badge sets thresholds of 3.59 kgCO2e per breakfast portion and 5.38 per lunch or dinner portion including land-use carbon opportunity cost (WRI, Oct 2020); Accor's 2023 averages are 1.66–6.57 kgCO2e per meal (Projet Celsius); a Danish meal-service LCA gives an average main of 3.70 kgCO2e with beef mains 8–11 times a vegetarian main (Int. J. LCA 2018); and the French ADEME labelling pre-study puts a 5-star night at 17.11 kgCO2e with breakfast 30% of the per-night footprint (ADEME via Nos Gestes Climat). Filimonau's verified breakfast figure of 1.3 kgCO2e per guest-night at a UK 3-star hotel is the only peer-reviewed per-breakfast number in the set (Filimonau et al. 2011). The Dolder's 5.4–6.1 kg per restaurant guest and Accor's upper bound of 6.57 kg per meal are the most defensible anchors for a luxury cover; a dinner with a beef main, cheese course and wine will exceed the Cool Food threshold by a wide margin on any of the factor sets.

Kitchen energy is smaller than the embodied footprint of the food but not negligible. Mudie et al. (2016) metered 14 English pub-restaurant kitchens in November 2012 at 294 kWh of electricity per day per kitchen and 1.52–3.32 kWh per meal, with refrigeration 41% of kitchen electricity and dishwashing, extraction and lighting excluded; the paper is in the International Journal of Low-Carbon Technologies, not Energy and Buildings as sometimes cited (Mudie et al. 2016). A UK benchmark re-study found real kitchen use 66% above published kWh-per-meal benchmarks (BSER&T 2016), and a Portuguese institutional kitchen audit used 1.28 kWh electricity plus 1.67 kWh gas per meal (Coimbra thesis). The US ENERGY STAR restaurant split is food preparation 35%, HVAC 28%, sanitation 18%, lighting 13% and refrigeration 6% (ENERGY STAR kitchen guide 2014); the 41% versus 6% refrigeration shares are both correct for their boundaries (kitchen-only overnight base load versus whole restaurant). No hotel-kitchen kWh-per-cover study exists; on these figures 3–5 kWh of delivered energy per cover, or roughly 0.5–1.5 kgCO2e depending on grid and gas share, is the inferred hotel range, below the food's embodied footprint but comparable to the guestroom plug load for a night.

Food waste adds a second F&B term that is easy to double count. Measured hotel waste clusters at 0.3–0.5 kg per cover: Ireland's EPA cites about 360 g per cover for daily service and 530 g for functions (fact-sheet PDFs returned 404; figures from search summaries and therefore unverified) (EPA Ireland daily sheet); a five-site Malaysian study including a restaurant inside a five-star hotel and a high-end banquet hall averaged 0.53 kg per customer, with the banquet hall preparing 30% more food than required as policy (Papargyropoulou et al. 2019); a Maltese five-star plate-waste audit gave buffet dinner 0.48 kg per person against à la carte 0.16–0.21 (Camilleri-Fenech et al. 2020); and a Slovenian four-star breakfast buffet (breakfast included in the rate) produced only 14.3 g of plate waste per person per day, rising from 1.8 g in the first hour to 31.8 g in the last (Juvan et al. 2021). The Champions 12.3 study of 42 hotel sites in 15 countries (21 luxury) found a 21% reduction in pre-consumer waste within a year at a 6.8:1 benefit-cost ratio (Champions 12.3 / WRAP 2018). Emission factors differ by boundary: UK DEFRA landfill disposal is about 627 kgCO2e per tonne and composting about 9 (Climatiq mirror of DEFRA), whereas the widely used cradle-to-grave figure of 1.9 kgCO2e per kg (BIO Intelligence 2010) and the approximately 4.35 kg/kg implied by Hilton's Green Ramadan results (7.4 tCO2e per 1.7 t of food, own arithmetic) include the embodied emissions of the wasted food, which already sit in purchased goods (Juvan et al. 2021; Hospitality Net, June 2024). The SHA's 17% food-waste share also carries embodied emissions.

Beverages, minibars and banqueting round out the F&B bucket. Beer is 510–842 gCO2e per litre in the UK depending on packaging (glass bottle worst, steel can best) (Amienyo & Azapagic 2016, EPA HERO record); wine is 0.9–2.0 kgCO2e per 0.75 L bottle across European studies with packaging and distribution the larger levers (University of Perugia chapter); Point, Tyedmers and Naugler (2012, third author corrected from "Theodore") find viticulture and consumer transport dominant for a Nova Scotia bottle but their abstract carries no kgCO2e value (Point et al. 2012); single-malt Scotch is 2.83 kgCO2e per 70 cl bottle in a 2025 calculator study with Scope 3 at 77% (J. Inst. Brewing 2025). Cocktail figures of 152–690 gCO2e per drink come from a catering supplier's marketing study and should be labelled as such (Morning Advertiser 2022). Per serve, a 125 ml glass of wine is 0.15–0.33 kgCO2e, a bottled beer 0.17–0.28 and a 50 ml spirit pour about 0.2 kgCO2e (own conversions), while ice at 3.6–5.6 kWh per 100 lb and dishwashing at 0.17–0.61 kWh per rack add a few grams each (ENERGY STAR ice machine listing; ENERGY STAR dishwasher listing). Absorption minibars of the quiet type still common in luxury rooms draw 0.53–0.70 kWh per 24 h (EN 62552-3 ratings), about 190–265 kWh per year, compressor units roughly a third of that, and about 70% of travellers never use one, so energy per transaction is several kWh (Dometic HiPro Evolution A40G; Tefcold catalogue 2025; CNN); on typical grids that is 0.1–0.3 kgCO2e per occupied room-night. For banqueting, the Dolder's 7.8–8 kgCO2e per event guest (2023–24) is the only full-boundary figure; Hilton's "Meet with Purpose" calculator publishes no per-attendee factor, and a Hilton Cardiff marketing sheet quoting 8 kgCO2 per person per full day of meals and 2 kgCO2 per m2 of meeting space per hour states no method (Hilton Meet with Purpose; Hilton Cardiff sheet). CHSB's HCMI meetings measure for NYC luxury is a median 0.0457 kgCO2e per m2 of meeting space per hour, which is an area-apportioned building number, not a catering footprint (CHSB 2025 Excel).

Heated water, not treatment rooms or gyms, is the wellness amenity that moves the hotel total

The wellness evidence divides sharply into wet areas, where the loads are large and the literature is confused about units, and dry areas, where the loads are small and the literature is thin. The pool-energy conflict in earlier notes is resolved in file 07. Kampel's NTNU thesis, which read Trianti-Stourna et al. (1998) directly, states that those authors "describe the FAEC for swimming facilities located in Mediterranean climate with 4300 kWh/m2 WS while it is about 5200 kWh/m2 WS for facilities located in continental climate" and adds that "there is no indication about where these numbers originate from", whereas the five Greek facilities actually audited averaged 450.1 kWh per m2 of usable floor area with a water-surface-to-floor ratio of 3.43, about 1,540 kWh per m2 of water surface (Kampel PhD thesis, NTNU 2015, Wayback copy; Trianti-Stourna et al. 1998). The 1,094.5 kWh/m2 figure attributed to the same paper by another secondary source was not found in any accessible text and remains unverified. Measured Norwegian facilities span roughly 1,000 to 11,000 kWh per m2 of water surface with a 28% saving potential (Kampel et al. 2013), and Kampel's 2016 benchmarking paper finds that "climate does not drive the total energy performance of swimming facilities", that water usage and visitor numbers correlate most strongly, and that energy "shall be preferably normalised with respect to the number of visitors" (Kampel et al. 2016). The kWh-per-visitor statistics (mean 26, median about 22, range 10–80) attributed to Kampel via a 2021 review are not in the 2016 abstract and were not located in the thesis (Smedegård et al. 2021). The UK CIBSE TM46 benchmark gives a swimming pool centre 245 kWh/m2 electricity plus 1,130 kWh/m2 fossil per year of floor area against 105 + 330 for a hotel, taken from a secondary reproduction (GreenCalculus TM46 table).

The one hotel-specific metered pool dataset is Polish. Heat meters at the 51-room Hotel Turówka in Kraków recorded pool-area heating of 399.6 kWh/day (June) to 657.4 kWh/day (May) against domestic hot water of 299–1,574 kWh/day, with pool dimensions not given (Borowski et al., CLIMA 2022); if the May–June range held year-round, the pool alone would add 32–52 kWh per m2 of hotel floor area, 7–12% of a TM46-typical hotel, before pumps, lighting and dehumidification electricity (own arithmetic, a lower bound because winter was not reported). Evaporation is consistently the dominant pool-water heat loss, "at least 60%" in the sources collated by a 2025 French modelling paper, which also found filtration pumps at 40% of its case-study demand (Benakcha et al. 2025, arXiv), and Sport England's design guidance (28–30°C water, 30°C hall air, 60% RH, 4–10 air changes, 40°C spa pools, 30 litres of make-up water per swimmer) supplies the physics for a bottom-up estimate (Sport England 2013 Appendix 2). Chemical carbon is small: Euro Chlor's cradle-to-gate factor is 0.90 kgCO2e per kg of chlorine and 0.93 per kg of sodium hypochlorite, so even several kilograms of active chlorine a day is two orders of magnitude below the heating load (Euro Chlor eco-profile). Among regression studies, the only one with a significant pool term is Santiago's six Gran Canaria hotels, where pool volume, revenue per available room, diners and guests per occupied room explained total energy demand and F&B exceeded half of energy (Santiago 2021); the Urban Green Council found that fitness centres in NYC hotels "did not have a measurable impact on their efficiency grades or carbon intensities" (Urban Green Council 2020). The widely repeated "up to 35% of total energy for additional services such as a spa" in Spanish hotels is unsourced on the page that carries it and almost certainly bundles heated pools with the spa (University of Thessaly project page).

Saunas, steam rooms and whirlpools have no measured hotel data anywhere the team reached. The usable inputs are nameplate ratings and duty-cycle statements: commercial sauna heaters of 9–20 kW (Harvia Club K10G–K15G, Harvia Virta Pro 20 kW, Tylö Commercial 20), Harvia's statement that a heater "stays on full power for the initial 30–40 minutes" then "about 50% of the maximum", a Dutch steam-room manufacturer's worked example of an 11.4 kW generator at 16 h/day consuming 36,516 kWh per year at 55% average load, and residential hot-tub averages of 2,514 kWh/yr (NSPI 2000) that are a floor, not an estimate, for a 40°C commercial whirlpool with agitation and dilution (Harvia; Cleopatra; Washington State factsheet; Home and Heat, Harvia Virta Pro). A 10 kW hotel sauna held hot 14 hours a day at 50% draw is about 25,600 kWh/yr (own arithmetic on Harvia's claim). Treatment rooms themselves are ordinary conditioned rooms plus a towel cabinet, a stone heater and a shower; the only metered hotel-spa electricity found is 7.24–11.22 kWh/m2/month at an airport hotel spa in Bali, a cooling-dominated student audit that is a lower bound for a New York spa with hydrotherapy (Politeknik Negeri Bali thesis). No ISPA, Global Wellness Institute or Spa Business benchmark of kWh per treatment or per m2 of spa exists in the public domain.

Fitness centres are a lighting and HVAC problem, not an equipment problem. CIBSE TM46 gives a fitness and health centre 160 kWh/m2 electricity plus 440 fossil per year (GreenCalculus TM46 table); the US ENERGY STAR "Recreation" group, which pools gyms with rinks and pools, has a median site EUI of 50.8 kBtu/ft2, about 160 kWh/m2 (ENERGY STAR National Median Table, Aug 2024). A metered 2 HP treadmill drew about 712 W at 10 mph, 96 W at 1 mph and 3.8 W idle (Ecocostsavings), so a 20-machine gym at 10% utilisation is about 7 kWh/day of plug load against roughly 24,000 kWh/yr of building energy for a 150 m2 room on the TM46 basis (own arithmetic). Energy-generating cardio equipment is a messaging measure: SportsArt claims up to 200–250 Wh per machine-hour, and a Bloomberg test with a 147 lb runner yielded 24 Wh over 30 minutes (Renewable Energy World).

The towel and robe laundry that pools, spas and gyms generate is the one treatment-specific driver that scales with throughput, and it can now be built from catalogue weights. Standard Textile's hospitality catalogue lists 27×54 inch bath towels at 15–17 lb per dozen (570–640 g each), hand towels at 3–4.5 lb per dozen (115–170 g) and bath mats at 7–10.5 lb per dozen (Standard Textile Express), so two bath towels and a hand towel are about 1.36 kg dry and a spa visit with a robe is roughly 2.5–3 kg (robe mass unsourced). TRSA's 2018 survey of 132 US member plants gives 1.47 gal/lb (12.3 L/kg) of water, 2,422 Btu/lb (1.57 kWh/kg) of in-plant energy and 0.413 lb CO2 per lb (0.41 kgCO2/kg) of textiles, though hotel and lodging were only 1.2% of the pounds sampled (TRSA 2018 Sustainability Report); UK trade benchmarks put commercial laundries at 1.6–1.7 kWh/kg typical and 0.8–1.0 attainable (Laundry & Cleaning News). A 0.6 kg towel at 1.6 kWh/kg is therefore about 1 kWh and about 0.4 kgCO2 (US fuel mix), an illustration of method rather than a sourced value.

Guestroom HVAC and hot water dominate the room itself; housekeeping, laundry, lifts and transfers are the measurable service lines

The only public sub-metered guestroom dataset is PNNL's 32-room sample in a 300-plus-room, 212,000 ft2 full-service hotel built in 1968 in the Washington DC area, metered October 2009 to January 2010. Whole-hotel daily electricity by end use was guestroom PTHPs 4,016 kWh, public-space resistance heat 2,804, lights 959, kitchen 997, AHU/ventilation 907, guestroom refrigerators 196, guestroom lights/TV/other 193, elevators 141 and laundry 44 kWh/day, with natural gas split 51% service hot water, about 25% kitchen and about 25% laundry (Placet et al., PNNL/ACEEE 2010). Divided over about 300 rooms (own arithmetic, available-room basis, winter), that is about 13 kWh per room-day of HVAC, 0.65 kWh of minibar refrigeration, 0.64 kWh of in-room lighting and plug loads, 0.47 kWh of elevators, 7.5 kWh thermal of hot water and 3.7 kWh thermal of laundry gas. The PNNL hotel is old, electrically heated and was running PTHPs in resistance mode because of a control fault, so its HVAC share is an upper bound for a modern luxury property; its 7% lighting share is likely a lower bound for luxury rooms with decorative lighting. Modelled PTAC energy per guest room at 63% occupancy is 2,806–3,217 kWh/yr in Baltimore, 2,112–2,517 in Fort Worth and 1,100–1,374 in Los Angeles (PNNL/DOE 2012, Table 6), i.e. 3–9 kWh per room-day. The same DOE demonstration in six Marriott-brand hotels found occupancy-based HVAC controls saved 10.8–26.5% (mean 18.4%) of guestroom HVAC energy, 167–589 kWh per room per year, with paybacks of 5.3–17.9 years, while two independent studies found savings not statistically significant or eroded by guest override; vendor claims of 33–50% rest on two to four rooms or uncontrolled comparisons (PNNL/DOE 2012, Tables 1, 7, 8; UC Davis WCEC). The notes record a decision to use the DOE figures over the vendor claims.

Hot water is physically bounded and luxury water use is well evidenced. EPA WaterSense gives 0.183 kWh per gallon heated through a 75°F rise, 0.048 kWh per litre before heater efficiency (EPA WaterSense at Work, Section 3.5), so a 250 L soaking tub is about 8.4 kWh of heat per fill and a 10-minute rain shower about 3.9 kWh (own arithmetic, fixture volumes from residential sources because no luxury-hotel fixture data exist). Total water per guest-night averages 419 L across 1,667 hotels in a 2025 systematic review that found quality level not significant (Schick & Redlein 2025), against a European frontrunner benchmark of 140 L (Styles et al. 2015), 594 L per overnight stay for five-star hotels in a 2021 review citing Ecologic 2007 (Ramazanova et al. 2021), 0.58 m3 per overnight stay at Spanish 5-star hotels in Puig's primary data (Puig et al. 2017) and 788 L per occupied room across Marriott's managed portfolio in 2022 (Marriott 2023 Serve 360 report). CHSB finds higher-rated hotels use more water per occupied room but not per square metre (CHSB 2023 report). No source separates hot from cold water for luxury guests.

Housekeeping and laundry line items rest on trade sources, corroborated by one hotel's own sign. Soiled linen runs 4.5–6.8 kg per occupied room-day in two trade sources, with an observed industry range of 8–55 lb and 12 lb per room cited for a full-service hotel with food service, pool and banquets (Hospitality Institute; Western State Design; Hotel Management, Jan–Feb 2021); HCMI's default for outsourced laundry is 5.12 kg per occupied room (HCMI v2.0, sec 5.3.3). At 6 kg and 1.65 kWh/kg this is about 10 kWh and 150 L per room-night for an on-premise washer-extractor laundry at 25 L/kg, or 6 kWh and 100 L at best practice (own arithmetic). The Sheraton Boston "Make a Green Choice" sign quantifies an opted-out night at 37.2 gallons of water, 0.19 kWh of electricity and 25,000 BTU (about 7.3 kWh) of gas, which corroborates the laundry estimate (Boston Globe 2018). Towel-reuse signage has modest, culturally bounded effects: Goldstein, Cialdini and Griskevicius's descriptive-norm cards lifted reuse from about 35% to 44–49% in a US hotel, as reported by a German replication that found German guests at 64–93% regardless of message; the percentages are not in the JCR abstract and come from the replication (Bohner & Schlüter 2014; Goldstein et al. 2008).

Elevators, toiletries, bottled water and transfers complete the service stack. Elevators were about 1% of electricity at the 12-storey PNNL hotel (PNNL/ACEEE 2010); CIBSE's worked examples give 0.038–0.27 kWh per cycle from a 630 kg geared lift to a 2,500 kg express car with 200–900 W standby, so a medium-usage hotel lift at 300 trips/day is about 47 kWh/day with standby a quarter of it (own arithmetic), and no high-rise luxury hotel measurement exists (Barney, CIBSE). Bottled water carries 5.6–10.2 MJ per litre dominated by PET manufacture for short-haul water, so two 500 mL bottles a night are 1.6–2.8 kWh of primary energy, on the order of the in-room plug load (Gleick & Cooley 2009). No independent LCA comparing miniature toiletries with dispensers exists; chain claims are plastic-mass based ("95% less plastic", one pump bottle equals 10–12 miniatures) (Anchorage Daily News 2019). Guest ground transport is absent from every hotel group's Scope 3, yet on DEFRA 2024 factors a luxury petrol car emits 0.319 kgCO2e/km, a luxury diesel 0.207 and a luxury PHEV 0.121, so a 40 km airport transfer is about 12.8 kgCO2e tailpipe in a petrol sedan and a return trip in such a car can exceed the room's Scope 1+2 footprint for the night (UK GHG Conversion Factors 2024); a hotel-owned fleet is Scope 1, a contracted limousine Scope 3 category 1 if the hotel pays. Butler and 24-hour services are labour, whose carbon is staff commuting (594,040 tCO2e at Marriott's non-franchised properties in 2022) allocated by staff-to-room ratios for which no luxury data exist (Marriott 2023 Serve 360 report).

Service line (per occupied room-night unless stated) Best available figure Unit, geography, year Basis and status
Whole room-night, energy only 28.4 (IQR 24–38) kgCO2e, NYC luxury, CY2023 CHSB/HCMI, location-based (CHSB 2025)
Whole room-night, full Scope 1–3 56.1 (5-star), 15.9–47.2 (3–4 star) kgCO2e, Ireland, 2026 preprint Not peer-reviewed (Attari & Hanrahan)
Whole stay, full Scope 1–3 38.1–39.3 kgCO2e per overnight stay, Zurich, 2023–25 Company inventory, unaudited (Dolder Grand)

Airport transfer, 40 km one way 4.8–12.8 kgCO2e, luxury PHEV to petrol DEFRA 2024 factors, own arithmetic (DEFRA 2024)

These rows are not additive: they come from different boundaries, climates, segments and years, and the table exists to show the order of magnitude of each line and the quality of the evidence behind it.

The Waldorf's public record shows a gas-fired flagship with 2,700 ft2 per key and no disclosed energy strategy

The renovated Waldorf Astoria New York is, on the public record, a 1.6–1.67 million ft2, 47-storey landmark reopened in phases from 15 July 2025 with 375 guest rooms (most over 570 ft2), 372 condominium residences on floors 19–52, 43,000 ft2 of event space, three F&B venues and a 7,000 ft2 fitness centre, priced from $1,500 a night (The Points Guy, 15 Jul 2025; SOM project page; Hilton Stories, 2 Dec 2024). Before closure on 1 March 2017 it had 1,413 rooms in 1.6 million ft2 (LL84 CY2010 filing). The Guerlain spa opened in October 2025 at 22,000 ft2 with 16 treatment rooms, cryotherapy, infrared sauna, steam room, hammam and snow cave per the trade press, 20,000 ft2 per Hilton, and 30,000 ft2 in a pre-opening projection that should not be used (Spa Business, 22 Oct 2025; Hilton Stories, 2 Dec 2024). No 2025 source documents a hotel-guest pool; the 25 m Starlight Pool sits within 50,000 ft2 of residents-only amenities on the 25th floor (Douglas Elliman; SOM), so the paper's pool module applies to the residences, not the guest-night, unless hotel access is confirmed.

The energy system has not been announced by the owner, Hilton or SOM, but the permit record describes it. The NYC DEP certificate to operate CW006321 (issued 22 January 2025) lists eight Viessmann Vitocrossal 300 CA3B 6.0 natural-gas condensing boilers on the 7th floor; DOB NOW jobs filed by Guth DeConzo Consulting Engineers cover the boilers ($800,000), their flues and piping ($1.1 million) and "INSTALLATION OF CHP UNITS AND VENTILATION SYSTEMS IN THE CHP ROOM ON THE 6TH FLOOR" ($2.6 million); AKRF advised on the exhaust stack of a "proposed 555 kilowatt (kW) combined heating and power plant"; SOM's 2017 Landmarks presentation shows a Level-20 cooling tower well; and Stantec filings describe four-pipe fan coils (NYC DEP CATS permits; DOB NOW Build filings; AKRF project page; LPC presentation, 25 Apr 2017). Pre-closure, Con Edison district steam supplied 105–133 billion Btu a year, 53–59% of site energy; the CY2024 LL84 filing records zero district steam, 10.74 million kWh of electricity and 95,031 therms of estimated gas, with gas ramping from zero in January–April to 6.16 million kBtu in December 2024 as the boilers came online (LL84 CY2016; LL84 2023–present, BBL 1013047501; LL84 monthly data). The inference that the building has left the steam system is strong but unannounced, and the CY2024 record is a construction year (site EUI 27.7, ENERGY STAR 97, estimated values) that the notes decided to reject as performance data. The whole lot is benchmarked as a single "Hotel" property, so LL84 and LL97 figures will combine hotel and residential energy unless the owner later splits the filing, which the paper must state as a boundary problem. The choice of gas boilers and gas CHP matters for everything that follows: it places the hotel's heating in Hilton's Scope 1, which Hilton describes as "almost exclusively natural gas", and it forgoes LL97's beneficial-electrification credits.

Pre-closure, the Waldorf was typical of its peer group rather than an outlier: site EUI 125–141 kBtu/ft2, source EUI 255–273, ENERGY STAR 23–30, 15,435–17,916 tCO2e per year (9.6–11.1 kgCO2e/ft2) and 77–90 gallons of water per ft2 in CY2013–2016 (LL84 CY2013–2016 datasets). In CY2024 the median NYC hotel had a site EUI of 86.4 kBtu/ft2 and 7.31 kgCO2e/ft2 (Manhattan 94.2 and 8.15), while eleven traceable Manhattan luxury hotels cluster at 110–195 kBtu/ft2 and 7–17 kgCO2e/ft2 with a median of about 135 and 11.3, pre-war steam-heated properties (St. Regis 11.8–12.1, Peninsula 12.1, Carlyle 12.6, Pierre 12.1, Aman 16.9 kgCO2e/ft2) sitting well above post-1990 towers (Four Seasons 57th 6.9, Ritz-Carlton Central Park 7.9) (NYC Open Data LL84 2023–present). These Portfolio Manager intensities use eGRID electricity factors (NYCW about 0.39 kgCO2e/kWh), about 36% above LL97's prescribed 0.289, so they overstate LL97-basis emissions for electricity-heavy hotels. Floor area per key is the structural reason the Waldorf's per-room-night footprint will be high whatever its efficiency: if even 60% of 1.67 million ft2 is hotel, that is about 2,700 ft2 per key, against about 1,800 ft2 per key at the St. Regis on the notes' 427,877 ft2 and 238-key figures (key count unverified). Manhattan's luxury class ran 76.4% occupancy and $490.75 ADR in the first half of 2025 (PwC Manhattan Lodging Index H1 2025); at that occupancy the hotel sells about 104,600 occupied room-nights a year, and a hotel portion of 1.0 million ft2 at the peer-median 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 would be about 11,300 tCO2e, or roughly 108 kgCO2e per occupied room-night on an energy-only, Portfolio Manager basis, nearly four times the CHSB NYC luxury median. That is a bounding illustration from stated assumptions, not a forecast: the hotel-only GFA, the hotel's actual energy use and the condominium split are all unpublished. No per-room-night carbon figure exists for any Manhattan hotel, so the paper's estimate will be the first of its kind and LL84 intensities per square foot are its only external check.

Hilton's validated targets are absolute, and its disclosures cannot show progress against them

Earlier notes described Hilton's science-based targets as intensity-only; file 08 corrects this from the SBTi's own dashboard. The SBTi company export (24 October 2026) records: "Hilton commits to reduce absolute scope 1 and 2 GHG emissions 46.2% by 2030 from a 2019 base year. Hilton also commits to reduce absolute scope 3 GHG emissions from franchises 27.5% within the same timeframe", validated at 1.5°C, with the long-term, net-zero status and net-zero year fields blank (SBTi Target Dashboard export). Hilton's own reporting uses a different frame: "Reduce Scope 1 and 2 carbon emissions intensity from managed hotels by 75% (MT CO2e/m²)" and "Reduce Scope 3 carbon emissions intensity from franchised hotels by 56% (MT CO2e/m²)" from a 2008 baseline, reported as 50.9% and 36.0% achieved by 2025 (Hilton 2025 Travel with Purpose Report; Hilton 2025 Performance Tables). Hilton's 2023 CDP response, now reachable only through the Wayback Machine, states that the absolute 46.2% commitment "is equivalent to a scope 1 & 2 GHG emissions intensity reduction of 74.8% per square foot by 2030 across our managed estate, and a 55.5% intensity reduction per square foot across our franchised estate", and reports 5.14% of the absolute Scope 1+2 target achieved by 2022, with franchise emissions 12.7% above the 2019 base of 3,884,715 tCO2e (Hilton CDP 2023, Wayback). The 2019 base-year Scope 1 and 2 values are blank in CDP and absent from the performance tables, so progress against the validated target cannot be computed from Hilton's disclosures.

The absolute series moves the wrong way for the managed estate. Market-based managed Scope 1+2 emissions were 2,229,587 tCO2e in 2008, 2,350,694 in 2022, 2,570,111 in 2023, 2,504,014 in 2024 and 2,443,342 in 2025, while intensity fell from 0.1567 to 0.0769 tCO2e/m2 because managed floor area roughly doubled (implied 14.2 to 31.8 million m2); franchised Scope 3 rose from 2,180,912 to 4,011,353 tCO2e (Hilton 2025 Performance Tables). Since 2022, the year CDP reported as 5.1% of the way to the 2030 target, absolute managed emissions have risen by about 4%. Scope 1 at managed hotels is "almost exclusively natural gas for comfort heating and hot water generation" and Scope 2 is grid electricity at managed properties, assured by DEKRA to ISO 14064-3 at limited assurance and 5% materiality (Hilton 2024 Assurance Statement). Renewable procurement is small: the gap between location- and market-based Scope 2 in 2025 was 118,530 tCO2e, about 6%, and the roughly 152 million kWh of renewable electricity reported for US managed hotels is about 2% of the 7.53 million MWh of managed energy (Hilton 2025 Travel with Purpose Report). Hilton reports no carbon credits against its targets; the only credit activity is 550 Gold Standard tonnes cancelled for voluntary offsetting in 2022 and the guest-facing Carbon Neutral Meetings product with ClimeCo at select hotels for meetings of ten or more rooms on peak (Hilton CDP 2023; Hilton Carbon Neutral Meetings). The FY2025 10-K mentions climate only as generic demand and weather risk and contains no occurrence of "Local Law 97", "building performance", "carbon", "greenhouse" or "SBTi" (Hilton 10-K FY2025). Food procurement sits in no Hilton target; a 2025 partnership with Watershed is to begin centralised supply-chain Scope 3 calculation (Hilton 2025 Travel with Purpose Report).

For the Waldorf this changes the question. Under an absolute 2019-based target, adding a 1.67 million ft2 gas-heated flagship to the managed estate adds absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions that must be offset by reductions elsewhere in the portfolio; under the intensity framing Hilton reports against, the property only needs its own per-square-metre intensity to be low. A guest-night comparison is possible on the intensity frame: Hilton's 2025 managed intensity of 0.0769 tCO2e/m2 (76.9 kgCO2e/m2, market-based, gross building area) compares with the CHSB NYC luxury median of 88.7 kgCO2e/m2 of conditioned space (location-based) and the Manhattan luxury peer median of about 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2, about 122 kgCO2e/m2 on the Portfolio Manager basis (Hilton 2025 Performance Tables; CHSB 2025 Excel; NYC Open Data LL84). Hilton's 2030 goal of a 75% cut from 0.1567 implies about 39 kgCO2e/m2 (own arithmetic), which is almost exactly LL97's 2030 ESPM hotel cap of 0.00385 tCO2e/ft2, about 41 kgCO2e/m2, although the two figures differ in area definition, scope and electricity factor. Both are roughly a third of where Manhattan's pre-war luxury hotels sit today.

The peer set shows that absolute, validated targets are the exception in luxury. Marriott has SBTi-validated absolute targets (−46.2% Scope 1+2 and −27.5% Scope 3 by 2030 vs 2019; net zero 2050 at −90%) but sourced 2.92% of energy from renewables in 2023 and measures progress market-based with European Guarantees of Origin (Marriott 2024 CDP response; Marriott 2024 Performance Tables). IHG's validated −46.2% absolute target covering franchises is self-declared off track, with total carbon up 7.7% since 2019 while carbon per available room fell 11.0% to 0.0258 tCO2e, about 25.8 kgCO2e per available room-night, market-based Scope 1+2 (IHG Annual Report 2025). Rosewood targets net zero by 2050 and −87% absolute Scope 1+2 by 2035 from 2023, "aligned with" but "not validated by" the SBTi, with Scope 3 "around 70%" of its profile (Rosewood Impact & Sustainability Report 2025); HSH/Peninsula reports 110 kgCO2e/m2 in 2024 and −33.6% absolute carbon since 2010 (HSH CRS Report 2024); Mandarin Oriental's per-m2 targets from 2012 coexist with rising absolute emissions as floor area more than doubled (Mandarin Oriental Sustainability Report 2023); Four Seasons publishes no group carbon total or percentage target, only LEED Gold for new builds and property-level incentive-linked targets (Four Seasons 2024 Progress Report, Wayback). None of these operators publishes carbon per room-night.

Local Law 97 binds the building's gas and grid emissions, and its 2030 cap is a third of today's luxury intensity

New York's Local Law 97 is the constraint that cannot be reframed. Hotels (occupancy group R-1) are capped at 0.00987 tCO2e per ft2 for 2024–2029, with the statute giving 0.00526 for 2030–2034 and the December 2023 rule assigning ENERGY STAR property types factors of 0.00385 (2030–34), 0.00264 (2035–39) and 0.00147 (2040–49) tCO2e/ft2 for "Hotel", mandatory for all owners from 2026; the penalty is the excess "multiplied by $268" per tonne per year (Local Law 97 of 2019; 1 RCNY 103-14). The prescribed coefficients are electricity 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh for 2024–29 and 0.000145 for 2030–34 (or hourly time-of-use), natural gas 0.00005311 tCO2e/kBtu (about 0.181 kgCO2e per kWh of fuel), and district steam 0.00004493 falling only to 0.0000432, a 3.9% relief against a roughly 50% drop for electricity (1 RCNY 103-14). RECs "may only be deducted from the emissions attributed to consumption of utility supplied electricity" and must come from a capacity resource located in or delivering into NYISO Zone J, retired by the owner for the same year; the only eligible sources are the Tier 4 lines, with the Champlain Hudson Power Express (1,250 MW, 10.4 TWh/yr, "equivalent to 20% of the City's energy needs") fully operational from June 2026 and Clean Path NY expected in 2027 (DOB REC policy, Sept 2023; DOB RECs FAQ; CHPE). Offsets are limited to the Affordable Housing Reinvestment Fund at $268 per tonne and "up to a maximum of 10 percent of a building's annual building emissions limit" (1 RCNY 103-14 (e)(3)). DOB's own break-even table shows RECs at $77/MWh equalling the penalty in 2024–29 but about $39/MWh after 2030, when "the cost of RECs is likely to exceed the cost of maximum penalties" (DOB REC policy). In year one, hotels were 604 of the Article 320 properties (5%) and had the lowest compliance rate of the major types at 91% (DOB LL97 Year One Results); on the Portfolio Manager basis 28% of Manhattan hotels exceeded 9.87 kgCO2e/ft2 and 90% exceeded 5.26 in CY2024 (NYC Open Data LL84).

Applied to a 1,666,763 ft2 "Hotel" filing, the annual cap is about 16,450 tCO2e for 2024–29, about 6,420 tCO2e for 2030–34 on the ESPM factor (8,770 on the statutory R-1 figure), and about 4,400 for 2035–39 (own arithmetic). The pre-closure Waldorf emitted 15,435–17,916 tCO2e per year on the Portfolio Manager basis; at the CY2024 luxury peer median of about 11.3 kgCO2e/ft2 the building would emit about 18,800 t, above even the current cap, and the 2030 shortfall of roughly 12,400 t would cost on the order of $3.3 million per year at $268/t. This is a bounding illustration: the renovated building's energy use is unknown, the Portfolio Manager factor overstates LL97 electricity emissions by about 36%, and a filing split into hotel and residential uses would apply different factors to the condominium floors (not researched). The 61% drop in the hotel cap between 2029 and 2030 is steeper than the halving of the electricity coefficient, so electrification alone does not guarantee compliance, and a gas-fired heating plant receives no coefficient relief at all. Con Edison's steam system, 99% gas-fired, targets net-zero Scope 1 for cogeneration by 2040 and all steam by 2050 and intends to seek a lower LL97 steam coefficient (Con Edison Steam Decarbonization Study); its 2018 delivered-steam footprint of 110 lb CO2 per Mlb brackets the LL97 coefficient, which is therefore not a concession (Con Edison 2018 Sustainability Report). The three electricity factors a New York paper must carry are compared below.

Electricity factor for NYC Value Basis Use in the paper
eGRID2023 NYCW total output rate 864.5 lb CO2/MWh ≈ 0.392 kgCO2e/kWh Measured 2023, location-based (EPA eGRID) CHSB/HCMI-comparable, Portfolio Manager, Hilton location-based
LL97 coefficient 2024–29 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh = 0.289 kg/kWh Prescribed (1 RCNY 103-14) Compliance basis; the notes' chosen primary factor

Downstate generation (NYISO Zones F–K) was about 10% zero-emission in 2024 against 89% upstate, and the state targets 70% renewable electricity by 2030 and 100% zero-emission by 2040 (NYISO 2025 Power Trends); CHPE's physical effect on the NYCW factor will not appear in eGRID until the 2026 data year, and it changes the LL97 coefficient only if DOB revises the rule.

A credible package is disclosure plus durable removals, not a "carbon-neutral stay"

The evidence on offsets points one way. West et al. (2023) found in Science that only 6.1% of credits from 18 REDD+ projects were linked to additional emission reductions and that "most projects did not significantly reduce deforestation", a finding Verra disputes on method (Mongabay summary of West et al. 2023; Verra fact check); Badgley et al. (2022) estimated 29.4% over-crediting (30.0 MtCO2e) in California's forest offsets (Badgley et al., bioRxiv); Coffield et al. (2022) found carbon gains on 37 improved-forest-management projects "generally not additional" (Coffield et al. 2022); and Haya et al. (2023) found IFM protocols "deviate from scientific understanding" on baselines, leakage and reversal (Haya et al. 2023). The ICVCM has given its Core Carbon Principles label to only three new REDD+ methodologies and excluded the legacy Verra methodologies that "account for the majority of REDD+ credits on the market" (ICVCM, Nov 2024). The Oxford Principles (2024) state that "most offsetting today is still not net zero aligned" and call for 100% durable removals by the net-zero date (Oxford Principles 2024). Durable removals cost around $180/t on average against about $35/t for forestry and a roughly $6 spot price for generic credits, with direct air capture offtakes near a $650/t median and biochar $50–150/t (ESG Post citing CDR.fyi, Jul 2025; Fastmarkets). For a luxury guest-night of 50–150 kgCO2e, durable removal at $180/t is $9–27 a night, 1–2% of a $1,500 rate (inference from the cited prices).

The accounting rules leave credits no role in meeting targets. SBTi's Corporate Net-Zero Standard V1.3.1, applicable through 2026, says carbon credits "do not count as reductions toward meeting near-term or long-term science-based targets" and may only neutralise residual emissions at net zero or finance mitigation beyond the value chain (SBTi V1.3.1). V2.0, published 11 June 2026 and mandatory from 31 January 2028, keeps that line, introduces a voluntary "Ongoing Emissions Responsibility" ladder (1%, 10% at $20/t, 100% at $80/t of ongoing emissions) that becomes mandatory for large companies from 2035, requires neutralisation of long-lived residuals with long-lived removals, and tightens market-based Scope 2 to same-deliverability-region contracts with plants no older than 15 years and hourly-matching thresholds of 50% to 2030, 75% to 2035 and 90% thereafter (SBTi V2.0). The GHG Protocol's own Scope 2 revision, consulted from October 2025 with a final text expected in 2027, proposes hourly matching and deliverability for contractual instruments (GHG Protocol blog). Zone J-deliverable CHPE RECs are therefore the one instrument that satisfies LL97, SBTi V2 deliverability and the GHG Protocol direction simultaneously; unbundled national RECs of the kind Hyatt cites for 14 US hotels are the instrument most at risk (Hyatt, 31 Jul 2023).

The legal risk of neutrality claims is now concrete and sits precisely on the amenity boundary. Directive (EU) 2024/825, applying from 27 September 2026, bans as a per se unfair practice claims that a product has a neutral, reduced or positive environmental impact based on offsetting outside the value chain, while permitting communication about credit investments if not presented as compensation (TLT summary of Directive 2024/825); the EUR-Lex text could not be fetched, so the Annex wording rests on legal summaries. The Amsterdam District Court found 15 of 19 KLM statements misleading (20 March 2024) (Clyde & Co); the German Federal Court of Justice (27 June 2024, I ZR 98/23) held that "klimaneutral" may be used only if the advertisement itself says whether emissions are avoided or offset (SKW Schwarz); and in the case directly on point, Landgericht Berlin II prohibited a Best Western hotel's "climate neutral" stay claims because its calculation covered electricity and gas but "did not adequately account for other relevant emissions sources, including waste disposal and laundry services" (Climate Case Chart, DUH v Best Western). Booking.com withdrew its "Travel Sustainable" badge in March 2024 after the Dutch regulator objected (PhocusWire); the UK CMA can fine up to 10% of global turnover since April 2025 and the ASA banned "eco hotel" wording in September 2026 (ITV News); the US FTC Green Guides remain at their 2012 revision (FTC Green Guides). Populus in Denver, self-described as the first "carbon positive" US hotel, saw 80% of its offset-planted spruce seedlings die in the first year (Dwell, Jan 2025).

What does count is physical reduction and demand-side design, and both have evidence. Willingness to pay is positive but small: 72.7% of surveyed tourists were willing to pay more for a local green certification, with a lower-bound mean of about US$1.55 per night (Nelson et al. 2021); a hedonic study found a $9–26 green-certification premium for a Virginia standard room (Kuminoff et al. 2010); a 2023 review puts the acceptable premium at about 5% (Damigos 2023); luxury and mid-priced guests are more willing than economy guests (Kang et al. 2012); and revealed opt-in behaviour is poor, with Expedia's CEO saying travellers choose the cheaper option "the overwhelming majority of the time" (Fortune, Oct 2023). On a $1,500 rate a 1–5% levy is $15–75 a night, which comfortably funds durable removal of a full-scope guest-night at current prices, but a mandatory levy must be marketed as a contribution, not neutrality. Menu carbon labels shift choices modestly: a Swedish restaurant trial raised low-emission dish sales 11.5% and cut emissions 3.6% (Brunner et al. 2018, via Cambridge BPP), canteen trials show 1.5–5% emission cuts with some null results, and consumers underestimate food emissions far more than appliance emissions but respond to labels (Camilleri et al. 2019). Given that F&B is 40–58% of a full-scope night and beef mains carry 8–11 times a vegetarian main, menu design, banquet default menus and procurement (Accor's meat at 3% of weight and 27% of food carbon) are the largest levers a luxury hotel controls without touching the building. Hilton already has the disclosure infrastructure: LightStay "enables hotels to report the environmental impact of stays" and 76,000-plus meetings used the Meeting Impact Calculator in 2025 (Hilton 2025 Travel with Purpose Report). The package that no source contradicts is a published per-stay footprint with a boundary that includes food, laundry and waste (the omissions that sank the Best Western claim), physical reductions and Zone J RECs that count under LL97 and SBTi, durable removals disclosed as a contribution with registry retirements, and demand-side measures (opt-in housekeeping, menu labelling, low-carbon banquet defaults) whose effect sizes are stated honestly as low single digits.

Methods recommendations for the paper

The functional unit should be one occupied room-night, reported alongside one guest-night using a measured or stated guests-per-room ratio (1.80–1.92 in full-service hotels per Attari & Hanrahan 2026), and alongside kgCO2e per m2 so the result can be checked against CHSB, LL84 and Hilton's intensity metric; Xuchao et al. (2010) is the peer-reviewed authority for treating these denominators as non-interchangeable (Xuchao et al. 2010). Occupancy must be an explicit input, not folded into a segment multiplier, because Puig's 2-star hotels at 30% occupancy out-emit 5-star hotels at 50% and the meta-analytic occupancy correlation is non-significant and negative for upscale (Puig et al. 2017; Arenhart et al. 2024); for Manhattan luxury the 76.4% H1 2025 class occupancy is more defensible than the all-class 82% (PwC H1 2025).

For allocation the notes record a decision for dual reporting: an activity-based attribution layered on an HCMI-comparable baseline, rather than a stand-alone LCA. Following the Product Standard hierarchy, the paper should first subdivide the hotel into service processes using metered or engineering end-use estimates structured on CBECS and ENERGY STAR end uses, then allocate each process by a physical key — guestroom HVAC, lighting and hot water to occupied room-nights; kitchens, dining and embodied food to covers; laundry to kilograms of linen per occupied room (HCMI's 5.12 kg default or measured); meeting space to m2-hours as HCMI does; pools and thermal suites to bathers or visits following Kampel's finding that visitor numbers and water use, not climate, drive pool energy (Kampel et al. 2016); spa treatments to treatment-hours plus linen per treatment; and truly shared loads (lobby, corridors, back of house, elevators, staff commuting) by floor area or guest-nights, using revenue shares only as a last resort. For wellness amenities the notes' decision is explicit: bottom-up sub-metered or engineering attribution beats both per-m2 water-surface benchmarks, whose published range is 1,000–11,000 kWh/m2 and whose most-quoted values are unsourced, and regression marginal effects, which mostly lack an amenity term because CBECS does not record pool area. The HCMI-comparable figure should always be printed beside the activity-based one so that the amenity-inclusive result can be benchmarked against the CHSB NYC luxury IQR of 24–38 kgCO2e per occupied room.

For grid factors the notes' decision is to use the LL97 prescribed coefficient (0.289 kgCO2e/kWh for 2024–29, 0.145 for 2030–34) as the primary electricity factor, with eGRID2023 NYCW (0.392) as a location-based sensitivity, and to reject marginal and time-of-use factors for the footprint total (1 RCNY 103-14; EPA eGRID). Because the two differ by 36% and the hotel is electricity-heavy, both columns must appear in every table, and a market-based column should be added if a Zone J REC or contract is assumed. Gas should use the LL97 0.05311 tCO2e/kBtu coefficient for compliance scenarios and a DEFRA or EPA factor for the GHG Protocol inventory; the Waldorf's gas boilers and CHP make Scope 1 a material line for the first time since the steam era. Refrigerants should use measured top-up rather than HCMI's 1% uplift where possible.

For food the notes' decision is to carry the embodied emissions of wasted food inside the purchases bucket and apply disposal-only waste factors (DEFRA about 627 kgCO2e/t to landfill, about 9 to compost, with an EPA WARM v16 check), rather than a cradle-to-grave 1.9 or 4.35 kg/kg factor that double counts with Scope 3 purchases. The factor set for purchased food must be named and its boundary stated, because Poore & Nemecek and Clune et al. differ by a factor of 3.5 on beef; a sensitivity on the two is warranted, and the Irish inventory's choice of Agribalyse and CONCITO factors (beef 16.0 kg/kg) is a third defensible option (Attari & Hanrahan 2026). Breakfast-inclusive rates should be modelled as covers served buffet-style, the format with the highest measured waste (0.48 kg per person at a Maltese five-star buffet dinner against 0.16–0.21 à la carte), and reported separately from the room so that the ADEME finding that breakfast is 30% of a French hotel night can be tested rather than assumed (Camilleri-Fenech et al. 2020; ADEME via Nos Gestes Climat). Kitchen energy should be allocated per cover from measured kWh/meal (Mudie's 1.5–3.3 kWh electricity plus a gas share) and the F&B share reported with the electricity factor alongside, since the Dolder (48–56%) and Accor (19%) shares are reconciled by grid intensity, not by error.

For the Waldorf specifically, the notes decided to benchmark the renovated building against the CY2024 Manhattan luxury peer set, to keep the CY2013–2016 filings only as labelled history, and to reject the CY2024 filing as performance data. The hotel-versus-residential boundary must be stated, the energy system should be modelled as the permitted gas-boiler-plus-CHP plant with an electrified counterfactual, and the first operating-year LL84 record (CY2026, disclosed 2027) should be flagged as the validation point.

Gaps the paper must state as future work

No published source gives measured energy for a commercial hotel sauna, steam room or whirlpool, a spa kWh-per-treatment or kWh-per-m2 benchmark, or a hotel pool's end-use split between water heating, hall heating, dehumidification, pumps and lighting; the only hotel pool metering (Kraków) omits pool size and winter months. No peer-reviewed hotel-kitchen kWh-per-cover study, banquet or room-service footprint, or per-attendee catering factor exists, and the Dolder's F&B sub-categories are locked in a chart. No source quantifies linen kilograms per spa treatment, robe mass, luxury linen per occupied room, hot water as a share of luxury guest water, or luxury fixture volumes. No hotel group quantifies guest ground transport or valet parking; no measured elevator energy exists for a high-rise luxury hotel; no independent LCA compares miniature toiletries with dispensers. No controlled trial has measured breakfast-included versus room-only rates, amenity carbon labels, low-carbon room rates or opt-in offset uptake at any hotel. Bibliographically, Deng & Burnett's 564 kWh/m2 is known only through citing papers, Bohdanowicz & Martinac's per-guest-night figures were never read, Rosselló-Batle's 20–31 kgCO2e per guest-night comes via Attari, Goldstein's reuse percentages come via a replication, Point et al.'s per-bottle value is unretrieved, and the 1,094.5 kWh/m2 pool figure remains unlocated. For the Waldorf, the hotel-only floor area, chiller specification, CHP commissioning status, Con Edison steam termination, any LEED or LL97 statement, and Hilton's 2019 absolute base-year emissions are all unpublished, and Hilton's 2024–2025 CDP responses and CDP score are not public. Several figures carried in this review are search-snippet or secondary only and are so labelled: EPA Ireland's per-cover waste, Radisson's "50–60% of Scope 3 from F&B", the Spanish "35% for spa" claim, Lu et al.'s 30.19 kWh per guest-night, Kampel's kWh-per-visitor statistics, the GHG Protocol allocation wording and the EU directive's Annex text.

Conclusion

The literature answers the headline question in a way the industry's own metrics cannot: the amenities that define a luxury urban hotel contribute to the guest-night footprint mainly through what is served, washed and heated rather than through the rooms themselves, and the single largest contributor, purchased food and drink, is excluded from HCMI, CHSB, LightStay and every corporate target reviewed. A paper that builds an activity-based attribution with a published HCMI-comparable baseline beside it will be the first to show a Manhattan luxury guest-night with its food, laundry, wellness and transfer lines separated, and the evidence base reviewed here is sufficient to do so for F&B, laundry, hot water, HVAC and the regulatory comparison, but only to bound, not measure, pools, thermal suites, spa treatments and gyms.

The regulatory and corporate comparison reframes what a "green commitment" can mean for a flagship like the Waldorf. Hilton's validated absolute targets, LL97's per-square-foot caps and the 2026 European claims regime all point to the same three facts: the building's gas and grid emissions are the compliance object, credits cannot substitute for them, and any claim must disclose its boundary. The one package consistent with every source found is therefore a measured, full-boundary footprint per stay published to guests, physical and Zone J-deliverable reductions that count under both regimes, menu and service design that attacks the F&B half of the footprint, and durable removals described as what they are, a contribution priced at a percent or two of the room rate. The paper's contribution is to make that boundary measurable.
