Research notes, digested

Each note answers key questions with cited findings, inferences and gaps; the takeaways are reproduced here.

Whole-hotel carbon footprint per guest-night: benchmarks, LCAs and allocation methodology01_baseline_and_allocation.md · 7,697 words · 7 questions

KQ1. What does the Cornell Hotel Sustainability Benchmarking Index (CHSB) report for carbon and energy intensity by segment and for New York City, and how has it trended since 2016?

The public CHSB PDF reports print only percentage changes and quartile "efficiency gap" ratios; the absolute benchmarks live in the companion Excel index tools. Parsed directly, CHSB 2025 (calendar-year 2023 data, 27,219 hotels) gives a New York City luxury non-resort median of 28.4 kgCO2e per occupied room (HCMI basis, n=21; mean 35.6, IQR 23.9–38.2), 102 kWh per occupied room, 322 kWh/m2 and 88.7 kgCO2e/m2; the US luxury non-resort median is 32.1 kgCO2e per occupied room, roughly 2.3x upper-midscale (14.0) and 2.7x midscale (11.7). US luxury intensity fell from 43.3 (CY2016) to ~32 kgCO2e/OCRM (CY2018 onward) and has been flat since; NYC luxury has hovered at 22–29 with small samples.

KQ2. What is the HCMI methodology and exactly how does it allocate emissions to rooms vs meeting space, laundry, F&B, spas and refrigerants? What are its limitations?

HCMI v2.0 (Sustainable Hospitality Alliance, updated 2016 and 2022, >30,000 hotels) is a whole-building operational-control inventory of scope 1 and 2 plus outsourced laundry (and optionally refrigerants), which it splits between guestrooms and meeting space strictly by conditioned floor-area ratio under an explicit "equal intensity for all areas" assumption; restaurants, spas, pools, fitness and back-of-house are not separate products but are folded proportionally into the two outputs. Food, waste, water, guest travel and all other scope 3 are excluded by design.

KQ3. What do peer-reviewed LCA and footprint studies report for a hotel stay (functional unit, boundary, data year, sample, headline)?

Operational footprints cluster at 10–30 kgCO2e per guest-night for European 3–5-star hotels and 25–45 for US luxury, but boundaries differ so much (scope 1+2 only, versus adding food, waste, consumables) that the same hotel can double its figure: the one study with a full scope 1–3 inventory (Ireland, 2026 preprint) finds 15.9–56.1 kgCO2e per room-night with food procurement 40–58% of the total. Older life-cycle work (Balearics) attributes 80–90% of lifetime emissions to the operational phase.

KQ4. How much more carbon-intensive is a luxury hotel than a midscale one per room-night, and which drivers explain the gap?

Measured ratios are consistent: US luxury is 2.3–2.7x upper-midscale/midscale per occupied room-night but only ~1.4x per m2 (CHSB CY2023); Brighter Planet's 2012 US model put the average upscale hotel 25% above budget per room-night with floor area per room the strongest property-level driver; Asian audit studies find 4≈5-star > 3-star per m2 with electricity (HVAC) dominant; the ENERGY STAR national model identifies room density, worker density and commercial refrigeration as the significant operational drivers, and a 2024 meta-analysis gives pooled correlations of 0.65 (floor area), 0.58 (star level), 0.78 (employees), 0.81 (room revenue) and non-significant occupancy.

KQ5. What do CBECS 2012/2018 report for lodging energy intensity and end uses, and what drives the ENERGY STAR hotel score?

Lodging site energy intensity fell from 96.9 kBtu/sqft (2012) to 85.7 (2018); water heating (16.5 kBtu/sqft, ~20%) and space heating (15.7, ~20%) are the largest end uses, with cooking 14.1, ventilation 12.5, lighting 7.2 and cooling 6.0; electricity averages 14.4 kWh/sqft. ENERGY STAR explains hotel source EUI with room density, worker density, commercial refrigeration density, degree days and a kitchen flag.

KQ6. What do GHG Protocol / ISO 14064 / ISO 14067 say about allocating shared services, and has anyone proposed amenity- or activity-level allocation of hotel emissions?

The product-footprint standards set a hierarchy — avoid allocation by subdividing or system expansion, then allocate on a physical relationship, then on economic value — but give no hotel-specific guidance; HCMI's floor-area rule is a physical allocation that ignores service intensity. No peer-reviewed amenity-level allocation of hotel emissions was found; the closest are the Irish full-scope study (which keeps services as embedded sub-units but reports only per room-night), an "activity-based" critique of HCMI, and a 2026 spa-visit calculator (~3.5–5 kgCO2e per visit).

KQ7. What is the typical Scope 1 / 2 / 3 split of a hotel's footprint, and what full Scope 3 inventories exist for hotel companies?

For managed/owned portfolios of US-centric groups, scope 2 (electricity, district steam) is 4–5x scope 1 (gas, refrigerants); when franchised hotels and supply chains are added, energy still accounts for roughly three-quarters of Marriott's 17.6 MtCO2e inventory and spend-based purchased goods ~22%. Hilton and Accor report only energy-related and waste scope 3; property-level full-scope studies show food procurement can exceed all scope 1+2 (40–58% of a room-night in Ireland).

Energy, water and carbon footprint of hotel wellness and leisure amenities02_wellness_amenities.md · 6,224 words · 7 questions

Key question 1: Indoor heated pools — measured and modelled energy intensities, drivers, and the evaporation share

Whole-facility benchmarks for public indoor pools cluster around 1,000–1,600 kWh/m2 of floor area per year in UK/Nordic sources (CIBSE TM46: 1,375 kWh/m2 floor area, 82% fossil-thermal) and, where a water-surface basis is used, roughly 1,100 kWh/m2 of water surface (Greek audits) up to the 4,000–5,200 kWh/m2 water-surface figures quoted secondhand for Norway and continental Europe; the basis discrepancy is unresolved and the original Trianti-Stourna and Kampel papers must be consulted before any of these are cited. Evaporation is consistently identified as the dominant pool-water heat loss (46–54% of total pool losses in one simulation; "at least 60%" of water heat loss in the sources collated by a 2025 French modelling paper), which is why pool-hall dehumidification and ventilation, not the water heater alone, drive the load.

Key question 2: Hot tubs/whirlpools and saunas/steam rooms — connected loads, duty cycles, annual kWh

Only residential hot-tub data are available (2,514 kWh/yr NSPI-2000 average; ~1,900 kWh/yr standby for a California-compliant 334-gal spa; 2,500–7,500 kWh/yr French 2023 estimate); commercial sauna heaters are 9–15 kW with a manufacturer-stated duty cycle of full power for 30–40 min then ~50%, and a vendor worked example puts an 11.4 kW steam generator at 36,500 kWh/yr for 16 h/day operation. No measured commercial hotel whirlpool, sauna or steam-room audit was found.

Key question 3: Spa/massage treatment rooms — HVAC, hot water, equipment and linen loads; spa energy benchmarks

No per-treatment energy benchmark exists in the public domain: ISPA, Global Wellness Institute and Spa Business material surfaced nothing quantitative, and the only "spa share of hotel energy" numbers are an unsourced "up to 35% of total energy for additional services such as a spa" (Spanish hotels) and a PwC cost-share figure (energy = 4% of hotel management costs for basic hotels vs 25% for hotels with spa and heated pools).

Key question 4: Fitness centres — equipment loads, lighting/HVAC, building intensity, energy-generating equipment

Building-level benchmarks for stand-alone fitness facilities are 160 kWh/m2/yr electricity + 440 fossil (CIBSE TM46 "Fitness & health centre", UK, 2008) and a US median site EUI of 50.8 kBtu/ft2 = 160 kWh/m2/yr (ENERGY STAR, CBECS "Recreation", which pools gyms with rinks and pools); equipment plug loads are small (a metered 2 HP treadmill drew ~712 W at 10 mph, 96 W at 1 mph, 3.8 W idle), so lighting and HVAC dominate. Energy-generating equipment returns at most 200–250 Wh per machine-hour and ~24 Wh in a real 30-minute test.

Key question 5: Marginal effect of pool/spa/gym on whole-hotel energy intensity — regression evidence

The large-N models mostly do not carry an amenity term: ENERGY STAR's hotel score (CBECS 2012) uses rooms, workers, refrigeration, kitchen presence, degree-days and % conditioned, and explicitly dropped laundry as non-significant; Chan 2005 (Hong Kong) found only cooling degree-days and occupied rooms; Wang 2012 (Taiwan) used area, rooms, occupancy and age. The only study found with a significant pool term is Santiago 2021 (six Gran Canaria hotels; pool volume significant among 31 characteristics), and Bohdanowicz & Martinac 2007 (184 Hilton/Scandic hotels) reported that amenities affect consumption even among uniform hotels, complicating benchmarking.

Key question 6: Towel and linen laundry attributable to pools, spas and gyms

Per-kg laundry intensities are well documented at plant level — TRSA member plants average 1.47 gal/lb (12.3 L/kg) water and 2,422 Btu/lb (1.57 kWh/kg) in-plant energy with 0.413 lb CO2 per lb (0.41 kg CO2/kg); UK trade benchmarks 1.6–1.7 kWh/kg typical, 0.8–1.0 attainable; on-premise hotel machines use 15–30 L/kg vs 3–6 L/kg for modern central plants — but no source quantifies kg of towels/robes per pool, spa or gym user, so the amenity attribution must be built from towel weights and issue counts.

Key question 7: Water and chemical footprint of pools; CO2e conversion of pool water and chemicals

Make-up/dilution water is standardised at 30 L per bather (Sport England, PWTAG incl. backwash, WHO/Nova Scotia, DIN 19643) with one Polish measurement at 40.6 L/bather/day; a single sand-filter backwash is ~16 m3 (38 L/s × 7 min); cradle-to-gate GWP factors from Euro Chlor are 0.90 kg CO2e/kg chlorine and 0.93 kg CO2e/kg sodium hypochlorite. No published study converting a pool's water and chemical use into CO2e was found.

Carbon footprint of hotel food and beverage operations03_food_and_beverage.md · 8,201 words · 8 questions

Key question 1: What share of a full-service hotel's total footprint is F&B?

The only hotel-level inventory found that itemises F&B with a full Scope 3 boundary (The Dolder Grand, a luxury urban hotel in Zurich, myclimate/ecoinvent method) puts food and beverages at 48% (2023), 52% (2024) and 56% (2025) of the whole-hotel footprint, with Scope 3 ~90% of the total. At group level, Accor's 2024 registration document reports catering and drinks at 19% of emissions versus energy at 65%, but that boundary is energy-dominated and the share of F&B rises sharply where grid electricity is low-carbon. Studies that exclude purchased food (e.g. a Greek 5-star resort, 81% energy) are not comparable.

Key question 2: Carbon footprint per meal by type

Per-kilogram factors are well established (Poore & Nemecek 2018 global means; Clune et al. 2017 medians) and diverge by an order of magnitude between beef and plant foods; per-meal studies consistently show beef-centred mains at roughly 8-11 times a vegetarian main. The US Cool Food Meals thresholds (3.59 kg CO2e breakfast, 5.38 kg lunch/dinner per portion) and Accor's reported 1.66-6.57 kg per meal give defensible bounds for hotel covers; the SHA estimate is that a typical hotel meal is 3x the global-average meal.

Key question 3: Commercial kitchen energy (operational, Scope 1/2)

Measured kitchen electricity in UK gastro-pub kitchens runs 1.5-3.3 kWh per meal (Mudie et al. 2016; 294 kWh/day/kitchen; refrigeration 41%), far above older CIBSE/Carbon Trust assumptions; benchmark re-studies found real use 66% above the published kWh/meal benchmark. Including gas, one audited institutional kitchen used 1.28 kWh electricity + 1.67 kWh gas per meal. US ENERGY STAR's restaurant end-use split is food preparation 35%, HVAC 28%, sanitation 18%, lighting 13%, refrigeration 6%. No hotel-specific kWh/cover study was found; SHA's 15-25% of whole-hotel energy for kitchens is interview-based.

Key question 4: Food waste in hotels

Measured hotel food waste clusters around 0.3-0.5 kg per cover (Ireland EPA: ~360 g/cover daily service, ~530 g/cover functions; Malaysia five-site study: 0.53 kg per customer average; Malta 5-star buffet dinner 0.48 kg/person plate waste), with buffets 2-3x à la carte. Hotel programmes with scales (Champions 12.3, 42 sites, 15 countries) cut waste 21% by weight in a year at a ~7:1 benefit-cost ratio. Emission factors: UK DEFRA ~627 kg CO2e per tonne of food waste landfilled (vs ~9 kg/t composted); a cradle-to-grave factor of 1.9 kg CO2e per kg of food wasted (BIO Intelligence 2010) is widely used; Winnow's Hilton campaign implies ~4.35 kg CO2e/kg.

Key question 5: Beverages (wine, spirits, beer, cocktails, ice, glassware)

Peer-reviewed LCAs give beer at 510-842 g CO2e per litre in the UK depending on packaging (Amienyo & Azapagic 2016), wine at ~0.9-2.0 kg CO2e per 0.75 L bottle across European studies with packaging and distribution the big levers, and single-malt Scotch at 2.83 kg CO2e per 70 cl bottle (2025 calculator study, Scope 3 = 77%). Cocktail figures (150-700 g per drink) come only from a supplier's marketing study. Bar equipment energy is small per serve (ice ~0.08-0.12 kWh/kg).

Key question 6: Minibars

Absorption minibars (the quiet type still common in luxury rooms) draw 0.5-0.7 kWh per 24 h (~190-265 kWh/yr per EN 62552-3 ratings), compressor units are claimed to use about a third of that, and manufacturers' catalogue range is 55-292 kWh/yr. Usage is low (surveys: ~70% of travellers never use one) and revenue is ~1% or less of hotel revenue, so energy per actual sale is high; no study quantifying minibar product waste was found.

Key question 7: Banqueting / events catering

The Dolder Grand reports 7.8-8 kg CO2e per event guest (2023-24) rising to 19.7 kg in 2025 (full Scope 3 hotel boundary, events = 430-646 t/yr); Ireland EPA puts hotel function food waste at ~530 g per cover; MeetGreen-derived figures give ~1.9 kg total waste per attendee-day. No published per-attendee catering carbon factor from Greenview, Hilton Meet with Purpose or the Events Industry Council was found.

Key question 8: Guest behaviour and amenity-driven demand

Field studies show plate waste rises with later buffet sittings (1.8 g to 31.8 g per person across a breakfast service), with the first meal of a stay, with children, and with hotel standard (four-star 14.3 g vs budget 9.2 g at breakfast); all-you-can-eat pricing and buffets are consistently the most wasteful formats; tourists eat ~500 g more food per day than at home. Interventions (smaller plates, norm messages, removing side plates) cut plate waste 14-34%. No study isolating "breakfast-included" packages as a causal driver of waste was found, though the Slovenian breakfast study was conducted at a hotel where breakfast was included in the rate.

Guestroom and guest-facing services: energy, water and carbon per luxury guest-night04_guestroom_and_services.md · 7,591 words · 9 questions

KQ1. End-use breakdown of guestroom energy (HVAC, lighting, DHW, plug loads)

The only public sub-metered guestroom data set found (PNNL, 300+ room full-service hotel, Washington DC area, Oct 2009–Jan 2010) shows guestroom HVAC (PTHPs) dominating at roughly 13 kWh per room-day in the heating season, with in-room lighting/TV/other plug loads and the minibar refrigerator each under 1 kWh per room-day; whole-building shares are HVAC and heating ~50–60% (winter), lighting ~7%, elevators ~1%, DHW 51% of gas. Survey-based sources (CBECS 2018, HES 2011) give water heating ~20% and space conditioning ~50% of total hotel energy, but none isolates the guestroom from the rest of the building.

KQ2. Domestic hot water: litres per guest-night, energy per litre, deep tubs and rain showers

Total hotel water per guest-night spans ~140 L (European frontrunner benchmark) to ~400–600 L (averages and 5-star), with multiple Spanish/European sources showing 5-star at roughly 1.5–2× lower categories; hot water is a subset not separately reported. Heating energy is physically fixed at ≈0.048 kWh per litre for a 42 K rise (EPA figure), before boiler efficiency. No study quantifying deep-soaking tubs or rain showers in hotels was found; only residential fixture capacities.

KQ3. Housekeeping and laundry: kg per room-night, kWh and litres per kg, reuse programme savings

Trade sources put soiled linen at 4.5–8 kg per occupied room-day; commercial laundries use 17–33 L/kg in washer-extractors and ≤17 L/kg in tunnel washers (EPA) and typically 1.6–1.7 kWh/kg (UK trade benchmark, 1.0 target, 0.8 attainable). Measured reuse-programme effects: descriptive-norm towel cards raise reuse from ~35% to 44–49% in a US hotel (Goldstein et al. 2008), German hotels sit at 64–93% regardless of message, and one Marriott Boston property quantifies an opted-out night at 37.2 gal water, 0.19 kWh electricity and 25,000 BTU gas.

KQ4. In-room amenities: miniature toiletries vs dispensers, bottled water, printed collateral

No independent LCA comparing miniature toiletries with bulk dispensers was found; chain claims are plastic-mass based (one pump bottle ≈ 10–12 miniatures; "95% less plastic") and one supplier claims 600 tCO2e saved. Bottled water has a well-cited energy intensity of 5.6–10.2 MJ per litre (Gleick & Cooley 2009), dominated by PET bottle manufacture for short-haul water.

KQ5. Elevators and vertical transport

Elevators were 141 kWh/day (≈1% of electricity, ≈0.47 kWh per room-day) in a 12-storey 300-room full-service hotel; literature shares are 2–8% of building electricity (offices), and ISO 25745-style guidance places hotels in the "medium" usage category (~300 trips/day). Measured reference cycles are 22–135 Wh per empty up-down cycle with 50–900 W standby.

KQ6. Guest ground transport attributable to the hotel (chauffeur, airport transfers, valet)

UK DEFRA 2024 factors give executive cars 0.170 (diesel), 0.208 (petrol), 0.179 (hybrid) kgCO2e/km and luxury cars 0.207 (diesel), 0.319 (petrol), 0.258 (hybrid), ~0.121 (PHEV) kgCO2e/km, with BEV tailpipe zero and electricity accounted separately. Major hotel groups' reported Scope 3 contains no guest-travel category: Marriott reports purchased goods, capital goods, fuel/energy, waste, business travel, commuting and franchises; Hilton reports franchises, landfilled waste and business travel; IHG's SBTi scope is Category 14 franchises. A hotel-owned chauffeur fleet would fall in Scope 1 under GHG Protocol logic.

KQ7. Behavioural and operational levers: key-card switches, occupancy thermostats, feedback, skip-housekeeping

The best independent evidence is the DOE/PNNL 2012 demonstration in six Marriott-brand hotels: occupancy-based HVAC controls saved 10.8–26.5% (mean 18.4%) of guest-room HVAC energy, 167–589 kWh per room per year, with paybacks of 5.3–17.9 years; earlier utility studies ranged 5.6–28% (62–462 kWh/room/yr). Vendor-linked case studies claim 33–50% but rest on 2–4 rooms or uncontrolled comparisons, and two independent studies found savings not statistically significant or eroded by guest overrides.

KQ8. Luxury vs midscale guestroom differences (room size, fixtures, 24-h public lighting, CHSB luxury data)

CHSB's own validity thresholds reveal the expected range: full-service luxury hotels are screened at 50–700 kWh per occupied room versus 20–300 for midscale, and CHSB finds luxury/5-star hotels use more water per occupied room (but not per m²) and showed the smallest 2019–2021 reductions; HES finds no significant EUI difference by star rating per m², implying luxury's higher per-room-night figures come from more area per key, more services and lower occupancy. Room-size evidence is design-guide grade only (midscale ~22–28 m², luxury ≥37 m²).

KQ9. Business centre, meeting rooms, 24-hour services (butler, room service)

No source quantifies energy or carbon per room-service order, butler service or business-centre use. The only established allocation method is HCMI's floor-area apportionment, which produces a meeting-space footprint per m² per hour (CHSB Measure 7) rather than a per-service figure.

Corporate climate targets (Hilton and peers), SBTi rules, offset integrity, hotel green packages and claim regulation05_corporate_targets_and_offsets.md · 8,503 words · 7 questions

Key question 1: Hilton "Travel with Purpose" 2030 climate goals, SBTi status, measurement (LightStay), renewables/EACs, offsets, Scope 3 approach

Hilton's validated 2030 targets are intensity targets (MT CO2e per m2, 2008 base): -75% Scope 1+2 at managed hotels and -56% Scope 3 at franchised hotels, validated by SBTi in June 2022 as 1.5°C-aligned (managed) with the franchised goal described as "below 2°C". By end-2025 Hilton reports -50.9% (managed) and -36.0% (franchised) in intensity, but its own tables show absolute market-based Scope 1+2 emissions from managed hotels rose from 2.23 Mt (2008) to 2.44 Mt (2025) and franchised Scope 3 from 2.18 Mt to 4.01 Mt because floor area roughly doubled. Hilton has no SBTi-validated net-zero (long-term) target that I could find; it reports market-based Scope 2 (so RECs/green contracts count), discloses no portfolio-wide renewable-electricity percentage, and uses purchased carbon credits only in the guest-facing "Carbon Neutral Meetings" product, not against its corporate targets.

Key question 2: Comparable targets at Marriott, Hyatt, IHG, Accor, Minor/NH, Six Senses, Iberostar, Scandic, Four Seasons, Mandarin Oriental, Rosewood, Kimpton/1 Hotels, Soneva

Marriott is the only major with SBTi-validated near-term and net-zero (2050) absolute targets (46.2% Scope 1+2 and 27.5% Scope 3 by 2030 vs 2019; 90% by 2050), yet it sourced only 2.92% of energy from renewables in 2023 and measures progress market-based. Hyatt (27.5% Scope 1+2 by 2030 vs 2019, SBTi-approved 2021) and Accor (46% Scope 1+2 by 2030 vs 2019, net zero 2050) have near-term targets; IHG's (15% absolute direct, 46% per m2 franchise) was submitted to SBTi but validation could not be confirmed; Minor International/NH have validated net-zero targets. Luxury independents either use intensity targets (Six Senses -46% per occupied room by 2030 vs 2019) or neutrality-by-2030 ambitions that explicitly rely on offsets (Iberostar). Nothing could be verified for Four Seasons, Mandarin Oriental, Rosewood, Kimpton or 1 Hotels.

Key question 3: What SBTi and Scope 2 accounting rules permit (V1.3.1, V2.0 final June 2026; April 2024 EAC controversy; GHG Protocol Scope 2 revision)

Under SBTi V1.3.1 (applicable through 2026) carbon credits "do not count as reductions toward meeting near-term or long-term science-based targets" and may only neutralise residual emissions at net zero or finance beyond-value-chain mitigation; V2.0 (published 11 June 2026, mandatory from 31 Jan 2028) keeps that line, replaces BVCM with a voluntary "Ongoing Emissions Responsibility" ladder (1%/10%/100% of ongoing emissions, $20–80/t benchmarks) that becomes mandatory for large companies from 2035 (1% rising to 100% by the net-zero year with phased durability), and requires neutralisation of long-lived residuals with long-lived removals. Market-based Scope 2 instruments remain allowed but V2.0 requires same-deliverability-region procurement, plants ≤15 years old, and hourly-matching reporting for large loads, while the GHG Protocol's own Scope 2 revision (consulted Oct 2025–Jan 2026; final expected 2027) proposes hourly matching and deliverability for contractual instruments.

Key question 4: Offset integrity (ICVCM, VCMI, Oxford Principles 2020/2024), over-crediting evidence, prices for removals vs avoidance, durable CDR market

Peer-reviewed evidence shows systematic over-crediting in the project types hotels have typically bought: West et al. (Science, Aug 2023) found only 6.1% of credits from 18 REDD+ projects were linked to additional reductions; Badgley et al. (GCB, 2022) found 29.4% over-crediting (30.0 Mt) in California's forest offsets; Coffield et al. (GCB, 2022) found carbon gains on 37 California IFM projects "generally not additional"; Haya et al. (2023) found IFM protocols deviate from science on baselines, leakage and reversal. The ICVCM has CCP-labelled only three new REDD+ methodologies (Nov 2024) and excluded the legacy Verra methodologies that generated most REDD+ credits; the Oxford Principles (2024) say "most offsetting today is still not net zero aligned" and call for 100% durable removals by the net-zero date. Durable removals cost roughly $180/t on average (DAC median ~$650/t; biochar $50–150/t) versus ~$35/t for nature-based and a ~$6 spot average for generic credits.

Key question 5: Documented hotel "green packages" and claims; guest opt-in at booking; Sustainable Hospitality Alliance; willingness-to-pay evidence

Hotel neutrality products are almost all avoidance-credit based and either paid by the hotel (Radisson since late 2012; Hilton meetings since 2012/ClimeCo; Hyatt 14 US hotels 2023; Bucuti & Tara CarbonNeutral since 2018) or by a levy/opt-in (Soneva 2% since 2008; Scandic/myclimate 2013; Six Senses Zighy rate). Evidence of effectiveness is thin and evidence of backlash is growing (Populus's 80% seedling mortality; Booking.com forced to drop its Travel Sustainable badge in March 2024; German court against Best Western "climate neutral" stays). Stated WTP studies find majorities "willing" but small amounts (Nelson 2021: 72.7% willing, mean ≈US$1.55/night; Kuminoff 2010 hedonic premium $9–26; Damigos 2023 review ≈5%), luxury/mid-price guests more willing than economy (Kang 2012), and revealed behaviour on OTA opt-ins is poor (Expedia CEO: cheaper option chosen "the overwhelming majority of the time").

Key question 6: Regulatory and legal constraints on green/neutrality claims (EU 2024/825, Green Claims Directive, UK CMA/DMCC, US FTC, New York, airline and hotel rulings)

From 27 September 2026 Directive (EU) 2024/825 bans, as an unfair practice, claims that a product has a neutral, reduced or positive environmental impact based on offsetting outside the value chain, along with unsubstantiated generic claims and non-certified sustainability labels; the separate Green Claims Directive is stalled since the Commission's June 2025 withdrawal signal. Courts have already applied existing unfair-practice law to offset-based neutrality: Amsterdam (KLM, 20 Mar 2024: 15 of 19 statements misleading), Cologne (Eurowings, LG Mar 2024; OLG 13 Dec 2024), the BGH (27 Jun 2024, I ZR 98/23: "klimaneutral" ads must say whether emissions are reduced or offset), and Berlin (Best Western Mannheim "climate neutral" stays misleading because laundry and waste emissions were omitted). The UK CMA can fine up to 10% of global turnover since 6 Apr 2025 and the ASA banned "eco hotel" ads in Sep 2026; the US FTC Green Guides (2012) remain unrevised. For a New York property the binding constraint is Local Law 97 (hotel limits 0.00987 then 0.00526 tCO2e/sf; $268/t penalty; offsets capped at 10% of the limit; RECs only for in-city-deliverable, same-year electricity).

Key question 7: Alternative "creative packages": green tariffs/PPAs, insetting, menu/amenity carbon labelling, choice architecture, low-carbon rates, disclosure-only claims

The evidence base supports demand-side measures that reduce inventory emissions rather than compensate for them: carbon labels on menus shift choices modestly (Brunner 2018: +11.5% low-emission dishes, -3.6% emissions; canteen trials 1.5–5% emission cuts; some workplace trials null), on-site/PPA renewables plus electrification (Hotel Marcel; Hilton's Istanbul/Dubrovnik PPAs covering 70% of demand) count directly toward SBTs and LL97, and Hilton already has choice-architecture infrastructure (Meeting Impact Calculator, 76K+ meetings in 2025; per-stay LightStay footprints). Disclosure-only framing is the one form of claim that the EU directive, the BGH and the CMA leave unambiguously permissible.

NYC regulatory, grid and Waldorf Astoria property context (Facet 6)06_nyc_and_waldorf_context.md · 7,257 words · 8 questions

Q1. Waldorf Astoria New York: physical facts, pre-closure vs post-reopening

The renovated building is a 1.6-1.67 million ft2, 47-storey landmark with a 375-key hotel on the lower ~18 floors and 372 condominium residences (floors 19-52), reopened in phases from 15 July 2025 (rooms), 5 September 2025 (ballroom/meetings) and October 2025 (22,000 ft2 Guerlain spa). Before closure it was a 1,413-room, 1.6 million ft2 hotel. The spa's size is reported inconsistently (20,000 / 22,000 / 30,000 ft2) and no 2025 source confirms a hotel-guest pool (the 25 m Starlight Pool is a residents' amenity).

Q2. Energy systems after renovation (district steam, electrification, certifications)

Nothing has been publicly disclosed about the renovated building's HVAC design, engineer of record, retention of Con Edison steam, heat pumps, or any LEED/WELL/Passive House certification. The one data point is the CY2024 LL84 filing, which reports zero district steam use (vs 105-133 GBtu/yr pre-closure), electricity 79% of site energy and estimated gas use — suggestive of a fuel switch but not proof.

Q3. LL84 benchmarking disclosures for the Waldorf and comparable Manhattan luxury hotels

Pre-closure (CY2013-2016) the Waldorf benchmarked at site EUI 125-141 kBtu/ft2, source EUI 255-273, ENERGY STAR 23-30, 15.4-17.9 ktCO2e/yr (9.6-11.1 kgCO2e/ft2) and 77-90 gal/ft2 water. The CY2024 filing (EUI 27.7, ES 97) is a construction year and not performance. In CY2024 the median NYC hotel had site EUI 86.4 kBtu/ft2 and 7.31 kgCO2e/ft2 (Manhattan: 94.2 and 8.15); the eleven traceable Manhattan luxury hotels cluster at 110-195 kBtu/ft2 and 7-17 kgCO2e/ft2 (median ~135 and ~11.3), i.e. roughly 1.4-1.6x the Manhattan hotel median.

Q4. Local Law 97: hotel limits, coefficients, penalties, RECs, offsets, 2023-24 rules, HANYC

LL97 caps hotels (occupancy group R-1 / ESPM 'Hotel') at 0.00987 tCO2e/ft2 for 2024-2029 and 0.00385 (ESPM) / 0.00526 (statute) for 2030-2034, falling to 0.00264 (2035-39) and 0.00147 (2040-49); overshoot costs $268/tCO2e/yr. The prescribed electricity coefficient is 0.000288962 tCO2e/kWh (2024-29) and 0.000145 (2030-34, or hourly TOU); gas 0.00005311 and steam 0.00004493 -> 0.0000432 tCO2e/kBtu. RECs may offset only electricity emissions and only from NYISO Zone J-delivered resources (currently Tier 4: CHPE, Clean Path NY); AHRF offsets are capped at 10% of the limit (2024-29). In year one, hotels were 5% of covered Article 320 properties and had the lowest compliance rate (91%) of the major types. HANYC has published no formal position; it frames LL97 as 'real financial risk' for 24/7 hotels.

Q5. NYC/NYS grid: Zone J emissions intensity, Indian Point, CHPE/Clean Path, CLCPA, Con Edison steam decarbonisation

Measured NYC grid intensity (eGRID2023 NYCW) is 864 lb CO2/MWh (0.392 kg/kWh), well above LL97's 0.289 coefficient; downstate (Zones F-K) generation in 2024 was dominated by dual-fuel gas/oil plants with roughly 10% zero-emission supply versus 89% upstate. CHPE (1,250 MW, 10.4 TWh/yr, ~20% of NYC demand) is now fully operational (2026) and is the only Zone J REC source to date; Clean Path NY is expected 2027. CLCPA targets are 70% renewable by 2030 and 100% zero-emission electricity by 2040. Con Edison's steam system (99% gas, ~1,500 accounts) targets net-zero Scope 1 for cogeneration by 2040 and all steam by 2050; its 2018 footprint was 110 lb CO2/Mlb.

Q6. Comparable hotel sustainability case studies (NYC and nearby)

Public, quantified per-room-night carbon data for individual NYC hotels is essentially absent; the usable comparators are LL84 filings (Q3), 1 Hotel Brooklyn Bridge's LEED Gold status, and Hilton's portfolio-level LightStay claims. Hotel Marcel (New Haven) and Hilton's property-level data could not be fetched.

Q7. NYC hotel market data 2024-2026

Manhattan ran 82.3% occupancy and $310.51 ADR in H1 2025 (PwC/STR); the luxury class ran 76.4% occupancy, $490.75 ADR and ~$375 RevPAR (+10.1% YoY), peaking at $585.65 ADR in May 2025. HVS puts 2025 ADR ~34% above 2019 with occupancy in the mid-80s and forecasts stabilisation at 88.7% occupancy in 2029. The Waldorf's $1,500 entry rate is ~3x the luxury-class ADR. No source gives average luxury length of stay.

Q8. Other NYC rules relevant to hotel carbon: LL84/LL33 grades, LL87/LL88, LL146 organics, Commercial Waste Zones, water

Hotels over 25,000 ft2 must benchmark annually (LL84, $500 penalties) and post an energy-grade label (LL33: A >= 85, B 70-84, C 55-69, D < 55 ENERGY STAR; $1,250 for non-posting). Food service in hotels with 100+ rooms must source-separate organics under LL146; the July 2026 Int. 31 amendment ties compliance timing to the Commercial Waste Zone rollout (now Phase 5 of 10). Pre-closure the Waldorf's ES 23-30 would have graded D.

Verified seminal papers on hotel energy and carbon (bibliography, abstracts, headline figures)07_verified_seminal_papers.md · 7,359 words · 6 questions

Key question 1: What do the whole-building hotel energy and carbon benchmark papers actually report, and are their bibliographic details correct?

All ten benchmark papers resolve cleanly in Crossref with the volumes and pages given in the brief, except Pieri et al. 2015 (Energy and Buildings 94:252-262, not 102:81-93). Headline intensities that can be read in primary text or publisher abstracts are: Singapore 427 kWh/m2/yr and 30.5 MWh/room/yr (Priyadarsini 2009, full text); Taiwan 280.1 to 143.6 kWh/m2/yr by hotel class and 26.7 to 9.4 MWh/room/yr (Wang 2012, abstract); Taiwan luxury 132 kgCO2e/m2/yr, 29 kgCO2e per guest-night, 50 kgCO2e per room-night (Huang 2015, abstract); Hong Kong 313 kWh/m2/yr electricity (Chan 2005, abstract) and 564 kWh/m2/yr total (Deng & Burnett 2000, value read only from citing papers).

Key question 2: What do the life-cycle and full-scope per-night carbon studies (Filimonau, Rosselló-Batle, Puig, Attari & Hanrahan) report, with functional unit and boundary?

Three studies were read in full. Filimonau 2011 (two UK 3-star hotels, LCEA per guest-night): 7.5 and 4.5 kgCO2e operational, 11.65 and 8.25 kgCO2e including breakfast, laundry and a 15% embodied-energy uplift. Puig 2017 (14 Spanish coastal hotels, per overnight stay, no food): 23 (2*), 22 (5*), 12 (4*) kgCO2e. Attari & Hanrahan 2026 (four Irish hotels, full Scope 1-3 incl. food procurement, per occupied room-night): 15.87 to 56.14 kgCO2e (mean 39.87), of which food procurement is 39.7-57.7%, versus 9.40-32.32 kgCO2e under Scope 1+2 only. Rosselló-Batle 2010 could be read only at abstract level (operating phase 70-80% of 50-year life-cycle energy).

Key question 3: What do the tourism-scale papers (Lenzen 2018, Gössling 2002, Becken 2001) report, and what share is accommodation?

Lenzen et al. 2018 is confirmed at Nature Climate Change 8(6):522-528 with an Author Correction; its open Supplementary Information (Tab. SI 6b) gives accommodation a carbon footprint of 282 Mt CO2-e (6.37%) in 2013 and food & beverage serving 227 Mt (5.11%), out of 4.5 Gt. Becken 2001's abstract puts hotels at 67% of New Zealand's 1.74 PJ accommodation energy (1999); Gössling 2002's abstract is qualitative.

Key question 4: Swimming pools - what do Trianti-Stourna 1998 and Kampel 2013/2016 report, and is pool energy about 1,094 or about 4,300 kWh/m2 of water surface per year?

Both numbers are attributed to Trianti-Stourna et al. 1998 in the literature, but they are different quantities. Kampel's 2013 and 2016 papers (which read the 1998 paper directly, confirmed via Kampel's 2015 NTNU thesis full text) report that Trianti-Stourna state 4,300 kWh/m2 of water surface per year for Mediterranean and about 5,200 for continental climates as general values with "no indication about where these numbers originate from", while the five Greek facilities they actually audited averaged 450.1 kWh/m2 of usable floor area with a water-surface-to-floor ratio of 3.43 (about 1,540 kWh/m2 WS). The 1,094.5 kWh/m2 WS figure could not be located in any accessible text.

Key question 5: Water - what do Gössling et al. 2012 and Deyà Tortella & Tirado 2011 report?

Both records are confirmed (Tourism Management 33(1):1-15; Journal of Environmental Management 92(10):2568-2579). Their abstracts carry no litres-per-guest-night values: Gössling's review states direct tourism water use is "considerably less than 1% of global consumption" and that indirect use is likely larger; Deyà Tortella's Mallorca regression finds higher-category, low-season, all-inclusive and management-system variables raise hotel water use.

Key question 6: Food, beverage, waste and guest behaviour - what do Gössling 2011, Point 2012, Pirani & Arafat 2014/2016 and Goldstein 2008 report?

All five records are confirmed; Point et al.'s third author is Naugler C (the brief's "Theodore C" is wrong). None of the four food/waste abstracts contains a per-meal, per-bottle or per-room-night quantity: Gössling 2011 explicitly flags "a lack of dependable data on the GHG intensity of foodstuffs"; Point 2012's functional unit is one 750 mL bottle with viticulture and consumer transport dominant; Pirani 2016 surveyed 45 UAE hotels/restaurants and introduced the FRESH indicator. Goldstein 2008's abstract confirms the provincial-norm result but not the 35.1%/44.1%/49.3% reuse rates.

Gap fill: Waldorf Astoria NY energy systems, Hilton CDP/10-K, wellness amenity data, luxury-brand targets08_gap_fill_waldorf_hilton_wellness.md · 5,178 words · 5 questions

Gap A1 - What is publicly known about the renovated Waldorf's MEP design (steam, boilers, CHP, cooling, certifications)?

The owner, Hilton and SOM have published nothing about the energy system, but the public permit record does: eight Viessmann Vitocrossal 300 CA3B 6.0 natural-gas condensing boilers on the 7th floor (DEP certificate to operate 22 Jan 2025), a 555 kW natural-gas CHP plant in a 6th-floor CHP room, cooling towers relocated into a Level-20 "cooling tower well", 4-pipe fan coils, and LL84 reporting of zero district steam in CY2024 after 105 million kBtu of Con Edison steam in CY2016. Con Ed steam appears to have been replaced by on-site gas, but no party has announced it. No LEED, WELL, ENERGY STAR certification or LL97 statement exists for the property.

Gap A2 - LL84 CY2024 record re-pull and CY2025 status; pool access and spa size

The CY2024 record (re-pulled) shows 10.74 million kWh grid electricity, 9.50 million kBtu natural gas (estimated), zero district steam, 4,723 tCO2e and an ENERGY STAR 97 earned during construction; no CY2025 record exists yet (dataset last updated 21 Apr 2026, report years 2022-2024 only). The 25 m Starlight Pool is a residents-only 25th-floor amenity; Hilton's own releases list no hotel pool; the Guerlain spa is 20,000 ft2 per Hilton, 22,000 ft2 with 16 rooms per Leisure Media, and 30,000 ft2 in a pre-opening Guerlain announcement.

Gap B - Hilton CDP response, 10-K climate language, net-zero status

Hilton's only public CDP response is the 2023 (FY2022) questionnaire, now reachable only via the Wayback Machine; it gives Scope 1 446,084, Scope 2 1,922,844 (location) / 1,904,610 (market), Scope 3 franchises 4,020,579 tCO2e, 550 Gold Standard credits cancelled, no internal carbon price, and no CDP letter score. The SBTi dashboard shows Hilton's validated targets are absolute (-46.2% Scope 1+2, -27.5% franchise Scope 3 by 2030 vs 2019), not the 75%/56% intensity goals Hilton reports against, and Hilton has no net-zero commitment. The FY2025 10-K mentions climate only as generic demand/weather risk and never mentions Local Law 97, building performance standards, carbon, greenhouse gases or science-based targets.

Gap C - Wellness amenity data (towel/linen mass, sauna/steam/whirlpool energy, spa benchmarks)

Towel masses can be computed from Standard Textile's catalogue weights (hospitality bath towel 27x54 in 15-17 lb/dozen = 570-640 g; hand towel 3-4.5 lb/dz = 115-170 g; bath mat 7-10.5 lb/dz), giving about 1.3-1.5 kg of dry towelling for a two-bath-plus-hand-towel spa or pool visit; whole-room laundry runs 8-55 lb per occupied room with ~12 lb typical for full-service hotels. Commercial sauna/steam data remain nameplate only (18-20 kW units) with no published duty cycle or hotel sub-metering. The only metered hotel-spa electricity figure found is 7.24-11.22 kWh/m2/month at a Bali hotel spa (student audit, electricity only).

Gap D - Luxury-brand climate targets (Four Seasons, Mandarin Oriental, Rosewood, Peninsula/HSH, Aman, SH Hotels/1 Hotels, Kimpton/IHG)

Only IHG (Kimpton's parent) has an SBTi-validated target (-46.2% absolute Scope 1+2+3 incl. franchises by 2030 vs 2019) and it is self-declared off track; Rosewood has SBTi-aligned but unvalidated targets (net zero 2050; -87% absolute Scope 1+2 by 2035 vs 2023); HSH/Peninsula and Mandarin Oriental have intensity targets vs 2010 and 2012 respectively with no SBTi entry; Four Seasons, Aman and 1 Hotels publish no quantified group carbon target. None of the seven publishes a per-room-night carbon figure; IHG reports per available room and Rosewood/HSH/Mandarin per m2.

Open questions

What the evidence does not settle, named by the agents who looked.

  • Hotel-only floor area of the Waldorf. The single-lot LL84 filing covers 1.67M ft² including 372 residences. The CHSB-implied default (90 m²/key) and the report's bounding case (~1.0M ft², ~250 m²/key) differ 2.7×; every kg/m² figure depends on which is right.
  • Covers per occupied room-night. The Studio (2.0) and the guidelines' worked example (2) assume it; kitchen energy per cover and the F&B share move with it. No hotel in the evidence base reports it.
  • Nothing hot except the pool is measured. Commercial sauna, steam and whirlpool energy are nameplate × duty everywhere; spa treatment-room intensity has one metered lower bound (Bali, electricity only).
  • No sub-metered luxury hotel separates spa, pool, kitchen and laundry from rooms. One such dataset would firm up a third of the general rules.
  • Length of stay is not in the evidence base; the housekeeping opt-out lever assumes a 2.2-night average and is labelled as such.
  • Primary texts still unread: Bohdanowicz & Martinac 2007 per-guest-night kWh, Deng & Burnett 2000 (564 kWh/m² via citing papers), Goldstein 2008 reuse percentages, Kampel 2016 kWh/visitor, Trianti-Stourna's 1,094.5 kWh/m² water surface.
  • First hypothesis to run (H11): does electrifying heat alone bring a gas-and-CHP Waldorf under the 2030 cap? The arithmetic says no (about 49% over), and only electrify + 30% demand cut + Zone J RECs complies. Storylines is set up for it.

Where the numbers live

The decision graph is the system of record; the Evidence Map is generated from its export and lists every observation with its source. The Studio's defaults are calibrated to the CHSB New York luxury medians; Storylines seeds its slices from the Studio and its events from the intervention library above.
